AWS Intermediate Tutorial
Build a production-ready app
on Amazon Web Services
This tutorial takes you from zero to a fully deployed, production-grade Node.js REST API across 22 AWS services. Every section includes an AWS Console walkthrough and a CLI / code alternative. We build one real application — TaskBoard API — throughout the entire tutorial, progressively adding more services and features.
Node.js 20 AWS SDK v3 PostgreSQL + Knex Docker / ECS Terraform / CDK 22 AWS Services

What we're building

TaskBoard is a multi-user task management REST API. By the end of this tutorial it will be fully deployed on AWS with:

  • EC2 + ALB + ASG running the Express.js server with auto-scaling
  • RDS PostgreSQL as the primary database, with RDS Proxy for connection pooling
  • DynamoDB for sessions, activity logs, and high-throughput data
  • S3 for file attachments, static website hosting, and object event triggers
  • Lambda + SQS + SNS for asynchronous email notifications and fan-out processing
  • Secrets Manager + SSM for credential storage and config management
  • CloudWatch for logging, metrics, alarms, dashboards, and anomaly detection
  • API Gateway for serverless HTTP API routing to Lambda
  • CloudFront for global CDN and SPA hosting with HTTPS
  • ElastiCache (Redis) for caching, rate limiting, and pub/sub
  • ECS on Fargate as a container-based alternative to EC2
  • Route 53 for DNS and domain management
  • SES for transactional email delivery
  • OpenSearch for full-text task search
  • Cognito for managed user authentication with JWT tokens
  • Terraform / CDK for infrastructure as code
  • VPC with public/private subnets, NAT Gateway, endpoints, NACLs, Flow Logs, Peering

AWS Global Infrastructure

Before diving in, understand how AWS is physically organized:

ConceptDefinitionExample
RegionA geographic area containing multiple data centers. All your resources live in a region. Data does not leave a region unless you explicitly replicate it.us-east-1 (N. Virginia), eu-west-1 (Ireland), ap-southeast-1 (Singapore)
Availability Zone (AZ)One or more discrete data centers inside a region, connected by low-latency links. If one AZ fails, others keep running.us-east-1a, us-east-1b, us-east-1c
Edge LocationCDN endpoints for CloudFront. Caches content close to end users. Not used to deploy apps.250+ cities globally
ARNAmazon Resource Name — a unique identifier for every AWS resource.arn:aws:s3:::my-bucket, arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:123456:function:my-fn
💡 Which region should I use?

Choose the region closest to your users for lowest latency. For learning, us-east-1 (N. Virginia) has the widest service support and lowest prices. All examples in this tutorial use us-east-1.

⚠️ Cost warning

Always set a billing alarm in CloudWatch before starting. Go to AWS Console → Billing → Billing Preferences → Enable "Receive Billing Alerts", then create a CloudWatch alarm at $1 to catch unexpected charges early.

Step 0
Project Setup
Set up your local machine, AWS CLI, and the TaskBoard Node.js project structure before touching any AWS service.

1. Install prerequisites

Terminalbash
# 1. Install Node.js 20 via nvm (recommended — avoids permission issues)
curl -o- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh/nvm/v0.39.7/install.sh | bash
source ~/.bashrc  # or ~/.zshrc on Mac
nvm install 20 && nvm use 20
node --version   # v20.x.x

# 2. Install AWS CLI v2
# macOS:
brew install awscli
# Linux:
curl "https://awscli.amazonaws.com/awscli-exe-linux-x86_64.zip" -o awscliv2.zip
unzip awscliv2.zip && sudo ./aws/install
aws --version   # aws-cli/2.x.x

2. Configure the AWS CLI

After creating your AWS account, create a programmatic IAM user (covered in the IAM section) and run:

Terminalbash
aws configure
# AWS Access Key ID [None]: AKIAIOSFODNN7EXAMPLE
# AWS Secret Access Key [None]: wJalrXUtn.../bPxRfiCYEXAMPLEKEY
# Default region name [None]: us-east-1
# Default output format [None]: json

# Verify it works — should return your account ID
aws sts get-caller-identity

This saves credentials to ~/.aws/credentials and config to ~/.aws/config. The AWS SDK automatically reads from these files.

3. Create the TaskBoard project

Terminalbash
mkdir taskboard-api && cd taskboard-api
npm init -y

# Core app dependencies
npm install express pg dotenv multer uuid

# AWS SDK v3 — install only the clients you need (tree-shakeable)
npm install \
  @aws-sdk/client-s3 \
  @aws-sdk/client-dynamodb \
  @aws-sdk/client-sqs \
  @aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager \
  @aws-sdk/client-cloudwatch-logs \
  @aws-sdk/s3-request-presigner \
  @aws-sdk/util-dynamodb

# Dev tools
npm install --save-dev nodemon jest supertest

4. Project structure

Directory layouttree
taskboard-api/
├── src/

│       └── logger.js       # CloudWatch logging
├── lambda/
│   └── emailNotifier/
│       └── index.mjs

│   └── teardown.sh        # Delete everything (save $$$)
├── .env.example
└── package.json

5. The .env file

.env.exampleenv
NODE_ENV=development
PORT=3000
AWS_REGION=us-east-1

# S3
S3_BUCKET_NAME=taskboard-attachments-YOUR_ACCOUNT_ID

# RDS
RDS_HOST=taskboard-db.xxxxxxx.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com
RDS_PORT=5432
RDS_DATABASE=taskboard
RDS_USER=taskadmin
RDS_PASSWORD=your_password_here

# DynamoDB
DYNAMO_SESSIONS_TABLE=taskboard-sessions
DYNAMO_LOGS_TABLE=taskboard-activity-logs

# SQS
SQS_NOTIFICATIONS_URL=https://sqs.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/ACCOUNT/taskboard-notifications

# Secrets Manager (production: fetch at startup instead of .env)
SECRETS_ARN=arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:secret:taskboard/prod
🚫 Never commit .env to git

Add .env to .gitignore immediately. For production on EC2/Lambda, use AWS Secrets Manager or Parameter Store to inject secrets at runtime — never bake them into your code or environment files.

6. AWS SDK v3 — key concepts

AWS SDK v3 (released 2020) is the modern JavaScript SDK. It's completely different from v2:

FeatureSDK v2 (legacy)SDK v3 (use this)
Import styleconst AWS = require('aws-sdk')import { S3Client } from "@aws-sdk/client-s3"
Bundle sizeEntire SDK (~400KB+)Per-service packages (tree-shakeable)
API styleCallbacks / .promise()Native async/await
MiddlewareLimitedFull middleware stack
TypeScriptSeparate @types packageBuilt-in types
src/config/aws.jsjavascript
// Create client singletons — instantiate once, reuse everywhere.
// Clients are thread-safe and handle connection pooling internally.
import { S3Client } from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";
import { DynamoDBClient } from "@aws-sdk/client-dynamodb";
import { SQSClient } from "@aws-sdk/client-sqs";
import { SecretsManagerClient } from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

const CONFIG = { region: process.env.AWS_REGION || "us-east-1" };

// In production on EC2/Lambda with an IAM Role attached,
// you don't pass any credentials — the SDK fetches them
// automatically from the EC2 instance metadata service (IMDS).
export const s3       = new S3Client(CONFIG);
export const dynamo   = new DynamoDBClient(CONFIG);
export const sqs      = new SQSClient(CONFIG);
export const secrets  = new SecretsManagerClient(CONFIG);
Service 1 of 7
IAM — Identity & Access Management
IAM is the security backbone of AWS. Every API call you make is authenticated (who are you?) and authorized (what are you allowed to do?) through IAM. Getting IAM right prevents catastrophic security breaches.
UsersRolesPoliciesGroupsSTS

Core IAM concepts

ConceptWhat it isWhen to use
Root AccountThe account you created AWS with. Has unlimited power.Only to create your first IAM admin user, then never again. Enable MFA immediately.
IAM UserA permanent identity for a person or service. Has long-lived access keys.Human developers who need console access, or legacy applications.
IAM RoleA temporary identity assumed by AWS services or federated users. Credentials expire automatically.EC2 instances, Lambda functions, ECS tasks — anything running inside AWS.
IAM GroupA collection of IAM users sharing the same policies.Apply the same permissions to your dev team in one place.
PolicyA JSON document defining allowed/denied actions on resources.Attached to users, groups, or roles to grant permissions.
STSSecurity Token Service — issues temporary credentials when a role is assumed.Cross-account access, CI/CD pipelines, federated login.

How IAM authorization works

Every AWS API call goes through this evaluation chain:

IAM Policy Evaluation Logic
API Call
→
Explicit DENY?
→ yes →
❌ DENY
↓ no
Explicit ALLOW?
→ yes →
✓ ALLOW
↓ no
❌ Implicit DENY
(default — everything denied unless explicitly allowed)

Setting up your IAM admin user

Never work in the root account day-to-day. These four steps take under five minutes and are non-negotiable for any real AWS project.

Phase 1 of 2 — Create the Administrators Group and User

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/groups
AWS Console
1

Create the Administrators group

In IAM left sidebar click User groups → Create group.

IAM › User groups › Create group
User group name*
Administrators
Attach permissions policies
AdministratorAccess
Search for AdministratorAccess and tick the checkbox. This is an AWS-managed policy — full access to all services. Only ever attach this to a human admin group, never to an application.

Click Create group.

2

Create your personal IAM user

In the sidebar click Users → Add users.

IAM › Users › Add users
User name*
john-dev
Use your name or handle. This is the identity you log in with every day.
AWS Management Console access
Enabled
Gives you the ability to sign in to the AWS web console with a username and password.
Console password
● Custom password
Set a strong password. Untick Users must create a new password at next sign-in for your own account.

Click Next. On the permissions page choose Add user to group and tick Administrators. Click through to Create user.

✓
Note the Sign-in URL on the success page — it looks like https://123456789012.signin.aws.amazon.com/console. Bookmark it. From now on, always sign in as this IAM user, not the root account.
3

Create a separate dedicated app user (taskboard-app)

Repeat the Add users flow for a programmatic-only user that your Node.js app uses. This user gets only the TaskboardAppPolicy — not AdministratorAccess.

User name*
taskboard-app
AWS Management Console access
Disabled — this user never logs in to the console

On the permissions page choose Attach policies directly and attach TaskboardAppPolicy (you create this policy in the next section). After creating the user, click it, go to Security credentials → Create access key. Select Application running outside AWS, create the key, and copy both values into your .env file. You cannot view the Secret Access Key again.

Phase 2 of 2 — Enable MFA on Both Accounts

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/security_credentials
AWS Console
1

Enable MFA on your new IAM user

Sign in as your new IAM user. Click your username in the top-right → Security credentials. Under Multi-factor authentication (MFA), click Assign MFA device.

MFA device name
my-phone
MFA device type
● Authenticator app
Use Google Authenticator, Authy, or 1Password. Scan the QR code shown in the console, then enter two consecutive 6-digit codes to verify.
🚫
Also enable MFA on your root account. Sign out, sign in as root, go to the top-right menu → Security credentials → Assign MFA. A root account without MFA is an open door.

Writing IAM Policies

Policies are JSON documents. The key fields are:

  • Effect — Allow or Deny
  • Action — the AWS API calls (e.g. s3:GetObject, sqs:SendMessage). Wildcards like s3:* are supported but avoid them in production.
  • Resource — the ARN(s) the action applies to. Use * only when the service doesn't support resource-level permissions.
  • Condition — optional constraints (e.g. only allow if source IP is in a range)
iam/taskboard-app-policy.jsonjson
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "S3Attachments",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "s3:GetObject",
        "s3:PutObject",
        "s3:DeleteObject"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::taskboard-attachments-*/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "S3ListBucket",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:ListBucket",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::taskboard-attachments-*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DynamoSessions",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "dynamodb:GetItem",
        "dynamodb:PutItem",
        "dynamodb:DeleteItem",
        "dynamodb:UpdateItem",
        "dynamodb:Query"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:dynamodb:us-east-1:*:table/taskboard-sessions",
        "arn:aws:dynamodb:us-east-1:*:table/taskboard-activity-logs"
      ]
    },
    {
      "Sid": "SQSSend",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "sqs:SendMessage",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:sqs:us-east-1:*:taskboard-notifications"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "SecretsRead",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
        "secretsmanager:DescribeSecret"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:*:secret:taskboard/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "CloudWatchLogs",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "logs:CreateLogGroup",
        "logs:CreateLogStream",
        "logs:PutLogEvents"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:logs:us-east-1:*:log-group:/taskboard/*"
    }
  ]
}

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/policies/create
AWS Console
1

Open IAM → Policies → Create policy

Go to IAM → Policies → Create policy. Click the JSON tab. Paste the full policy JSON from the file above. Click Next.

IAM › Policies › Create policy → JSON tab
💡
The Visual editor tab lets you build policies interactively by picking a service, then choosing actions and resources from dropdowns — useful for learning. The JSON tab is faster once you know what you need.
2

Name and create the policy

Policy name*
TaskboardAppPolicy
Description
Least-privilege policy for the TaskBoard Node.js application

Click Create policy.

3

Attach the policy to the taskboard-app user

Go to IAM → Users → taskboard-app → Add permissions → Attach policies directly. Search for TaskboardAppPolicy, tick it, and click Add permissions.

✓
The user now has exactly the permissions it needs — nothing more. Then go to Security credentials → Create access key (use case: Application running outside AWS) and save both values to your .env file.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create and attach the policybash
# Create the custom policy
aws iam create-policy \
  --policy-name TaskboardAppPolicy \
  --policy-document file://iam/taskboard-app-policy.json

# Attach to your app user (replace ACCOUNT_ID)
aws iam attach-user-policy \
  --user-name taskboard-app \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:policy/TaskboardAppPolicy

# Create access keys for this user
aws iam create-access-key --user-name taskboard-app
# Save the output! You cannot retrieve the SecretAccessKey again.

IAM Roles for EC2 and Lambda

When code runs inside AWS (on EC2 or Lambda), you should never use access keys. Instead, attach an IAM Role. The SDK fetches temporary credentials automatically from the EC2 Instance Metadata Service (IMDS) or the Lambda execution environment. These credentials rotate every 15 minutes.

Option A — AWS Console (recommended for beginners)

The console walks you through three distinct pages: first you create the Role, then you attach a policy to it, and finally you attach the Role to your EC2 instance. Follow these steps in order.

Phase 1 of 3 — Create the IAM Role

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/roles/create
AWS Console
1

Open the IAM Roles page

In the AWS Console top search bar, type IAM and click the result. In the left sidebar, click Roles. Then click the orange Create role button in the top-right.

AWS Console › IAM › Roles › Create role
Step 1 — Select trusted entity
2

Choose "AWS service" as the trusted entity type

You will see four tiles. Select AWS service — this means an AWS service (EC2 in this case) will be the one assuming the role, not a human user.

Trusted entity type*
● AWS service
Use case*
EC2
Allows EC2 instances to call AWS services on your behalf. This is the correct choice for a Node.js server running on EC2.
💡
The Use case drop-down filters to the most common role types. Select EC2 from the list. This pre-fills the Trust Policy so EC2 can assume the role — you don't need to write any JSON yourself.

Click Next.

Step 2 — Add permissions
3

Attach the TaskBoard custom policy

In the search box, type TaskboardAppPolicy (the policy you created earlier in this tutorial). Check the checkbox next to it when it appears in the results table.

Filter policies
TaskboardAppPolicy
If you haven't created it yet, you can also search for AmazonS3FullAccess as a temporary stand-in during development.
⚠️
Make sure you tick the checkbox in the table row — a yellow highlight and checkmark appear when it's selected. You can attach multiple policies here. Click Next when done.
Step 3 — Name, review, and create
4

Name the role and review

Give the role a clear, descriptive name. This is what you will see when attaching it to EC2 instances later.

Role name*
TaskboardEC2Role
Use a name that identifies the app and what this role is for. No spaces — use hyphens.
Description
Allows TaskBoard EC2 instances to access S3, DynamoDB, SQS and Secrets Manager
Tags (optional)
Project = taskboard  |  Environment = production
Tags help you find and filter resources later. Good habit to add them from the start.

Scroll down to review the Permissions summary and Trust policy panels — verify TaskboardAppPolicy is listed and the trust relationship shows ec2.amazonaws.com. Then click Create role.

✓
You will see a green success banner: "Role TaskboardEC2Role created." The role now exists but is not attached to anything yet — that happens in Phase 3.

Phase 2 of 3 — Verify the Trust Policy

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/roles/TaskboardEC2Role
AWS Console
1

Open the new role and check the Trust Relationships tab

From the success banner, click the role name link, or go to IAM → Roles and search for TaskboardEC2Role. Click the role to open its detail page, then click the Trust relationships tab.

IAM › Roles › TaskboardEC2Role › Trust relationships

You will see the auto-generated Trust Policy JSON. Confirm it looks like this — ec2.amazonaws.com must be the Principal:

Trusted entities
ec2.amazonaws.com
This is what lets EC2 instances assume the role. If you see anything else here, click "Edit trust policy" and correct it.
💡
The Permissions tab shows which policies are attached. The Trust relationships tab controls who can use this role. Both must be correct for the role to work.

Phase 3 of 3 — Attach the Role to your EC2 Instance

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#Instances
AWS Console
1

Navigate to EC2 Instances

In the top search bar type EC2 and click the result. In the left sidebar, click Instances under the Instances group. You will see a table listing all your running and stopped instances.

AWS Console › EC2 › Instances
2

Select your TaskBoard instance

Tick the checkbox on the left of your taskboard-server instance row. The row will highlight blue. Do not click the instance name link — that opens the detail panel instead of selecting for action.

💡
The instance can be running or stopped — you can attach a role to a stopped instance and it takes effect immediately when it next starts. For a running instance, the role is available within seconds of attaching.
3

Open Actions → Security → Modify IAM role

With the instance checkbox ticked, click the Actions dropdown button at the top of the table. Hover over Security to expand the submenu, then click Modify IAM role.

Actions › Security › Modify IAM role
⚠️
If Modify IAM role is greyed out, it means you have not selected an instance (checkbox not ticked), or you have selected more than one instance. Deselect all and re-select just the one you want.
4

Select TaskboardEC2Role from the drop-down

A new page opens with a single dropdown labelled IAM role. Click it and type TaskboardEC2Role to filter. Select it when it appears.

IAM role*
TaskboardEC2Role
Only roles with an EC2 trust policy appear here. If your role is missing, go back to IAM and verify the Trusted entity is ec2.amazonaws.com.

Click Update IAM role.

✓
Success banner: "Successfully modified IAM role of instance i-xxxxxxxxx." Your EC2 instance can now access S3, DynamoDB, SQS, and Secrets Manager with no access keys anywhere in your code.
5

Verify the role is attached

Go back to Instances, click your instance name to open its detail panel, and scroll down to the Security tab. Under IAM Role you should see TaskboardEC2Role as a clickable link.

EC2 › Instances › taskboard-server › Security

You can also verify from inside the instance by SSH-ing in and running:

curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/
# Should print: TaskboardEC2Role

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

The CLI approach does the same thing in four commands. Use this for automated provisioning scripts, CI/CD pipelines, or when you are comfortable with the terminal. The console walkthrough above is the best way to understand what each command actually does.

Terminal — create EC2 instance rolebash
# 1. Create a trust policy (allows EC2 to assume this role)
cat > ec2-trust-policy.json << 'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
  }]
}
EOF

# 2. Create the role
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name TaskboardEC2Role \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://ec2-trust-policy.json

# 3. Attach our custom policy
aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name TaskboardEC2Role \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:policy/TaskboardAppPolicy

# 4. Create an instance profile (required to attach role to EC2)
aws iam create-instance-profile --instance-profile-name TaskboardEC2Profile
aws iam add-role-to-instance-profile \
  --instance-profile-name TaskboardEC2Profile \
  --role-name TaskboardEC2Role
✅ Role vs User credentials

With a role attached, your Node.js code needs zero credential configuration. new S3Client({ region: 'us-east-1' }) is all you need — the SDK handles the rest. This is the production-grade approach.

IAM Policy Simulator

Before deploying, test your policies at policysim.aws.amazon.com — it simulates whether a given user/role can perform an action without making real API calls. Essential for debugging permission errors.

Option A — AWS Console

policysim.aws.amazon.com/home/index.jsp
AWS Console
1

Open the IAM Policy Simulator

Go to policysim.aws.amazon.com directly, or from the IAM console click Policy Simulator in the left sidebar under Access reports. Make sure you are signed in as your admin user.

IAM › Access reports › Policy Simulator
2

Select the user or role to test

In the left panel under Users, Groups, and Roles, find and click taskboard-app. The simulator loads all policies attached to that user.

💡
You can also test a role — expand Roles and select TaskboardEC2Role to verify that EC2 instances can access the services they need.
3

Choose a service and action to simulate

In the right panel, under Select service, choose S3. Under Select actions, tick PutObject. Then enter the resource ARN to test against.

Service
Amazon S3
Actions
☑ PutObject
Resource ARN (Simulation Settings)
arn:aws:s3:::taskboard-attachments-123456789012/tasks/1/file.pdf
Click Simulation Settings below the action list to enter a specific resource ARN. Without a resource, the simulator tests against all resources (*).

Click Run Simulation.

✓
The results panel shows allowed or implicitDeny for each action. Click a result row to expand it and see exactly which policy statement allowed or denied the action — invaluable for debugging.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — simulate a policybash
# Simulate whether taskboard-app can put objects to S3
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:user/taskboard-app \
  --action-names "s3:PutObject" \
  --resource-arns "arn:aws:s3:::taskboard-attachments-123456/tasks/1/file.pdf"

# Response will include EvalDecision: "allowed" or "implicitDeny"

Permission Boundaries

A permission boundary is an IAM managed policy that sets the maximum permissions an entity can have, regardless of what other policies are attached. Even if you attach AdministratorAccess to a role, a permission boundary can cap it to only S3 and DynamoDB access. The effective permissions are the intersection of identity-based policies AND the boundary.

Effective permissions = Identity Policy ∩ Permission Boundary
Identity policy allows: S3, EC2, RDS, Lambda
∩
Boundary allows: S3, DynamoDB only
=
Effective: S3 only

The primary use case is delegating role creation to developers safely. Without boundaries, a developer with iam:CreateRole permission could create a role with AdministratorAccess and use it to escalate their own privileges. With a boundary policy attached to all developer-created roles, any role they create is automatically capped.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/iam/home#/policies/create
AWS Console
1

Create the boundary policy

Go to IAM → Policies → Create policy → JSON tab. Paste the boundary policy JSON (e.g. allowing only s3:* and dynamodb:*). Name it TaskboardDeveloperBoundary and click Create policy.

2

Attach the boundary when creating a role

When creating a new role (IAM → Roles → Create role), on the permissions page scroll to the bottom and expand Set permissions boundary. Select Use a permissions boundary to control the maximum role permissions, then choose TaskboardDeveloperBoundary.

💡
To add a boundary to an existing role: open the role → Permissions tab → Set boundary button. The role's effective permissions are immediately capped to the intersection of its identity policies and the boundary — no restart or redeployment needed.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — attach a permission boundary to a rolebash
# Create a boundary policy (limits max permissions to S3 and DynamoDB)
aws iam create-policy \
  --policy-name TaskboardDeveloperBoundary \
  --policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[
      {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["s3:*","dynamodb:*"],"Resource":"*"}
    ]
  }'

# Attach the boundary when creating a role
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name developer-created-role \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://trust.json \
  --permissions-boundary arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:policy/TaskboardDeveloperBoundary

# Attach boundary to an existing role
aws iam put-role-permissions-boundary \
  --role-name developer-created-role \
  --permissions-boundary arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:policy/TaskboardDeveloperBoundary

Service Control Policies (SCPs)

SCPs are organization-wide guardrails set in AWS Organizations. They apply to entire AWS accounts and restrict what any principal in those accounts can do — including the root user. SCPs do not grant permissions; they define the ceiling of what identity policies inside the account can grant.

FeatureIAM PolicyPermission BoundarySCP
Applies toIndividual user/roleIndividual user/roleEntire AWS account
Can restrict root userNoNoYes
Grants permissionsYesNo (ceiling only)No (ceiling only)
Managed inIAMIAMAWS Organizations
Example SCP — deny leaving AWS Organizations and deny disabling GuardDutyjson
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyLeaveOrganization",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": "organizations:LeaveOrganization",
      "Resource": "*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DenyDisableGuardDuty",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": [
        "guardduty:DeleteDetector",
        "guardduty:DisassociateFromMasterAccount",
        "guardduty:StopMonitoringMembers"
      ],
      "Resource": "*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DenyRootUserActions",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": "*",
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringLike": { "aws:PrincipalArn": "arn:aws:iam::*:root" }
      }
    }
  ]
}
💡 Console — AWS Organizations → Service control policies

SCPs are managed at AWS Organizations → Policies → Service control policies. Enable SCPs for your organization first (Organizations → Policies → enable Service control policies). To create one: click Create policy, paste the JSON, then click Attach to apply it to an OU or account. All accounts in an OU inherit SCPs from parent OUs. The AWS console does not provide a dedicated JSON editor for SCPs beyond a plain text area — write the policy locally and paste it in, or use the CLI below for repeatability.

Terminal (CLI)

IAM Identity Center (SSO)

IAM Identity Center (formerly AWS SSO) replaces per-account IAM users with a single login that grants access to multiple AWS accounts and applications. Instead of creating an IAM user in every account and managing separate passwords and access keys, engineers log in once through the Identity Center portal and are granted temporary credentials to any account their permission set allows.

IAM Identity Center — multi-account access
Engineer logs in once
Identity Center portal or CLI
→
Identity Center
→
Dev account
Staging account
Prod account
ConceptWhat it is
Identity sourceWhere users are stored. Can be Identity Center's built-in directory, Active Directory (via AD Connector), or an external IdP (Okta, Google Workspace, Azure AD) via SAML 2.0.
Permission setA template of IAM policies assigned to a user for a specific account. Example: Developers get ReadOnlyAccess to Prod and PowerUserAccess to Dev.
Account assignmentPairs a user/group + permission set + AWS account. Creates a role in the target account that the user assumes when they select that account in the portal.
AWS access portalA web page (your_subdomain.awsapps.com/start) where users pick an account and role, then get console access or temporary CLI credentials.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/singlesignon/home
AWS Console
1

Enable IAM Identity Center

Search for IAM Identity Center in the AWS console. Click Enable. This is a one-time action per AWS Organization — it creates the Identity Center instance in your management account.

⚠️
Identity Center must be enabled in the AWS Organizations management account. If your account is not part of an organization, go to AWS Organizations → Create an organization first.
2

Create a user and a permission set

Go to Identity Center → Users → Add user. Fill in name, email, and send an invitation. Then go to Permission sets → Create permission set → select Predefined permission set → PowerUserAccess (or create a custom one). Name it DeveloperAccess.

3

Assign the user to an account with the permission set

Go to AWS accounts in the Identity Center sidebar. Select your account, click Assign users or groups. Select the user, select DeveloperAccess, click Submit.

✓
The user can now log in at the AWS access portal URL shown on the Identity Center dashboard, select the account, and get console or CLI access. No IAM user or access key needed.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — configure the AWS CLI to use Identity Centerbash
# Configure a named profile for SSO (run once per account/role)
aws configure sso
# SSO session name: my-org
# SSO start URL: https://mycompany.awsapps.com/start
# SSO region: us-east-1
# (opens browser to authenticate)
# Select the account and role to use
# CLI profile name: taskboard-dev

# Use the profile in any command
aws s3 ls --profile taskboard-dev

# Refresh credentials when they expire (8-hour sessions by default)
aws sso login --profile taskboard-dev

# List all configured SSO profiles
aws configure list-profiles
✅ When to use Identity Center

Use Identity Center for any team with more than 2–3 people, or any setup with more than one AWS account. It eliminates the need to manage access keys, rotate passwords, and create IAM users in every account. Engineers get temporary credentials that expire automatically — no long-lived keys to rotate or accidentally commit to git.

Networking
VPC — Virtual Private Cloud
A VPC is your own isolated section of the AWS network. Every EC2 instance, RDS database, and Lambda function lives inside a VPC. Understanding VPC is critical — misconfigured networking is the #1 reason apps fail to connect to databases or the internet. This section covers the full networking stack: subnets, gateways, routing, firewalls, flow logs, and VPC connectivity patterns.
SubnetsRoute TablesSecurity GroupsNAT GatewayVPC EndpointsNetwork ACLsFlow LogsVPC Peering

VPC building blocks

ComponentPurposeCovered in this section
VPCA logically isolated virtual network with your own IP address range (CIDR block, e.g. 10.0.0.0/16 — 65,536 IP addresses).Create the VPC
SubnetA subdivision of the VPC in a specific AZ (e.g. 10.0.1.0/24 — 256 IPs). Resources go in subnets, not directly in the VPC.Create the VPC
Internet Gateway (IGW)Connects your VPC to the public internet. Attach one IGW to the VPC, then route public subnets through it.Create the VPC
NAT GatewayAllows private subnet resources (RDS, Lambda) to make outbound internet calls without being publicly reachable. Lives in a public subnet. Costs $0.045/hr.NAT Gateway
Route TableA set of rules that determine where network traffic is directed. Public subnets route to the IGW; private subnets route to the NAT Gateway.Create the VPC
Security GroupA stateful virtual firewall for individual resources. Allows inbound/outbound rules by port, protocol, and source/destination. Return traffic automatically allowed.Security Groups
Network ACL (NACL)A stateless subnet-level firewall. Rules evaluated in number order. Must explicitly allow both directions. Supports DENY rules (security groups cannot deny).Network ACLs
VPC EndpointPrivate connection to AWS services (S3, DynamoDB, Secrets Manager) without NAT or IGW. Gateway type is free; Interface type costs ~$7/month.VPC Endpoints
VPC Flow LogsCaptures metadata for all IP traffic (source, destination, port, bytes, accepted/rejected). Sent to CloudWatch Logs or S3 for analysis.VPC Flow Logs
VPC PeeringPoint-to-point private connection between two VPCs. Resources communicate using private IPs. Non-transitive — requires route table entries in both VPCs.VPC Peering
Transit GatewayHub-and-spoke router connecting many VPCs and on-premise networks. Simpler than mesh peering beyond 3 VPCs. Costs $0.05/hr per attachment.Next steps

Recommended architecture: public + private subnets

For TaskBoard, we use a classic two-tier VPC layout: the EC2 app server is in a public subnet (reachable from the internet), while the RDS database is in a private subnet (no direct internet access).

TaskBoard VPC Layout (us-east-1, 2 AZs)
Internet
↕
Internet Gateway
AZ: us-east-1a
Public Subnet 10.0.1.0/24
EC2 App Server
Private Subnet 10.0.3.0/24
RDS Primary
AZ: us-east-1b
Public Subnet 10.0.2.0/24
NAT Gateway
Private Subnet 10.0.4.0/24
RDS Standby

Create the VPC

We will build the full two-tier network shown in the diagram above: one VPC, two public subnets (for EC2), two private subnets (for RDS), an Internet Gateway, and a route table that sends public traffic to the internet. Follow Phase A through Phase E in order.

Option A — AWS Console (recommended for beginners)

Phase A — Create the VPC

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#vpcs
AWS Console
1

Open the VPC Dashboard

In the AWS Console top search bar type VPC and click the result. You land on the VPC Dashboard. In the left sidebar click Your VPCs, then click the orange Create VPC button.

AWS Console › VPC › Your VPCs › Create VPC
VPC settings
2

Choose "VPC only" and set the IP range

At the top of the form you will see two options — VPC only and VPC and more. Select VPC only. We will create subnets manually so you understand what each piece does.

Resources to create*
● VPC only
Name tag
taskboard-vpc
This becomes a Name tag on the VPC so you can identify it in lists.
IPv4 CIDR*
10.0.0.0/16
This gives you 65,536 IP addresses (10.0.0.0 – 10.0.255.255) to carve subnets from. /16 is the standard starting point for a VPC.
IPv6 CIDR block
No IPv6 CIDR block
Tenancy
Default
Default tenancy means your instances share physical hardware with other AWS customers (fully isolated at software level). Dedicated tenancy costs significantly more and is not needed here.

Click Create VPC.

3

Enable DNS hostnames on the VPC

By default, EC2 instances in a VPC do not get public DNS names. We need to turn this on so we can SSH using a hostname instead of a raw IP. Tick the VPC checkbox in the list, then go to Actions → Edit VPC settings.

Your VPCs › taskboard-vpc › Actions › Edit VPC settings
Enable DNS hostnames
Enabled
Turn this on. Without it your EC2 instances will only have private DNS names and no public hostname.
Enable DNS resolution
Enabled
This should already be on by default. Keep it on — it lets instances resolve AWS service endpoints like S3 and RDS by name.

Click Save.

With this on, any EC2 instance launched here gets a public IP without extra configuration. Leave this OFF on private subnets.

Phase D — Create the Public Route Table

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#RouteTables
AWS Console
1

Create a new route table

In the left sidebar click Route tables. You will see one route table already listed — that is the main route table auto-created with your VPC. Do not delete it. Click Create route table.

Name
taskboard-public-rt
VPC*
taskboard-vpc (vpc-xxxxxxxxx)

Click Create route table.

2

Add a route to the Internet Gateway

You are taken to the new route table detail page. Click the Routes tab, then click Edit routes. You will see one default route (10.0.0.0/16 → local) — this lets all subnets in the VPC talk to each other. Click Add route to add the internet route.

Destination
0.0.0.0/0
This means "all traffic not going to a local subnet". It is the default route — the equivalent of a network's gateway of last resort.
Target*
Internet Gateway → taskboard-igw
Click the Target dropdown, select "Internet Gateway", then select taskboard-igw from the sub-list that appears.

Click Save changes.

💡
The private subnets will use the main route table which has no internet route — that is what makes them private. The private subnets are never associated with this new route table.
3

Associate both public subnets with this route table

Still on the taskboard-public-rt detail page, click the Subnet associations tab, then click Edit subnet associations.

taskboard-public-rt › Subnet associations › Edit subnet associations

Tick the checkboxes for taskboard-public-1a and taskboard-public-1b. Leave the private subnets unticked.

⚠️
Only tick the public subnets here. If you accidentally associate a private subnet with the public route table, your RDS database will become internet-reachable.

Click Save associations.

✓
Your VPC is now fully configured. Public subnets route to the internet via the IGW. Private subnets are isolated. You are ready to create security groups.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

The shell script below does everything in Phases A–D in a single run. Use it for repeatable, automated environment creation.

scripts/setup-vpc.shbash
#!/bin/bash
set -e

# 1. Create VPC
VPC_ID=$(aws ec2 create-vpc --cidr-block 10.0.0.0/16 \
  --query 'Vpc.VpcId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-tags --resources $VPC_ID --tags Key=Name,Value=taskboard-vpc
aws ec2 modify-vpc-attribute --vpc-id $VPC_ID --enable-dns-hostnames
echo "VPC: $VPC_ID"

# 2. Create Internet Gateway and attach
IGW_ID=$(aws ec2 create-internet-gateway --query 'InternetGateway.InternetGatewayId' --output text)
aws ec2 attach-internet-gateway --internet-gateway-id $IGW_ID --vpc-id $VPC_ID

# 3. Create public subnets (in 2 AZs for HA)
PUB1=$(aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.1.0/24 \
  --availability-zone us-east-1a --query 'Subnet.SubnetId' --output text)
PUB2=$(aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.2.0/24 \
  --availability-zone us-east-1b --query 'Subnet.SubnetId' --output text)

# 4. Create private subnets (for RDS — no direct internet access)
PRIV1=$(aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.3.0/24 \
  --availability-zone us-east-1a --query 'Subnet.SubnetId' --output text)
PRIV2=$(aws ec2 create-subnet --vpc-id $VPC_ID --cidr-block 10.0.4.0/24 \
  --availability-zone us-east-1b --query 'Subnet.SubnetId' --output text)

# 5. Create and configure public route table
PUB_RT=$(aws ec2 create-route-table --vpc-id $VPC_ID --query 'RouteTable.RouteTableId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-route --route-table-id $PUB_RT --destination-cidr-block 0.0.0.0/0 --gateway-id $IGW_ID
aws ec2 associate-route-table --route-table-id $PUB_RT --subnet-id $PUB1
aws ec2 associate-route-table --route-table-id $PUB_RT --subnet-id $PUB2

# Enable auto-assign public IPs in public subnets
aws ec2 modify-subnet-attribute --subnet-id $PUB1 --map-public-ip-on-launch
aws ec2 modify-subnet-attribute --subnet-id $PUB2 --map-public-ip-on-launch

echo "Public subnets: $PUB1, $PUB2"
echo "Private subnets: $PRIV1, $PRIV2"
echo "Save these IDs — needed for EC2 and RDS setup!"

Security Groups

Security groups are your primary traffic filter. They are stateful — if you allow inbound traffic on port 3000, the response automatically flows back out. You don't need to add an outbound rule for each inbound rule. We need two: one for EC2, one for RDS.

Option A — AWS Console

Security Group 1 of 2 — EC2 (taskboard-ec2-sg)

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#SecurityGroups
AWS Console
1

Open Security Groups under EC2

In the AWS Console search for EC2. In the left sidebar scroll down to Network & Security and click Security Groups. Click Create security group.

EC2 › Network & Security › Security Groups › Create security group
Basic details
2

Name it and assign it to taskboard-vpc

Security group name*
taskboard-ec2-sg
Description*
TaskBoard EC2 — SSH, HTTP, App port
VPC*
taskboard-vpc (vpc-xxxxxxxxx)
Security groups are VPC-scoped. Make sure you select taskboard-vpc and not the default VPC.
Inbound rules
3

Add three inbound rules

Click Add rule three times to add the rows below. The table shows what to fill in for each row.

TypeProtocolPort rangeSourceDescription
SSHTCP22 My IP — click the Source dropdown and select "My IP". AWS fills in your current public IP automatically. SSH access — my laptop only
Custom TCPTCP3000 0.0.0.0/0 — select "Anywhere-IPv4" Node.js app port
HTTPTCP80 0.0.0.0/0 — select "Anywhere-IPv4" HTTP traffic
⚠️
For SSH, always use My IP rather than 0.0.0.0/0. Opening port 22 to the world invites automated brute-force login attempts within minutes of launch.
Outbound rules
4

Leave outbound rules as default

The default outbound rule allows all traffic out (0.0.0.0/0 on all ports). Leave this in place — your EC2 instance needs to reach the internet to download packages, call S3, connect to RDS, etc.

Outbound rule (default)
All traffic → 0.0.0.0/0
Do not delete this rule. Security groups are stateful — inbound rules are what matter for controlling who can reach your server.

Click Create security group.

✓
EC2 security group created. Note the Security group ID (sg-xxxxxxxxx) — you will use it as the source when creating the RDS security group next.

Security Group 2 of 2 — RDS (taskboard-rds-sg)

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#SecurityGroups
AWS Console
1

Create a second security group for RDS

Still in EC2 → Security Groups, click Create security group again.

Security group name*
taskboard-rds-sg
Description*
TaskBoard RDS — PostgreSQL from EC2 only
VPC*
taskboard-vpc (vpc-xxxxxxxxx)
Inbound rules — the critical security step
2

Add one inbound rule — source is the EC2 security group

Click Add rule. This is the most important security configuration in the tutorial — you are not using an IP address as the source, you are using the other security group as the source. This means only EC2 instances assigned taskboard-ec2-sg can reach the database.

TypeProtocolPort rangeSourceDescription
PostgreSQLTCP5432 Select Custom from the source dropdown, then in the text field type sg- and select taskboard-ec2-sg from the autocomplete list. PostgreSQL from EC2 only
💡
Using a security group as the source is the correct pattern. If you used a specific IP address, you'd need to update this rule every time your EC2 instance changed IPs. With the security group reference, any EC2 instance assigned taskboard-ec2-sg automatically gets database access — now and in the future.
3

Remove the default outbound rule, then save

For a database security group, best practice is to delete the default outbound allow-all rule. Your database should only be accepting connections, never initiating outbound traffic to arbitrary destinations. Click the ✕ on the default outbound rule to remove it, then click Create security group.

✓
RDS security group created. Your network is now fully locked down: EC2 is reachable from the internet on ports 22 (your IP only), 80 and 3000. RDS is only reachable from EC2 — invisible to the outside world.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Creates both security groups and all rules in one script. Useful once you know what each rule does.

scripts/setup-security-groups.shbash
#!/bin/bash
VPC_ID="vpc-xxxxxxxx"  # from previous script
MY_IP=$(curl -s https://checkip.amazonaws.com)

# EC2 Security Group: allow SSH from your IP, HTTP from anywhere
EC2_SG=$(aws ec2 create-security-group \
  --group-name taskboard-ec2-sg \
  --description "TaskBoard EC2 security group" \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --query 'GroupId' --output text)

aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-id $EC2_SG \
  --protocol tcp --port 22 --cidr ${MY_IP}/32    # SSH — your IP only

aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-id $EC2_SG \
  --protocol tcp --port 3000 --cidr 0.0.0.0/0   # App port — public

aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-id $EC2_SG \
  --protocol tcp --port 80 --cidr 0.0.0.0/0     # HTTP

# RDS Security Group: ONLY allow traffic from EC2 security group
# This is the key security pattern — DB is never directly reachable
RDS_SG=$(aws ec2 create-security-group \
  --group-name taskboard-rds-sg \
  --description "TaskBoard RDS security group" \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --query 'GroupId' --output text)

aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-id $RDS_SG \
  --protocol tcp --port 5432 \
  --source-group $EC2_SG   # Only EC2 instances in EC2_SG can connect

echo "EC2 SG: $EC2_SG"
echo "RDS SG: $RDS_SG"
✅ Security group best practice

Reference security groups in the source, not IP ranges, wherever possible. --source-group $EC2_SG means "any resource assigned EC2_SG" — so as your fleet scales, new EC2 instances automatically get database access without manual IP updates.

NAT Gateway

A NAT (Network Address Translation) Gateway allows resources in private subnets — your RDS database, Lambda functions running in a VPC — to make outbound internet connections (download patches, call external APIs) without being directly reachable from the internet. It lives in a public subnet and acts as the private subnet's door to the outside world.

NAT Gateway traffic flow
RDS / Lambda (private subnet)
→
NAT Gateway (public subnet)
→
Internet Gateway
→
Internet
Inbound connections from the internet cannot reach the private subnet — only outbound traffic initiated from inside is allowed.
⚠️ NAT Gateway costs money

A NAT Gateway costs $0.045/hr (~$33/month) plus $0.045/GB of data processed. For this tutorial's free-tier setup skip it. Add it when you have Lambda functions or other private-subnet resources that must call external APIs or download packages.

Option A — AWS Console

Phase 1 of 2 — Create the NAT Gateway

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#NatGateways
AWS Console
1

Open NAT Gateways and click Create

In the VPC left sidebar click NAT Gateways, then click Create NAT gateway.

VPC › NAT gateways › Create NAT gateway
2

Configure and allocate an Elastic IP

Name
taskboard-nat-gw
Subnet*
taskboard-public-1a (10.0.1.0/24)
NAT Gateways must live in a public subnet — they need a path to the Internet Gateway. Place it in us-east-1a to match your primary private subnet.
Connectivity type
● Public
Elastic IP allocation ID*
Allocate Elastic IP
Click Allocate Elastic IP — the NAT Gateway needs a static public IP. This Elastic IP is used for all outbound traffic from your private subnets.

Click Create NAT gateway. Status shows Pending for ~60 seconds then Available.

Phase 2 of 2 — Add a route in the private route table

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#RouteTables
AWS Console
1

Create a private route table and point it at the NAT Gateway

In the VPC sidebar click Route tables → Create route table.

Name
taskboard-private-rt
VPC
taskboard-vpc

Click Create route table. On the detail page click Routes → Edit routes → Add route.

Destination
0.0.0.0/0
Target
NAT Gateway → taskboard-nat-gw
This routes all outbound traffic from private subnets through the NAT Gateway — not the Internet Gateway. Private resources can initiate connections out but are unreachable from outside.

Click Save changes. Then on the Subnet associations tab, associate both private subnets (taskboard-private-1a and taskboard-private-1b) with this route table.

✓
Private subnets now have outbound internet access through the NAT Gateway. RDS can download OS updates, Lambda can call external APIs — but nothing from the internet can initiate a connection inward.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create NAT Gateway and private route tablebash
# Allocate an Elastic IP for the NAT Gateway
NAT_EIP=$(aws ec2 allocate-address --domain vpc --query 'AllocationId' --output text)

# Create the NAT Gateway in the first public subnet
NAT_GW=$(aws ec2 create-nat-gateway \
  --subnet-id $PUB1 \
  --allocation-id $NAT_EIP \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=natgateway,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-nat-gw}]' \
  --query 'NatGateway.NatGatewayId' --output text)

echo "Waiting for NAT Gateway to become available..."
aws ec2 wait nat-gateway-available --nat-gateway-ids $NAT_GW
echo "NAT Gateway ready: $NAT_GW"

# Create a route table for the private subnets
PRIV_RT=$(aws ec2 create-route-table \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --query 'RouteTable.RouteTableId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-tags --resources $PRIV_RT \
  --tags Key=Name,Value=taskboard-private-rt

# Route all outbound traffic from private subnets through the NAT Gateway
aws ec2 create-route \
  --route-table-id $PRIV_RT \
  --destination-cidr-block 0.0.0.0/0 \
  --nat-gateway-id $NAT_GW

# Associate both private subnets with this route table
aws ec2 associate-route-table --route-table-id $PRIV_RT --subnet-id $PRIV1
aws ec2 associate-route-table --route-table-id $PRIV_RT --subnet-id $PRIV2

echo "Private route table configured: $PRIV_RT"

VPC Endpoints

VPC Endpoints let resources in your private subnets communicate with AWS services like S3, DynamoDB, and Secrets Manager without leaving the AWS network — no NAT Gateway, no Internet Gateway, no data transfer charges. This is both cheaper and more secure than routing through the public internet.

Endpoint typeSupported servicesCostHow it works
Gateway endpointS3, DynamoDB onlyFreeAdds an entry to your route table. Traffic to S3/DynamoDB is routed directly within AWS.
Interface endpointEvery other AWS service (Secrets Manager, SQS, SNS, CloudWatch, etc.)~$7/month per endpointCreates a private network interface in your subnet with a private IP. DNS resolves the service hostname to this IP.

Option A — AWS Console

Create Gateway endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB (free)

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#Endpoints
AWS Console
1

Open Endpoints and click Create endpoint

In the VPC left sidebar click Endpoints → Create endpoint.

VPC › Endpoints › Create endpoint
S3 Gateway endpoint
2

Configure the S3 Gateway endpoint

Name tag
taskboard-s3-endpoint
Service category
● AWS services
Services
com.amazonaws.us-east-1.s3 (Type: Gateway)
Search for s3 and select the one with Type: Gateway — not the Interface type. Gateway endpoints are free and always preferred for S3 and DynamoDB.
VPC
taskboard-vpc
Route tables
☑ taskboard-private-rt   ☑ taskboard-public-rt
Tick both route tables. The endpoint adds an automatic route entry — all traffic to S3 is redirected through the endpoint instead of going to the internet.
Policy
● Full access
Full access allows any resource in your VPC to access any S3 bucket. Restrict this to your specific bucket ARN in a production security-hardened environment.

Click Create endpoint. Repeat for DynamoDB: search dynamodb, select Type: Gateway, tick both route tables.

✓
S3 and DynamoDB traffic from your EC2 instances and Lambda functions now travels entirely within AWS — never touches the public internet, no data transfer charges, faster latency.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create S3 and DynamoDB Gateway endpointsbash
# Get the route table IDs for your VPC
RT_IDS=$(aws ec2 describe-route-tables \
  --filters "Name=vpc-id,Values=$VPC_ID" \
  --query 'RouteTables[*].RouteTableId' \
  --output text | tr '\t' ' ')

# Create S3 Gateway endpoint (free — adds route to route tables)
aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --service-name com.amazonaws.us-east-1.s3 \
  --route-table-ids $RT_IDS \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=vpc-endpoint,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-s3-endpoint}]'

# Create DynamoDB Gateway endpoint (free)
aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --service-name com.amazonaws.us-east-1.dynamodb \
  --route-table-ids $RT_IDS \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=vpc-endpoint,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-dynamodb-endpoint}]'

# Verify — gateway endpoint routes appear as "vpce-..." in the route table
aws ec2 describe-route-tables \
  --filters "Name=vpc-id,Values=$VPC_ID" \
  --query 'RouteTables[*].Routes[?GatewayId!=null]'

Network ACLs (NACLs)

Network ACLs are a stateless subnet-level firewall. Unlike security groups (which are stateful — responses are automatically allowed), NACLs evaluate every packet independently in both directions. Rules are numbered and evaluated lowest-first, stopping at the first match. They act as a second line of defence at the subnet boundary, before traffic reaches individual security groups.

FeatureSecurity GroupNetwork ACL
Applies toIndividual resource (EC2, RDS)Entire subnet
StateStateful — return traffic auto-allowedStateless — must explicitly allow both directions
Rule evaluationAll rules evaluated, most permissive winsLowest rule number matches first, stops there
DefaultDeny all inbound, allow all outboundAllow all inbound and outbound (default NACL)
Supports DENYNo — only ALLOW rulesYes — can explicitly block IPs or ranges

Option A — AWS Console

Create a custom NACL for private subnets

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#acls
AWS Console
1

Create the NACL

In the VPC left sidebar click Network ACLs → Create network ACL.

VPC › Network ACLs › Create network ACL
Name
taskboard-private-nacl
VPC
taskboard-vpc

Click Create network ACL. A new NACL starts with a DENY ALL rule for both inbound and outbound — you must add explicit allow rules.

⚠️
Because NACLs are stateless, you must add rules in both directions. For PostgreSQL on port 5432 you need an inbound rule allowing port 5432 from the EC2 subnet, AND an outbound rule allowing the ephemeral port range (1024–65535) back to the EC2 subnet.
2

Add inbound rules

Click the Inbound rules tab → Edit inbound rules → Add new rule.

Rule #TypeProtocolPortSourceAllow/Deny
100PostgreSQLTCP543210.0.1.0/24 (EC2 public subnet 1a)ALLOW
110PostgreSQLTCP543210.0.2.0/24 (EC2 public subnet 1b)ALLOW
*All trafficAllAll0.0.0.0/0DENY (auto)

Click Save changes.

3

Add outbound rules (required — NACLs are stateless)

Click the Outbound rules tab → Edit outbound rules → Add new rule.

Rule #TypeProtocolPortDestinationAllow/Deny
100Custom TCPTCP1024–6553510.0.1.0/24ALLOW
110Custom TCPTCP1024–6553510.0.2.0/24ALLOW
*All trafficAllAll0.0.0.0/0DENY (auto)
Ephemeral ports (1024–65535) are the random source ports used by clients to receive responses. Since NACLs are stateless, you must explicitly allow these return ports outbound.
4

Associate with the private subnets

Click the Subnet associations tab → Edit subnet associations. Tick taskboard-private-1a and taskboard-private-1b. Click Save changes.

✓
The private subnets now have a subnet-level firewall. Only traffic from your EC2 public subnets can reach port 5432 — all other inbound traffic is denied at the NACL before even reaching the RDS security group.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create and configure a NACL for private subnetsbash
# Create the NACL
NACL_ID=$(aws ec2 create-network-acl \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=network-acl,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-private-nacl}]' \
  --query 'NetworkAcl.NetworkAclId' --output text)

# Inbound: allow PostgreSQL from both EC2 public subnets
aws ec2 create-network-acl-entry --network-acl-id $NACL_ID \
  --ingress --rule-number 100 --protocol tcp \
  --port-range From=5432,To=5432 --cidr-block 10.0.1.0/24 --rule-action allow

aws ec2 create-network-acl-entry --network-acl-id $NACL_ID \
  --ingress --rule-number 110 --protocol tcp \
  --port-range From=5432,To=5432 --cidr-block 10.0.2.0/24 --rule-action allow

# Outbound: allow ephemeral ports back to EC2 subnets (stateless — required!)
aws ec2 create-network-acl-entry --network-acl-id $NACL_ID \
  --egress --rule-number 100 --protocol tcp \
  --port-range From=1024,To=65535 --cidr-block 10.0.1.0/24 --rule-action allow

aws ec2 create-network-acl-entry --network-acl-id $NACL_ID \
  --egress --rule-number 110 --protocol tcp \
  --port-range From=1024,To=65535 --cidr-block 10.0.2.0/24 --rule-action allow

# Associate with both private subnets
# First find the existing default NACL association IDs for the private subnets
for SUBNET_ID in $PRIV1 $PRIV2; do
  ASSOC_ID=$(aws ec2 describe-network-acls \
    --filters "Name=association.subnet-id,Values=$SUBNET_ID" \
    --query 'NetworkAcls[0].Associations[?SubnetId==`'"$SUBNET_ID"'`].NetworkAclAssociationId' \
    --output text)
  aws ec2 replace-network-acl-association \
    --association-id $ASSOC_ID \
    --network-acl-id $NACL_ID
done

echo "NACL $NACL_ID associated with private subnets"

VPC Flow Logs

VPC Flow Logs capture metadata about every IP packet accepted or rejected by network interfaces in your VPC — source IP, destination IP, port, protocol, bytes, and whether it was accepted or rejected. They are essential for security auditing, troubleshooting connectivity issues, and detecting anomalous traffic.

💡 Flow Logs vs packet capture

Flow Logs record connection metadata, not packet contents. They tell you "EC2 instance i-xxx sent 5,000 bytes to 1.2.3.4 on port 443 and it was accepted" — not what was in those bytes. For full packet inspection you need a dedicated network capture tool.

Option A — AWS Console

Enable Flow Logs on the VPC

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#vpcs
AWS Console
1

Open the VPC and go to Flow logs tab

In the VPC sidebar click Your VPCs. Tick taskboard-vpc. Click the Flow logs tab at the bottom of the page. Click Create flow log.

VPC › Your VPCs › taskboard-vpc › Flow logs › Create flow log
2

Configure the flow log destination

Name
taskboard-vpc-flow-logs
Filter
● All
Capture both accepted and rejected traffic. Choose Reject only if storage cost is a concern — rejected traffic is often the most interesting for security auditing.
Maximum aggregation interval
10 minutes
How often flow records are aggregated and published. 1 minute gives near-real-time data but costs more in CloudWatch Logs ingestion. 10 minutes is a good balance.
Destination
● Send to CloudWatch Logs
CloudWatch Logs lets you query flow logs with Logs Insights. For large-scale analysis, send to S3 and query with Athena instead — cheaper at high volume.
Destination log group
/taskboard/vpc-flow-logs
Type a new log group name — it will be created automatically.
IAM role
Create new role — flowlogsRole
Click Set up permissions to auto-create an IAM role that allows VPC Flow Logs to write to CloudWatch. This is a one-time setup.

Click Create flow log.

✓
Flow logs begin arriving in CloudWatch within a few minutes. Query them in Logs Insights: fields @timestamp, srcAddr, dstAddr, dstPort, action | filter action = "REJECT" | sort @timestamp desc to see all blocked connections.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — enable VPC Flow Logs to CloudWatchbash
# Create the log group
aws logs create-log-group --log-group-name /taskboard/vpc-flow-logs
aws logs put-retention-policy \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/vpc-flow-logs \
  --retention-in-days 30

# Create an IAM role that allows Flow Logs to write to CloudWatch
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name flowlogsRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[{
      "Effect":"Allow",
      "Principal":{"Service":"vpc-flow-logs.amazonaws.com"},
      "Action":"sts:AssumeRole"
    }]
  }'

aws iam put-role-policy \
  --role-name flowlogsRole \
  --policy-name flowlogsPolicy \
  --policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[{
      "Effect":"Allow",
      "Action":["logs:CreateLogStream","logs:PutLogEvents","logs:DescribeLogGroups","logs:DescribeLogStreams"],
      "Resource":"*"
    }]
  }'

FLOW_ROLE_ARN=$(aws iam get-role --role-name flowlogsRole --query 'Role.Arn' --output text)

# Enable flow logs on the VPC
aws ec2 create-flow-logs \
  --resource-type VPC \
  --resource-ids $VPC_ID \
  --traffic-type ALL \
  --log-destination-type cloud-watch-logs \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/vpc-flow-logs \
  --deliver-logs-permission-arn $FLOW_ROLE_ARN \
  --max-aggregation-interval 600

echo "Flow logs enabled for VPC $VPC_ID"
CloudWatch Logs Insights — useful flow log queriessql
-- All rejected connections (blocked by security group or NACL)
fields @timestamp, srcAddr, dstAddr, dstPort, protocol, action
| filter action = "REJECT"
| sort @timestamp desc
| limit 50

-- Top source IPs by data volume (detect heavy users or exfiltration)
fields srcAddr, bytes
| stats sum(bytes) as totalBytes by srcAddr
| sort totalBytes desc
| limit 20

-- Connections to a specific port (e.g. someone scanning port 22)
fields @timestamp, srcAddr, dstAddr, dstPort, action
| filter dstPort = 22
| stats count() as attempts by srcAddr
| sort attempts desc

-- Traffic between EC2 and RDS (verify DB connections are working)
fields @timestamp, srcAddr, dstAddr, dstPort, bytes, action
| filter dstPort = 5432
| sort @timestamp desc

VPC Peering

VPC Peering connects two VPCs so resources in each can communicate using private IP addresses — as if they were on the same network. Common use cases: connecting a dev VPC to a staging VPC, sharing a central services VPC (bastion host, CI/CD agents, monitoring), or connecting VPCs across AWS accounts.

VPC Peering — TaskBoard dev ↔ staging
VPC A (dev)
10.0.0.0/16
↔ Peering connection ↔
VPC B (staging)
10.1.0.0/16
CIDR blocks must not overlap. Each VPC's route tables must have a route pointing the other VPC's CIDR at the peering connection.
💡 VPC Peering vs Transit Gateway

VPC Peering is point-to-point and free (you pay only for data transfer). With 3 VPCs you need 3 peering connections; with 10 VPCs you need 45. Transit Gateway is a hub-and-spoke router — every VPC connects once to the TGW and can reach all others. Costs $0.05/hr per attachment but is far simpler to manage beyond 3–4 VPCs.

Option A — AWS Console

Create a VPC Peering connection

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#PeeringConnections
AWS Console
1

Create the peering connection (requester side)

In the VPC sidebar click Peering connections → Create peering connection.

VPC › Peering connections › Create peering connection
Name
taskboard-dev-to-staging
VPC ID (Requester)*
taskboard-vpc (10.0.0.0/16) — dev
Account
● My account
For cross-account peering, select Another account and enter the accepter account's Account ID and VPC ID. The accepter account owner must then accept the request.
VPC ID (Accepter)*
taskboard-staging-vpc (10.1.0.0/16)
The CIDR blocks of the two VPCs must not overlap. 10.0.x.x and 10.1.x.x do not overlap — this is correct.

Click Create peering connection.

2

Accept the peering request

The connection shows Pending acceptance. Tick it and click Actions → Accept request. For same-account peering you accept immediately. For cross-account peering, the accepter account owner must do this step in their own console.

💡
Peering is established — but traffic still won't flow. You must add routes in both VPCs' route tables pointing each other's CIDR at the peering connection ID, and update security groups to allow the other VPC's CIDR as a source.
3

Add routes in both VPCs' route tables

Go to Route tables. Open taskboard-public-rt (dev VPC). Click Routes → Edit routes → Add route.

Destination
10.1.0.0/16 (staging VPC CIDR)
Target
Peering Connection → taskboard-dev-to-staging

Save. Then repeat in the staging VPC's route table: destination 10.0.0.0/16 → same peering connection. Finally update security groups in each VPC to allow the other VPC's CIDR block on the required ports.

✓
Resources in both VPCs can now communicate using private IPs. Test with ping or nc -zv 10.1.x.x PORT from an EC2 instance in the dev VPC.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — VPC Peering between dev and staging VPCsbash
DEV_VPC_ID="vpc-dev-xxxxxxxx"       # taskboard-vpc (10.0.0.0/16)
STAGING_VPC_ID="vpc-stg-xxxxxxxx"  # taskboard-staging-vpc (10.1.0.0/16)

# 1. Create the peering connection
PEERING_ID=$(aws ec2 create-vpc-peering-connection \
  --vpc-id $DEV_VPC_ID \
  --peer-vpc-id $STAGING_VPC_ID \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=vpc-peering-connection,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-dev-to-staging}]' \
  --query 'VpcPeeringConnection.VpcPeeringConnectionId' --output text)

# 2. Accept the connection (same account — can do immediately)
aws ec2 accept-vpc-peering-connection \
  --vpc-peering-connection-id $PEERING_ID

# 3. Add routes in dev VPC's route tables
DEV_RT="rtb-dev-xxxxxxxx"  # dev public route table ID
aws ec2 create-route \
  --route-table-id $DEV_RT \
  --destination-cidr-block 10.1.0.0/16 \
  --vpc-peering-connection-id $PEERING_ID

# 4. Add routes in staging VPC's route tables
STG_RT="rtb-stg-xxxxxxxx"  # staging route table ID
aws ec2 create-route \
  --route-table-id $STG_RT \
  --destination-cidr-block 10.0.0.0/16 \
  --vpc-peering-connection-id $PEERING_ID

echo "VPC Peering established: $PEERING_ID"
echo "Remember to update security groups to allow the peer VPC CIDR as a source"
✅ Security group best practice

Reference security groups in the source, not IP ranges, wherever possible. --source-group $EC2_SG means "any resource assigned EC2_SG" — so as your fleet scales, new EC2 instances automatically get database access without manual IP updates.

AWS PrivateLink

AWS PrivateLink lets you expose a service running in your VPC to other VPCs (even in different accounts) privately — without VPC peering, without an Internet Gateway, and without making the service public. Traffic never leaves the AWS network. It is the technology powering Interface VPC Endpoints.

PrivateLink — expose TaskBoard API to a partner account privately
Partner VPC
Interface endpoint (ENI)
→ private IP →
PrivateLink
Endpoint Service
→
Your VPC
NLB → EC2 (TaskBoard API)
No VPC peering, no IGW, no public IPs needed. Partner traffic arrives at your NLB via a private IP in their subnet.

How it works

  • You (provider) create an Endpoint Service backed by a Network Load Balancer (NLB) in your VPC. Your actual service (EC2, containers) sits behind the NLB. You can whitelist which AWS accounts are allowed to connect.
  • Consumer creates an Interface Endpoint in their VPC pointing at your service name. This places an ENI (elastic network interface) with a private IP directly in their subnet. DNS resolves the service name to this private IP.
  • Traffic flows from the consumer's ENI → PrivateLink → your NLB → your service. The consumer never knows your real instance IPs.

Option A — AWS Console

Create an Endpoint Service (provider side)

console.aws.amazon.com/vpc/home#EndpointServices
AWS Console
1

Open Endpoint services and click Create

In the VPC sidebar click Endpoint services → Create endpoint service.

VPC › Endpoint services › Create endpoint service
Load balancer type
● Network
PrivateLink requires a Network Load Balancer (NLB). Create an NLB in front of your EC2 instances first (at least port 3000 listener → target group of your EC2 instances).
Available load balancers
taskboard-nlb
Acceptance required
Enabled — you approve each consumer connection request
With acceptance required, consumers send a connection request that you must explicitly approve. Disable this only for fully automated, trusted consumer accounts.

Click Create. Copy the Service name shown (e.g. com.amazonaws.vpce.us-east-1.vpce-svc-xxxxxxxx) — share this with the consumer account.

2

Whitelist the consumer account

Click the Allow principals tab → Add principals. Enter the consumer's AWS account ARN: arn:aws:iam::CONSUMER_ACCOUNT_ID:root.

✓
The consumer can now create an Interface Endpoint pointing at your service name. When they do, a connection request appears in the Endpoint connections tab for you to accept.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create an endpoint service (provider)bash
NLB_ARN="arn:aws:elasticloadbalancing:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:loadbalancer/net/taskboard-nlb/xxxx"

# Create the endpoint service backed by the NLB
SERVICE_ID=$(aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint-service-configuration \
  --network-load-balancer-arns $NLB_ARN \
  --acceptance-required \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=vpc-endpoint-service,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-api-endpoint-service}]' \
  --query 'ServiceConfiguration.ServiceId' --output text)

echo "Endpoint Service: $SERVICE_ID"
# Get the service name to share with consumers
aws ec2 describe-vpc-endpoint-service-configurations \
  --service-ids $SERVICE_ID \
  --query 'ServiceConfigurations[0].ServiceName' --output text

# Whitelist consumer account
aws ec2 modify-vpc-endpoint-service-permissions \
  --service-id $SERVICE_ID \
  --add-allowed-principals arn:aws:iam::CONSUMER_ACCOUNT_ID:root

# Consumer side: create an interface endpoint to your service
aws ec2 create-vpc-endpoint \
  --vpc-id $CONSUMER_VPC_ID \
  --vpc-endpoint-type Interface \
  --service-name com.amazonaws.vpce.us-east-1.vpce-svc-xxxxxxxx \
  --subnet-ids $CONSUMER_SUBNET_ID \
  --security-group-ids $CONSUMER_SG_ID
Service 2 of 7
EC2 — Elastic Compute Cloud
EC2 gives you virtual Linux/Windows servers in the cloud. You have full root/Administrator access, choose the OS and size, and pay by the hour. This is where the TaskBoard Express.js server runs.
InstancesAMIsUser DataEBSElastic IPAuto ScalingALBLaunch Templates

Instance types explained

Instance type names encode their family, generation, and size: t3.micro = family t (burstable), generation 3, size micro.

FamilyOptimized forExampleUse case
t3/t4gBurstable general purposet3.micro, t3.smallDev/staging, low traffic web apps. Free tier: t2.micro
m6i/m7gBalanced compute + memorym6i.large, m6i.xlargeProduction web servers, app servers
c6i/c7gCompute optimizedc6i.largeCPU-intensive: video encoding, ML inference
r6i/r7gMemory optimizedr6i.largeIn-memory caches, heavy databases

Launch EC2 with User Data (automated setup)

User Data is a bootstrap script that runs once when the instance first starts. Use it to install Node.js and deploy your app automatically — no manual SSH needed. The console wizard walks you through every setting on a single page.

Option A — AWS Console

Phase 1 of 2 — Create a Key Pair

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#KeyPairs
AWS Console
1

Create a key pair for SSH access

In EC2's left sidebar under Network & Security, click Key Pairs. Click Create key pair.

EC2 › Network & Security › Key Pairs › Create key pair
Name*
taskboard-key
Key pair type
● RSA
Private key file format
● .pem (for Mac/Linux)
If you are on Windows and use PuTTY, choose .ppk instead. For WSL or PowerShell with OpenSSH, use .pem.

Click Create key pair. Your browser immediately downloads taskboard-key.pem. Move it somewhere safe — ~/.ssh/taskboard-key.pem is conventional. You cannot download it again.

🚫
Never commit .pem files to git. Add *.pem to your .gitignore immediately. If a key is ever exposed, delete it in the console and create a new one.

Phase 2 of 2 — Launch the Instance

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#LaunchInstances
AWS Console

Paste the full User Data script from Option B below into this text area. It runs automatically as root on first boot — no SSH required for initial setup.
💡
The User Data field accepts plain text. Paste the script from the code block below verbatim, starting with #!/bin/bash. Execution logs are written to /var/log/cloud-init-output.log — check there if the app doesn't start.
9

Review the summary panel and launch

✓
Green banner: "Successfully initiated launch of instance i-xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx". Click the instance ID link to go to the Instances page. The Instance state column will show Pending for 30–60 seconds, then switch to Running. The Status check column will show Initializing for another 1–2 minutes while the User Data script runs.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

The User Data script and run-instances command below produce an identical result to the console walkthrough above. Copy the User Data script first — you also paste it into the console's Advanced details field in Step 8 above.

scripts/ec2-userdata.shbash
#!/bin/bash
# This script runs as root when the EC2 instance first boots.
# Logs are written to /var/log/cloud-init-output.log

set -e
exec > >(tee /var/log/userdata.log|logger -t user-data -s 2>/dev/console) 2>&1

# Update the system
dnf update -y

# Install Node.js 20 via NodeSource
curl -fsSL https://rpm.nodesource.com/setup_20.x | bash -
dnf install -y nodejs git

# Install pm2 globally — process manager for Node.js
npm install -g pm2

# Create app user (don't run apps as root)
useradd -m -s /bin/bash appuser

# Clone the app (replace with your actual repo)
cd /home/appuser
git clone https://github.com/yourname/taskboard-api.git app
cd app
npm install --production

# Create .env from Secrets Manager (fetch at boot time)
# In production, your app should fetch secrets at runtime, not from .env
SECRET=$(aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --secret-id taskboard/prod \
  --query SecretString \
  --output text)
echo "$SECRET" | python3 -c "
import sys, json
data = json.load(sys.stdin)
for k, v in data.items():
    print(f'{k}={v}')
" > /home/appuser/app/.env

chown -R appuser:appuser /home/appuser/app

# Start with pm2 as appuser
su -c "cd /home/appuser/app && pm2 start src/server.js --name taskboard && pm2 save" appuser
pm2 startup systemd -u appuser --hp /home/appuser
systemctl enable pm2-appuser
Terminal — launch the EC2 instancebash
INSTANCE_ID=$(aws ec2 run-instances \
  --image-id ami-0c02fb55956c7d316 \
  --instance-type t2.micro \
  --key-name taskboard-key \
  --security-group-ids sg-xxxxxxxxx \
  --subnet-id subnet-xxxxxxxxx \
  --iam-instance-profile Name=TaskboardEC2Profile \
  --user-data file://scripts/ec2-userdata.sh \
  --block-device-mappings '[{"DeviceName":"/dev/xvda","Ebs":{"VolumeSize":20,"VolumeType":"gp3"}}]' \
  --tag-specifications 'ResourceType=instance,Tags=[{Key=Name,Value=taskboard-server}]' \
  --query 'Instances[0].InstanceId' --output text)

echo "Instance ID: $INSTANCE_ID"

# Wait until the instance is running
aws ec2 wait instance-running --instance-ids $INSTANCE_ID

# Get the public IP
PUBLIC_IP=$(aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --instance-ids $INSTANCE_ID \
  --query 'Reservations[0].Instances[0].PublicIpAddress' \
  --output text)
echo "Public IP: $PUBLIC_IP"
