Allocate an Elastic IP (static public IP)

EC2 public IPs change every time you stop and start the instance. An Elastic IP is a static public IP that stays yours until you release it — essential for DNS records and stable API endpoints.

Option A — AWS Console

Phase 1 of 2 — Allocate the Address

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#Addresses
AWS Console
1

Open Elastic IPs

In the EC2 left sidebar under Network & Security, click Elastic IPs. Click Allocate Elastic IP address.

EC2 › Network & Security › Elastic IPs › Allocate Elastic IP address
2

Allocate from Amazon's pool

Network Border Group
us-east-1
Public IPv4 address pool
● Amazon's pool of IPv4 addresses
Leave the default. The "Bring your own" option is for enterprises migrating existing IP ranges — not needed here.

Click Allocate.

✓
A new row appears in the Elastic IPs table with a public IPv4 address (e.g. 54.123.45.67) and Association ID: — (not yet associated). Note this address — it becomes your permanent server IP.

Phase 2 of 2 — Associate with the EC2 Instance

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#Addresses
AWS Console
1

Select the Elastic IP and open Actions

Tick the checkbox on the new Elastic IP row. Click Actions → Associate Elastic IP address.

Elastic IPs › [your IP] › Actions › Associate Elastic IP address
2

Associate with taskboard-server

Resource type
● Instance
Instance*
taskboard-server (i-xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx)
Type the instance ID or name to filter the dropdown.
Private IP address
10.0.1.x (auto-selected)
Leave as auto-selected. Only relevant if the instance has multiple network interfaces.
Reassociation
☐ Allow this Elastic IP to be reassociated
Leave unchecked for now. Tick it only if you plan to move this IP between instances later.

Click Associate.

✓
The Associated instance ID column now shows your instance. Go to EC2 → Instances → taskboard-server and confirm the Elastic IP address field in the details panel matches. Use this IP in your SSH command and DNS records going forward.
⚠️
Billing: Elastic IPs are free while associated with a running instance. AWS charges ~$0.005/hr if the IP is allocated but the instance is stopped or the IP is unattached. Release IPs you are not using.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminalbash
# Allocate an Elastic IP
ALLOC_ID=$(aws ec2 allocate-address --domain vpc --query 'AllocationId' --output text)

# Associate it with your running instance
aws ec2 associate-address \
  --instance-id $INSTANCE_ID \
  --allocation-id $ALLOC_ID

# Your app is now permanently at this IP
aws ec2 describe-addresses \
  --allocation-ids $ALLOC_ID \
  --query 'Addresses[0].PublicIp' --output text
⚠️ Elastic IP billing

Elastic IPs are free while associated with a running instance. AWS charges you if the IP is allocated but not associated (you're holding a public IP without using it). Always release EIPs you don't need.

EBS Storage — understanding your disk

Every EC2 instance has at least one EBS (Elastic Block Store) volume as its root disk. Think of it as a USB SSD that can be attached to instances.

Volume TypeUse CaseMax IOPSCost
gp3General purpose (use this by default)16,000$0.08/GB/month
gp2Old general purpose (legacy, prefer gp3)16,000$0.10/GB/month
io2High performance databases64,000$0.125/GB/month
st1Throughput-optimized (big data, logs)500$0.045/GB/month

The complete Express server

src/server.jsjavascript
import express from "express";
import dotenv from "dotenv";
import { initDb } from "./config/db.js";
import tasksRouter from "./routes/tasks.js";
import usersRouter from "./routes/users.js";
import attachmentsRouter from "./routes/attachments.js";
import { requestLogger } from "./middleware/logger.js";

dotenv.config();
const app = express();

app.use(express.json({ limit: "10mb" }));
app.use(express.urlencoded({ extended: true }));
app.use(requestLogger);  // logs to CloudWatch

// Health check — used by load balancers and monitoring
app.get("/health", (req, res) => {
  res.json({
    status: "ok",
    service: "taskboard-api",
    region: process.env.AWS_REGION,
    timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),
  });
});

app.use("/api/tasks", tasksRouter);
app.use("/api/users", usersRouter);
app.use("/api/attachments", attachmentsRouter);

// Global error handler
app.use((err, req, res, next) => {
  console.error(JSON.stringify({
    level: "ERROR",
    message: err.message,
    stack: err.stack,
    path: req.path,
    method: req.method,
  }));
  res.status(err.status || 500).json({ error: err.message || "Internal server error" });
});

async function start() {
  await initDb();  // run migrations on RDS
  const port = process.env.PORT || 3000;
  app.listen(port, () => console.log(`TaskBoard API running on port ${port}`));
}

start().catch(console.error);

Launch Templates

A Launch Template is a versioned, reusable EC2 configuration — the AMI, instance type, key pair, security groups, user data, and IAM profile all saved in one place. Auto Scaling Groups require a Launch Template (Launch Configurations are legacy and no longer recommended). You can also use them to launch single instances consistently without re-entering settings.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#LaunchTemplates
AWS Console
1

Create a Launch Template

In the EC2 sidebar click Launch Templates → Create launch template. Fill in the same settings you used when launching your EC2 instance manually — AMI, instance type, key pair, security group, IAM profile, and user data. Give it a version description like v1 — initial Node.js 20 setup.

EC2 › Launch Templates › Create launch template
Template name
taskboard-lt
Template version description
v1 — Node.js 20, gp3 20GB
AMI
Amazon Linux 2023
Instance type
t3.small
Key pair
taskboard-key
Security groups
taskboard-ec2-sg
IAM instance profile
TaskboardEC2Profile

Paste your user data script in Advanced details → User data. Click Create launch template. To update the configuration later, create a new version of the template — old versions are preserved and can be rolled back to.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create a Launch Templatebash
aws ec2 create-launch-template \
  --launch-template-name taskboard-lt \
  --version-description "v1 Node.js 20 gp3 20GB" \
  --launch-template-data '{
    "ImageId": "ami-0c02fb55956c7d316",
    "InstanceType": "t3.small",
    "KeyName": "taskboard-key",
    "SecurityGroupIds": ["sg-EC2_SG_ID"],
    "IamInstanceProfile": {"Name": "TaskboardEC2Profile"},
    "BlockDeviceMappings": [{
      "DeviceName": "/dev/xvda",
      "Ebs": {"VolumeSize": 20, "VolumeType": "gp3"}
    }],
    "UserData": "'"$(base64 -w0 scripts/ec2-userdata.sh)"'"
  }'

Application Load Balancer (ALB)

An ALB distributes incoming HTTP/HTTPS traffic across multiple EC2 instances using listener rules. It performs health checks and automatically stops sending traffic to unhealthy instances. It also terminates SSL so your Node.js app only handles plain HTTP internally. Required for any multi-instance setup and for Auto Scaling Groups.

TaskBoard with ALB + two EC2 instances
Internet
→ HTTPS :443 →
ALB
SSL termination
→ HTTP :3000 →
EC2 instance 1
EC2 instance 2

Option A — AWS Console

Phase 1 of 2 — Create a Target Group

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#TargetGroups
AWS Console
1

Create a target group for your EC2 instances

In the EC2 sidebar under Load Balancing, click Target Groups → Create target group.

EC2 › Load Balancing › Target Groups › Create target group
Target type
● Instances
Target group name
taskboard-tg
Protocol / Port
HTTP : 3000
This is the port your Node.js app listens on. The ALB forwards requests to instances on this port.
VPC
taskboard-vpc
Health check path
/health
The ALB calls GET /health every 30 seconds. Instances that return non-2xx responses are marked unhealthy and removed from rotation.

Click Next. Select your EC2 instance from the list, click Include as pending below, then Create target group.

Phase 2 of 2 — Create the Application Load Balancer

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#LoadBalancers
AWS Console
1

Create the ALB

Click Load Balancers → Create load balancer → Application Load Balancer → Create.

Name
taskboard-alb
Scheme
● Internet-facing
VPC
taskboard-vpc
Mappings (subnets)
☑ taskboard-public-1a   ☑ taskboard-public-1b
Select both public subnets. ALBs must span at least 2 AZs.
Security groups
taskboard-ec2-sg
Or create a dedicated ALB security group that allows inbound 80 and 443 from 0.0.0.0/0.

Under Listeners and routing, add a listener: Protocol HTTP, Port 80, Forward to taskboard-tg. Click Create load balancer.

✓
After ~2 minutes the ALB state changes to Active. Copy the DNS name (e.g. taskboard-alb-xxxx.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com). Use this URL instead of the EC2 instance IP — point your domain's CNAME at it via Route 53.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create target group and ALBbash
# Create target group
TG_ARN=$(aws elbv2 create-target-group \
  --name taskboard-tg \
  --protocol HTTP --port 3000 \
  --vpc-id $VPC_ID \
  --health-check-path /health \
  --health-check-interval-seconds 30 \
  --healthy-threshold-count 2 \
  --query 'TargetGroups[0].TargetGroupArn' --output text)

# Register your EC2 instance
aws elbv2 register-targets \
  --target-group-arn $TG_ARN \
  --targets Id=$INSTANCE_ID

# Create the ALB
ALB_ARN=$(aws elbv2 create-load-balancer \
  --name taskboard-alb \
  --type application \
  --subnets $PUB1 $PUB2 \
  --security-groups $EC2_SG \
  --query 'LoadBalancers[0].LoadBalancerArn' --output text)

# Add HTTP listener
aws elbv2 create-listener \
  --load-balancer-arn $ALB_ARN \
  --protocol HTTP --port 80 \
  --default-actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=$TG_ARN

# Get the ALB DNS name
aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers \
  --load-balancer-arns $ALB_ARN \
  --query 'LoadBalancers[0].DNSName' --output text

Auto Scaling Groups (ASG)

An Auto Scaling Group maintains a fleet of EC2 instances and automatically adds or removes them based on demand. When CPU goes above 70%, ASG launches another instance. When traffic drops, it terminates excess instances. Combined with an ALB and Launch Template, this is the complete production-grade EC2 compute pattern.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#AutoScalingGroups
AWS Console
1

Create an Auto Scaling Group

In EC2 sidebar click Auto Scaling → Auto Scaling Groups → Create Auto Scaling group.

EC2 › Auto Scaling › Auto Scaling Groups › Create Auto Scaling group
Auto Scaling group name
taskboard-asg
Launch template
taskboard-lt (version: Latest)

Click Next.

2

Configure networking and attach the ALB

VPC
taskboard-vpc
Availability Zones and subnets
☑ taskboard-public-1a   ☑ taskboard-public-1b
Load balancing
● Attach to an existing load balancer
Existing load balancer target groups
taskboard-tg
New instances launched by the ASG are automatically registered in the ALB target group and start receiving traffic once healthy.
Health check type
● ELB
Use ELB health checks — if the ALB marks an instance unhealthy (app not responding on /health), the ASG terminates and replaces it automatically.

Click Next.

3

Set capacity and scaling policy

Desired capacity
1
Minimum capacity
1
Maximum capacity
4
ASG will never run more than 4 instances. Set this to a number you're comfortable being billed for.
Scaling policies
● Target tracking scaling policy
Metric type
Average CPU utilization
Target value
60
ASG scales out when average CPU exceeds 60% and scales in when it drops below. CloudWatch alarms are created automatically.

Click Next → Next → Create Auto Scaling group.

✓
The ASG launches 1 instance immediately (desired capacity). Load test your app and watch the ASG add instances automatically in the Activity tab. Instances appear in the ALB target group within ~2 minutes of launch.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create Auto Scaling Groupbash
LT_ID=$(aws ec2 describe-launch-templates \
  --filters "Name=launch-template-name,Values=taskboard-lt" \
  --query 'LaunchTemplates[0].LaunchTemplateId' --output text)

aws autoscaling create-auto-scaling-group \
  --auto-scaling-group-name taskboard-asg \
  --launch-template LaunchTemplateId=$LT_ID,Version='$Latest' \
  --min-size 1 --max-size 4 --desired-capacity 1 \
  --vpc-zone-identifier "$PUB1,$PUB2" \
  --target-group-arns $TG_ARN \
  --health-check-type ELB \
  --health-check-grace-period 120

# Attach a target tracking scaling policy (scale at 60% CPU)
aws autoscaling put-scaling-policy \
  --auto-scaling-group-name taskboard-asg \
  --policy-name taskboard-cpu-scaling \
  --policy-type TargetTrackingScaling \
  --target-tracking-configuration '{
    "PredefinedMetricSpecification": {
      "PredefinedMetricType": "ASGAverageCPUUtilization"
    },
    "TargetValue": 60.0
  }'
Service 3 of 7
S3 — Simple Storage Service
S3 is AWS's infinitely scalable object storage. Objects (files) live in buckets. TaskBoard uses S3 to store task file attachments. You'll learn bucket creation, upload/download with Node.js, presigned URLs, lifecycle rules, and access control.
BucketsObjectsPresigned URLsMultipart UploadLifecycleVersioning

S3 fundamentals

  • Bucket — a globally unique container. Like a top-level directory. Name must be lowercase, 3-63 chars, DNS-compatible.
  • Object — a file stored in S3. Identified by a key (path-like string, e.g. tasks/42/report.pdf).
  • Object URL — https://BUCKET.s3.REGION.amazonaws.com/KEY. Public only if the bucket/object is public.
  • Durability — 11 nines (99.999999999%). S3 automatically replicates data across 3+ AZs.
  • Storage classes — Standard (frequent access), Standard-IA (infrequent), Glacier (archival, hours to retrieve), Glacier Instant (ms retrieval).

Create and configure a bucket

Creating a bucket takes about 2 minutes in the console. We then configure four additional settings on the bucket: public access block, versioning, encryption, and a lifecycle rule.

Option A — AWS Console

Phase 1 of 2 — Create the Bucket

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/bucket/create
AWS Console
1

Open S3 and click Create bucket

In the AWS Console top search bar type S3 and click the result. Click the orange Create bucket button.

AWS Console › S3 › Create bucket
General configuration
2

Choose a globally unique bucket name and region

AWS Region
US East (N. Virginia) us-east-1
Choose the same region as your EC2 instance. Data transfer between S3 and EC2 in the same region is free.
Bucket name*
taskboard-attachments-123456789012
Replace 123456789012 with your AWS Account ID (found in the top-right menu). Bucket names are global across all AWS accounts — appending your account ID guarantees uniqueness. Must be lowercase, 3–63 characters, no spaces or underscores.
Object Ownership
● ACLs disabled (recommended)
Leave the default. ACLs are a legacy access model. With ACLs disabled, access is controlled entirely through bucket policies and IAM — the modern approach.
Block Public Access settings
3

Keep all public access blocked

The four checkboxes below Block all public access should all be ticked by default. Leave them all on. Files will be shared via presigned URLs, not by making the bucket public.

Block all public access
On (all four sub-options ticked)
A public S3 bucket is one of the most common causes of data breaches on AWS. Keep this on unless you are hosting a public static website.
Bucket Versioning
4

Enable versioning

Versioning keeps every version of every object. If a user overwrites or deletes a file, you can restore the previous version.

Bucket Versioning
● Enable
Recommended for any bucket holding user-uploaded data. Note: versioned objects still count toward storage billing. Use lifecycle rules (Phase 2) to expire old versions.
Default encryption
5

Enable server-side encryption

Encryption type
● Server-side encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3)
SSE-S3 (AES-256) is free and automatic. Every object is encrypted at rest. Use SSE-KMS only if you need to control your own encryption keys — it adds cost and complexity.
Bucket Key
● Enable
Reduces the number of KMS API calls if you ever switch to SSE-KMS. Leave enabled.

Click Create bucket.

✓
Green banner: "Successfully created bucket taskboard-attachments-xxxx." The bucket appears in your S3 list. Now add the lifecycle rule in Phase 2.

Phase 2 of 2 — Add Lifecycle Rules

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/buckets/taskboard-attachments-xxxx?tab=lifecycle
AWS Console
1

Open the bucket's Management tab

Click your new bucket name in the S3 list to open it. Click the Management tab at the top, then scroll down to Lifecycle rules and click Create lifecycle rule.

S3 › taskboard-attachments-xxxx › Management › Create lifecycle rule
Rule 1 — Clean up incomplete multipart uploads
2

Create the abort-multipart rule

When large file uploads are interrupted, partial data sits in S3 and accrues storage charges invisibly. This rule cleans it up automatically.

Lifecycle rule name*
abort-incomplete-multipart
Rule scope
● Apply to all objects in the bucket
Lifecycle rule actions
☑ Delete expired object delete markers or incomplete multipart uploads
Delete incomplete multipart uploads
Enabled — delete after 7 days

Click Create rule. Then click Create lifecycle rule again to add the second rule.

Rule 2 — Move old attachments to cheaper storage
3

Create the transition-to-IA rule

Task attachments older than 30 days are rarely re-downloaded. Moving them to Standard-IA (Infrequent Access) cuts storage cost by ~60% with no change to your application code.

Lifecycle rule name*
move-attachments-to-ia
Rule scope — Limit the scope using filters
● Limit the scope to specific prefixes
Prefix
tasks/
Only objects whose key starts with tasks/ will be transitioned. This matches all task attachment uploads from the Node.js service.
Lifecycle rule actions
☑ Transition current versions of objects between storage classes
Storage class transitions
Standard-IA — after 30 days from creation
Objects must be at least 128 KB to benefit from Standard-IA pricing — smaller objects are cheaper to leave in Standard. S3 enforces a minimum 30-day transition period.

Click Create rule.

✓
Both lifecycle rules are now active. Your bucket is fully configured: private, versioned, encrypted at rest, with automatic cost optimisation and upload cleanup.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Performs all of the above — bucket creation, public access block, versioning, encryption, and both lifecycle rules — in a single script run.

Terminalbash
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
BUCKET="taskboard-attachments-${ACCOUNT_ID}"

# Create bucket
aws s3api create-bucket --bucket $BUCKET --region us-east-1

# Block all public access (use presigned URLs instead)
aws s3api put-public-access-block --bucket $BUCKET \
  --public-access-block-configuration \
  "BlockPublicAcls=true,IgnorePublicAcls=true,BlockPublicPolicy=true,RestrictPublicBuckets=true"

# Enable versioning (keeps all object versions — safe for important files)
aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket $BUCKET \
  --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled

# Enable server-side encryption (AES-256 — no extra cost)
aws s3api put-bucket-encryption --bucket $BUCKET \
  --server-side-encryption-configuration '{
    "Rules":[{"ApplyServerSideEncryptionByDefault":{"SSEAlgorithm":"AES256"}}]
  }'

# Lifecycle rule: delete incomplete multipart uploads after 7 days
# and transition task attachments to cheaper storage after 30 days
aws s3api put-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket $BUCKET \
  --lifecycle-configuration '{
    "Rules":[{
      "ID":"abort-multipart",
      "Status":"Enabled",
      "Filter":{"Prefix":""},
      "AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload":{"DaysAfterInitiation":7}
    },{
      "ID":"move-old-attachments-to-ia",
      "Status":"Enabled",
      "Filter":{"Prefix":"tasks/"},
      "Transitions":[{"Days":30,"StorageClass":"STANDARD_IA"}]
    }]
  }'

echo "Bucket ready: $BUCKET"

Complete S3 service module

src/services/s3Service.jsjavascript
import {
  S3Client,
  PutObjectCommand,
  GetObjectCommand,
  DeleteObjectCommand,
  HeadObjectCommand,
  ListObjectsV2Command,
  CreateMultipartUploadCommand,
  UploadPartCommand,
  CompleteMultipartUploadCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";
import { getSignedUrl } from "@aws-sdk/s3-request-presigner";
import { s3 } from "../config/aws.js";

const BUCKET = process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME;

/**
 * Upload a file buffer to S3.
 * Key structure: tasks/{taskId}/{uuid}-{filename}
 */
export async function uploadAttachment(taskId, fileBuffer, fileName, mimeType) {
  const key = `tasks/${taskId}/${Date.now()}-${fileName}`;

  await s3.send(new PutObjectCommand({
    Bucket: BUCKET,
    Key: key,
    Body: fileBuffer,
    ContentType: mimeType,
    Metadata: {
      taskId: String(taskId),
      originalName: fileName,
      uploadedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
    },
  }));

  return key;  // store this key in RDS alongside the task
}

/**
 * Generate a presigned GET URL. Expires in `expiresIn` seconds.
 * The URL is a temporary, signed link — anyone with it can download the file.
 */
export async function getPresignedDownloadUrl(key, expiresIn = 3600) {
  const command = new GetObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key });
  return getSignedUrl(s3, command, { expiresIn });
}

/**
 * Generate a presigned PUT URL for direct browser-to-S3 uploads.
 * Client uploads directly to S3 — your server never receives the file data.
 * This is the scalable pattern for large files.
 */
export async function getPresignedUploadUrl(key, mimeType, expiresIn = 900) {
  const command = new PutObjectCommand({
    Bucket: BUCKET,
    Key: key,
    ContentType: mimeType,
  });
  return getSignedUrl(s3, command, { expiresIn });
}

/**
 * Check if an object exists without downloading it.
 * HeadObject is cheap — just fetches metadata.
 */
export async function objectExists(key) {
  try {
    await s3.send(new HeadObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key }));
    return true;
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.name === "NotFound") return false;
    throw err;
  }
}

/**
 * List all attachments for a task.
 */
export async function listAttachments(taskId) {
  const { Contents = [] } = await s3.send(new ListObjectsV2Command({
    Bucket: BUCKET,
    Prefix: `tasks/${taskId}/`,
  }));

  return Contents.map(obj => ({
    key: obj.Key,
    size: obj.Size,
    lastModified: obj.LastModified,
    fileName: obj.Key.split("/").pop().replace(/^\d+-/, ""),  // strip timestamp prefix
  }));
}

/**
 * Delete an object. Used when a task or attachment is removed.
 */
export async function deleteAttachment(key) {
  await s3.send(new DeleteObjectCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key }));
}

Attachment routes with presigned upload

src/routes/attachments.jsjavascript
import express from "express";
import { v4 as uuidv4 } from "uuid";
import multer from "multer";
import { uploadAttachment, getPresignedDownloadUrl,
         getPresignedUploadUrl, listAttachments, deleteAttachment } from "../services/s3Service.js";
import pool from "../config/db.js";

const router = express.Router();
const upload = multer({ storage: multer.memoryStorage(), limits: { fileSize: 10 * 1024 * 1024 } }); // 10MB

// Pattern 1: Server-side upload (file passes through your server)
// Good for small files (<10MB), easy auth checking
router.post("/:taskId/upload", upload.single("file"), async (req, res) => {
  const { taskId } = req.params;
  const { buffer, originalname, mimetype } = req.file;

  const key = await uploadAttachment(taskId, buffer, originalname, mimetype);

  // Store key in DB so we can list/delete later
  await pool.query(
    "INSERT INTO attachments (task_id, s3_key, file_name, mime_type) VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4)",
    [taskId, key, originalname, mimetype]
  );

  res.json({ key, fileName: originalname });
});

// Pattern 2: Presigned upload URL (client uploads directly to S3)
// Better for large files — no server bottleneck, no memory pressure
router.post("/:taskId/presigned-upload", async (req, res) => {
  const { taskId } = req.params;
  const { fileName, mimeType } = req.body;

  const key = `tasks/${taskId}/${uuidv4()}-${fileName}`;
  const uploadUrl = await getPresignedUploadUrl(key, mimeType, 900); // 15 min

  // Return URL — client PUTs the file directly to this URL
  res.json({ uploadUrl, key });
  // After client finishes upload, they call /confirm-upload to register in DB
});

router.post("/:taskId/confirm-upload", async (req, res) => {
  const { taskId } = req.params;
  const { key, fileName, mimeType } = req.body;
  await pool.query(
    "INSERT INTO attachments (task_id, s3_key, file_name, mime_type) VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4)",
    [taskId, key, fileName, mimeType]
  );
  res.json({ success: true });
});

// List attachments for a task
router.get("/:taskId", async (req, res) => {
  const { rows } = await pool.query(
    "SELECT * FROM attachments WHERE task_id = $1 ORDER BY created_at DESC",
    [req.params.taskId]
  );
  const withUrls = await Promise.all(rows.map(async (row) => ({
    ...row,
    downloadUrl: await getPresignedDownloadUrl(row.s3_key, 3600),
  })));
  res.json(withUrls);
});

// Delete attachment
router.delete("/:taskId/:attachmentId", async (req, res) => {
  const { rows } = await pool.query(
    "DELETE FROM attachments WHERE id=$1 AND task_id=$2 RETURNING s3_key",
    [req.params.attachmentId, req.params.taskId]
  );
  if (!rows.length) return res.status(404).json({ error: "Not found" });
  await deleteAttachment(rows[0].s3_key);
  res.json({ deleted: true });
});

export default router;
Terminal — test the upload flowbash
# Upload via server
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/attachments/42/upload \
  -F "file=@/path/to/report.pdf"

# Get a presigned upload URL
curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/attachments/42/presigned-upload \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"fileName":"design.png","mimeType":"image/png"}'
# Returns: {"uploadUrl":"https://s3...","key":"tasks/42/uuid-design.png"}

# Upload directly to S3 using the presigned URL
UPLOAD_URL="https://taskboard-attachments-xxxx.s3.amazonaws.com/..."
curl -X PUT "$UPLOAD_URL" \
  -H "Content-Type: image/png" \
  --data-binary @/path/to/design.png

# List attachments with download links
curl http://localhost:3000/api/attachments/42

S3 Event Notifications

S3 can invoke a Lambda function, send an SQS message, or publish to an SNS topic whenever an object is created, deleted, or restored. This is the foundation of event-driven file processing pipelines — thumbnail generation, virus scanning, CSV parsing, and content indexing — with zero polling and zero extra infrastructure.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/buckets/taskboard-attachments-xxxx?tab=properties

Open the bucket, click the Properties tab. Scroll to Event notifications → Create event notification.

Event name
taskboard-attachment-uploaded
Prefix (optional)
tasks/
Only trigger for objects under the tasks/ prefix — avoids firing for unrelated objects in the same bucket.
Event types
☑ All object create events (s3:ObjectCreated:*)
Destination
● Lambda function
Lambda function
taskboard-file-processor
The Lambda must already exist. S3 automatically adds the resource-based permission that allows S3 to invoke it.

Click Save changes. Upload a test file — the Lambda is invoked within seconds.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — configure S3 event notification to Lambdabash
LAMBDA_ARN="arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:function:taskboard-file-processor"

# Grant S3 permission to invoke the Lambda
aws lambda add-permission \
  --function-name taskboard-file-processor \
  --statement-id s3-invoke \
  --action lambda:InvokeFunction \
  --principal s3.amazonaws.com \
  --source-arn arn:aws:s3:::$BUCKET \
  --source-account $ACCOUNT_ID

# Configure the notification on the bucket
aws s3api put-bucket-notification-configuration \
  --bucket $BUCKET \
  --notification-configuration '{
    "LambdaFunctionConfigurations": [{
      "Id": "taskboard-attachment-uploaded",
      "LambdaFunctionArn": "'"$LAMBDA_ARN"'",
      "Events": ["s3:ObjectCreated:*"],
      "Filter": {
        "Key": {"FilterRules": [{"Name":"prefix","Value":"tasks/"}]}
      }
    }]
  }'

Cross-Region Replication (CRR)

CRR automatically copies every new object written to your source bucket into a destination bucket in a different region. Use cases: disaster recovery (your data survives a regional outage), serving assets from a region closer to users, and compliance requirements that mandate data copies in specific geographies. Versioning must be enabled on both buckets.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/buckets/taskboard-attachments-xxxx?tab=management
AWS Console
1

Create a replication rule

Open your source bucket → Management → Replication rules → Create replication rule.

S3 › source-bucket › Management › Replication rules › Create replication rule
Replication rule name
replicate-to-eu-west-1
Status
● Enabled
Source
● Apply to all objects in the bucket
Destination
Browse S3 → taskboard-attachments-eu-xxxx (eu-west-1)
The destination bucket must already exist and have versioning enabled. It can be in another account.
IAM role
Create new role
AWS auto-creates a role with permission to read from the source and write to the destination.
Replication Time Control (RTC)
● Disabled (free) or Enabled ($0.015/GB for 15-min SLA)

Click Save. Objects uploaded from this point forward are replicated. Existing objects are not replicated unless you use S3 Batch Operations.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — configure Cross-Region Replicationbash
SOURCE_BUCKET="taskboard-attachments-$ACCOUNT_ID"
DEST_BUCKET="taskboard-attachments-eu-$ACCOUNT_ID"
DEST_REGION="eu-west-1"

# Create destination bucket with versioning
aws s3api create-bucket --bucket $DEST_BUCKET \
  --region $DEST_REGION \
  --create-bucket-configuration LocationConstraint=$DEST_REGION
aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket $DEST_BUCKET \
  --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled

# Create IAM role for replication
aws iam create-role --role-name s3-replication-role \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[{"Effect":"Allow","Principal":{"Service":"s3.amazonaws.com"},"Action":"sts:AssumeRole"}]
  }'
aws iam put-role-policy --role-name s3-replication-role \
  --policy-name replication-policy \
  --policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[
      {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["s3:GetReplicationConfiguration","s3:ListBucket"],"Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::'"$SOURCE_BUCKET"'"},
      {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["s3:GetObjectVersion","s3:GetObjectVersionAcl"],"Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::'"$SOURCE_BUCKET"'/*"},
      {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["s3:ReplicateObject","s3:ReplicateDelete"],"Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::'"$DEST_BUCKET"'/*"}
    ]
  }'

ROLE_ARN=$(aws iam get-role --role-name s3-replication-role --query 'Role.Arn' --output text)

aws s3api put-bucket-replication --bucket $SOURCE_BUCKET \
  --replication-configuration '{
    "Role": "'"$ROLE_ARN"'",
    "Rules": [{
      "ID": "replicate-to-eu-west-1",
      "Status": "Enabled",
      "Filter": {"Prefix": ""},
      "Destination": {"Bucket": "arn:aws:s3:::'"$DEST_BUCKET"'", "StorageClass": "STANDARD"}
    }]
  }'

S3 Object Lock (WORM)

Object Lock enforces a write-once-read-many (WORM) model. Once enabled, objects cannot be deleted or overwritten for a specified retention period. Required for financial records, healthcare data, and any compliance mandate (SEC 17a-4, HIPAA, FINRA). Object Lock must be enabled at bucket creation — it cannot be added later.

ModeWho can delete before retention expiresUse case
GovernanceUsers with s3:BypassGovernanceRetention permissionInternal compliance — protects from accidental deletion while allowing authorised overrides
ComplianceNobody — not even rootRegulatory compliance (SEC, FINRA) — immutable once set
⚠️ Enable at bucket creation only

Object Lock cannot be enabled on an existing bucket. Create a new bucket and check Enable Object Lock during creation. Versioning is automatically enabled when Object Lock is turned on.

Terminal — create a bucket with Object Lock and apply a default retentionbash
# Create bucket with Object Lock enabled
aws s3api create-bucket \
  --bucket taskboard-compliance-records \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --object-lock-enabled-for-bucket

# Apply a default 7-year Compliance retention to all new objects
aws s3api put-object-lock-configuration \
  --bucket taskboard-compliance-records \
  --object-lock-configuration '{
    "ObjectLockEnabled": "Enabled",
    "Rule": {
      "DefaultRetention": {
        "Mode": "COMPLIANCE",
        "Years": 7
      }
    }
  }'

# Objects uploaded to this bucket cannot be deleted for 7 years
aws s3 cp invoice-2024.pdf s3://taskboard-compliance-records/

Multipart Upload for Large Files

For files larger than 100 MB, use the S3 Multipart Upload API. It splits the file into parts (minimum 5 MB each), uploads them in parallel, and S3 assembles them server-side. Benefits: faster uploads on high-bandwidth connections, resume capability if a part fails, and it is the only way to upload files over 5 GB (the single PUT limit).

src/services/s3MultipartService.js — multipart upload helperjavascript
import {
  S3Client,
  CreateMultipartUploadCommand,
  UploadPartCommand,
  CompleteMultipartUploadCommand,
  AbortMultipartUploadCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";
import { createReadStream, statSync } from "fs";

const s3 = new S3Client({ region: process.env.AWS_REGION });
const BUCKET = process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME;
const PART_SIZE = 10 * 1024 * 1024; // 10 MB per part

export async function multipartUpload(key, filePath) {
  const fileSize = statSync(filePath).size;
  const totalParts = Math.ceil(fileSize / PART_SIZE);

  // 1. Initiate the upload — get an UploadId
  const { UploadId } = await s3.send(new CreateMultipartUploadCommand({
    Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key,
  }));

  console.log(`Starting multipart upload: ${key}, ${totalParts} parts, UploadId: ${UploadId}`);

  const completedParts = [];

  try {
    for (let partNumber = 1; partNumber <= totalParts; partNumber++) {
      const start = (partNumber - 1) * PART_SIZE;
      const end = Math.min(start + PART_SIZE, fileSize);

      const stream = createReadStream(filePath, { start, end: end - 1 });

      // 2. Upload each part — returns an ETag
      const { ETag } = await s3.send(new UploadPartCommand({
        Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key, UploadId,
        PartNumber: partNumber, Body: stream,
        ContentLength: end - start,
      }));

      completedParts.push({ PartNumber: partNumber, ETag });
      console.log(`Part ${partNumber}/${totalParts} uploaded`);
    }

    // 3. Complete — S3 assembles the parts into one object
    await s3.send(new CompleteMultipartUploadCommand({
      Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key, UploadId,
      MultipartUpload: { Parts: completedParts },
    }));

    console.log(`Multipart upload complete: ${key}`);
    return key;

  } catch (err) {
    // Always abort on failure — otherwise incomplete parts accrue storage charges
    await s3.send(new AbortMultipartUploadCommand({ Bucket: BUCKET, Key: key, UploadId }));
    throw err;
  }
}

S3 Transfer Acceleration

Transfer Acceleration routes upload traffic through CloudFront edge locations instead of directly to the S3 endpoint. Users in distant regions (e.g. uploading from Asia to a US bucket) see 50–500% faster upload speeds. You pay a small surcharge per GB accelerated. Only beneficial for files over 1 GB or users more than 500 miles from the bucket region.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/buckets/taskboard-attachments-xxxx?tab=properties
AWS Console
1

Enable Transfer Acceleration

Open the bucket → Properties tab → scroll to Transfer acceleration → Edit → Enabled → Save.

S3 › bucket › Properties › Transfer acceleration › Edit → Enabled

After enabling, use the accelerated endpoint in your SDK config:

Standard endpoint
taskboard-bucket.s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com
Accelerated endpoint
taskboard-bucket.s3-accelerate.amazonaws.com

  --bucket $BUCKET \
  --accelerate-configuration Status=Enabled

Node.js — use accelerated endpoint in the SDKjavascript
import { S3Client } from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";

// Enable transfer acceleration via useAccelerateEndpoint flag
const s3Accelerated = new S3Client({
  region: "us-east-1",
  useAccelerateEndpoint: true,   // routes through CloudFront edge locations
});

// Use s3Accelerated for large user uploads, s3 (standard) for internal operations

Static Website Hosting

S3 can serve a React, Vue, or Angular SPA directly from a bucket — no EC2, no server, no maintenance. Paired with CloudFront for HTTPS and global distribution, it is the cheapest and most scalable way to host a frontend. Typical cost: under $1/month for a low-traffic app, vs ~$8–15/month minimum for EC2.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/s3/buckets/taskboard-frontend?tab=properties
AWS Console
1

Create a public bucket for the frontend

Create a new S3 bucket named after your domain (e.g. app.taskboard.io). Disable Block all public access for this bucket — the frontend HTML/JS/CSS must be publicly readable.

⚠️
Only disable public access for the static website bucket. Never disable it for the attachments bucket which holds user files.
2

Enable static website hosting

Open the bucket → Properties → Static website hosting → Edit → Enable.

Index document
index.html
Error document
index.html
For SPAs, set the error document to index.html too. This handles client-side routing — S3 returns the app shell for all paths, and React Router handles the rest.

Click Save changes. Note the Bucket website endpoint URL — this is your HTTP endpoint (CloudFront will add HTTPS on top).

3

Add a bucket policy to allow public reads

Go to the Permissions tab → Bucket policy → Edit. Paste this policy:

Bucket policy — public readjson
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "PublicReadGetObject",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": "*",
    "Action": "s3:GetObject",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::app.taskboard.io/*"
  }]
}

Deploy your built frontend: npm run build && aws s3 sync dist/ s3://app.taskboard.io --delete

✓
Your SPA is live at the bucket website endpoint. For production, point a CloudFront distribution at this bucket origin — CloudFront adds HTTPS, custom domain, caching, and global edge delivery.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — set up and deploy a static websitebash
SITE_BUCKET="app.taskboard.io"

aws s3api create-bucket --bucket $SITE_BUCKET --region us-east-1

# Allow public access (required for static hosting)
aws s3api put-public-access-block --bucket $SITE_BUCKET \
  --public-access-block-configuration \
  "BlockPublicAcls=false,IgnorePublicAcls=false,BlockPublicPolicy=false,RestrictPublicBuckets=false"

# Enable static website hosting
aws s3api put-bucket-website --bucket $SITE_BUCKET \
  --website-configuration '{
    "IndexDocument": {"Suffix": "index.html"},
    "ErrorDocument": {"Key": "index.html"}
  }'

# Add public read policy
aws s3api put-bucket-policy --bucket $SITE_BUCKET --policy '{
  "Version":"2012-10-17",
  "Statement":[{"Effect":"Allow","Principal":"*","Action":"s3:GetObject","Resource":"arn:aws:s3:::'"$SITE_BUCKET"'/*"}]
}'

# Build and deploy (Vite/React)
npm run build
aws s3 sync dist/ s3://$SITE_BUCKET --delete

# Get the website endpoint
echo "http://$SITE_BUCKET.s3-website-us-east-1.amazonaws.com"

Bucket Policies vs ACLs

There are two access control systems for S3. Bucket policies are the modern, recommended approach — JSON IAM-style policies attached directly to the bucket that control access at the bucket and object level. ACLs (Access Control Lists) are a legacy mechanism predating IAM, with coarse-grained permissions and limited flexibility. AWS now recommends disabling ACLs entirely on all new buckets.

FeatureBucket Policy (use this)ACL (legacy — disable)
FormatJSON (same as IAM policies)Predefined grants (READ, WRITE, FULL_CONTROL)
ConditionsFull IAM condition support (IP, time, MFA, VPC)None
Cross-accountYes — specify principal by account ARNYes — but limited to predefined grants
Object-levelVia resource ARN wildcard (bucket/*)Per-object ACL on each upload (often forgotten)
Recommended✅ Yes❌ No — disable with Object Ownership = ACLs disabled
✅ Already done correctly in this tutorial

When you created the attachments bucket, you set Object Ownership = ACLs disabled and blocked all public access. This is the correct modern setup. Bucket policies control access when needed (e.g. the public read policy for the static website bucket above).

Service 4 of 7
RDS — Relational Database Service
RDS is AWS's managed relational database. It handles backups, automated patching, Multi-AZ failover, and read replicas. TaskBoard uses PostgreSQL on RDS for all structured data: users, tasks, projects, and attachments.
PostgreSQLConnection PoolingMigrationsMulti-AZRead ReplicasSnapshots

Key RDS concepts

FeatureWhat it doesFree tier?
Automated BackupsDaily snapshots + transaction logs. Allows point-in-time restore up to 35 days back.Yes (for db.t3.micro)
Multi-AZSynchronous standby in a second AZ. Auto-failover in ~60s if primary fails.No (costs 2x)
Read ReplicasAsynchronous copies for read-heavy workloads. Route SELECT queries to replicas.No
Parameter GroupsConfigure DB engine settings (e.g. max_connections, work_mem).Yes
SnapshotsManual point-in-time backups you can restore or share.Partial

Automatically increases storage when it fills up. Set the maximum to a value you are comfortable being billed for.

This is the subnet group you created in Phase 1. RDS will place the instance in one of the two private subnets.

Creates the subnet group, parameter group, and RDS instance in one script. Equivalent to all console steps above.

Terminalbash
# Create a DB subnet group (RDS needs subnets in 2+ AZs)
aws rds create-db-subnet-group \
  --db-subnet-group-name taskboard-subnet-group \
  --db-subnet-group-description "TaskBoard RDS subnet group" \
  --subnet-ids subnet-PRIV1 subnet-PRIV2

# Create a custom parameter group for tuning
aws rds create-db-parameter-group \
  --db-parameter-group-name taskboard-pg-params \
  --db-parameter-group-family postgres15 \
  --description "TaskBoard PostgreSQL 15 params"

# Tune connection limits (t3.micro has 1GB RAM — keep max_connections low)
aws rds modify-db-parameter-group \
  --db-parameter-group-name taskboard-pg-params \
  --parameters ParameterName=max_connections,ParameterValue=100,ApplyMethod=pending-reboot

# Launch the RDS instance
aws rds create-db-instance \
  --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db \
  --db-instance-class db.t3.micro \
  --engine postgres \
  --engine-version "15.4" \
  --master-username taskadmin \
  --master-user-password "$(openssl rand -base64 24)" \
  --allocated-storage 20 \
  --storage-type gp3 \
  --db-name taskboard \
  --vpc-security-group-ids sg-RDS_SG \
  --db-subnet-group-name taskboard-subnet-group \
  --db-parameter-group-name taskboard-pg-params \
  --backup-retention-period 7 \
  --no-multi-az \
  --no-publicly-accessible \
  --deletion-protection \
  --tags Key=Project,Value=taskboard

watch -n 30 "aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].DBInstanceStatus' --output text"

Database config and migrations with Knex.js

We use Knex.js as our query builder and migration runner. Knex gives you versioned migration files (so schema changes are tracked in git), a clean JavaScript API instead of raw SQL strings, and a connection pool built on top of pg. Install it first:

Terminal — install Knexbash
npm install knex pg
# knex is the query builder; pg is the PostgreSQL driver it uses underneath
src/config/db.js — Knex connectionjavascript
import Knex from "knex";
import { getSecret } from "../services/secretsService.js";

let db;

/**
 * Build the Knex connection config.
 * In production, credentials come from Secrets Manager.
 * In development, they come from .env variables.
 */
async function buildConfig() {
  let connection;

  if (process.env.NODE_ENV === "production") {
    const secret = await getSecret(process.env.SECRETS_ARN);
    connection = {
      host:     secret.host,
      port:     secret.port,
      database: secret.dbname,
      user:     secret.username,
      password: secret.password,
    };
  } else {
    connection = {
      host:     process.env.RDS_HOST,
      port:     Number(process.env.RDS_PORT),
      database: process.env.RDS_DATABASE,
      user:     process.env.RDS_USER,
      password: process.env.RDS_PASSWORD,
    };
  }

  return {
    client: "pg",
    connection: { ...connection, ssl: { rejectUnauthorized: false } },
    pool: {
      min: 2,
      max: 10,
      acquireTimeoutMillis: 5000,
      idleTimeoutMillis:   30000,
    },
    // Knex migration config — points at our migrations folder
    migrations: {
      directory: "./src/migrations",
      extension: "js",
    },
  };
}

/**
 * initDb — call once at server startup.
 * Creates the Knex instance and runs any pending migrations automatically.
 */
export async function initDb() {
  const config = await buildConfig();
  db = Knex(config);

  // Verify the connection before running migrations
  await db.raw("SELECT 1");
  console.log("Database connected:", process.env.RDS_DATABASE || process.env.NODE_ENV);

  // Run all pending migrations (safe to call on every deploy)
  const [batch, migrations] = await db.migrate.latest();
  if (migrations.length) {
    console.log(`Ran ${migrations.length} migration(s) in batch ${batch}:`, migrations);
  } else {
    console.log("No pending migrations.");
  }
}

// Export the Knex instance — import this in routes and services
export default () => db;

Migration files

Each migration is a numbered file. Knex tracks which have been run in a knex_migrations table it manages automatically. Run npx knex migrate:make <name> to create a new file.

src/migrations/20240101_01_initial_schema.jsjavascript
/**
 * Migration: initial schema
 * Creates users, tasks, and attachments tables with indexes.
 * The "up" function runs when migrating forward.
 * The "down" function rolls back — always implement it.
 */
export async function up(knex) {
  // Enable pgcrypto for gen_random_uuid() — needed for UUID primary keys
  await knex.raw('CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS "pgcrypto"');

  await knex.schema.createTable("users", (t) => {
    t.uuid("id").primary().defaultTo(knex.raw("gen_random_uuid()"));
    t.string("email").unique().notNullable();
    t.string("name").notNullable();
    t.timestamp("created_at", { useTz: true }).defaultTo(knex.fn.now());
  });

  await knex.schema.createTable("tasks", (t) => {
    t.increments("id").primary();
    t.string("title").notNullable();
    t.text("description");
    t.enum("status", ["todo", "in_progress", "done"]).defaultTo("todo").notNullable();
    t.enum("priority", ["low", "medium", "high"]).defaultTo("medium").notNullable();
    t.uuid("assignee_id").references("id").inTable("users").onDelete("SET NULL").nullable();
    t.date("due_date").nullable();
    t.timestamp("created_at", { useTz: true }).defaultTo(knex.fn.now());
    t.timestamp("updated_at", { useTz: true }).defaultTo(knex.fn.now());

    // Indexes for the most common query filters
    t.index("status");
    t.index("assignee_id");
  });

  await knex.schema.createTable("attachments", (t) => {
    t.increments("id").primary();
    t.integer("task_id").references("id").inTable("tasks").onDelete("CASCADE").notNullable();
    t.string("s3_key").notNullable();
    t.string("file_name").notNullable();
    t.string("mime_type");
    t.timestamp("created_at", { useTz: true }).defaultTo(knex.fn.now());

    t.index("task_id");
  });
}

export async function down(knex) {
  await knex.schema.dropTableIfExists("attachments");
  await knex.schema.dropTableIfExists("tasks");
  await knex.schema.dropTableIfExists("users");
}
src/migrations/20240115_02_add_task_tags.js — example of a later migrationjavascript
/**
 * Migration: add tags column to tasks
 * Demonstrates adding a column without touching the rest of the schema.
 */
export async function up(knex) {
  await knex.schema.alterTable("tasks", (t) => {
    t.specificType("tags", "text[]").defaultTo("{}").comment("Array of free-form tag strings");
  });
}

export async function down(knex) {
  await knex.schema.alterTable("tasks", (t) => {
    t.dropColumn("tags");
  });
}
Terminal — migration commandsbash
# Run all pending migrations
npx knex migrate:latest --knexfile src/config/db.js

# Roll back the most recent batch
npx knex migrate:rollback --knexfile src/config/db.js

# Create a new migration file
npx knex migrate:make add_task_comments --knexfile src/config/db.js

# See which migrations have run
npx knex migrate:list --knexfile src/config/db.js

Task CRUD routes with Knex

Knex's query builder replaces raw $1, $2 parameterised SQL with a readable, composable JavaScript API. The logic is identical — the syntax is far cleaner.

src/routes/tasks.jsjavascript
import express from "express";
import getDb from "../config/db.js";
import { notifyTaskComplete } from "../services/sqsService.js";

const router = express.Router();

// GET /api/tasks — list with filtering and pagination
router.get("/", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const db = getDb();
    const { status, assignee, page = 1, limit = 20 } = req.query;
    const offset = (page - 1) * limit;

    // Build the query incrementally — Knex only adds clauses when values are present
    const query = db("tasks as t")
      .select("t.*", "u.name as assignee_name")
      .leftJoin("users as u", "t.assignee_id", "u.id")
      .orderBy("t.created_at", "desc")
      .limit(limit)
      .offset(offset);

    if (status)   query.where("t.status", status);
    if (assignee) query.where("t.assignee_id", assignee);

    const [tasks, [{ count }]] = await Promise.all([
      query,
      db("tasks").count("id as count"),
    ]);

    res.json({ tasks, total: Number(count), page: +page, limit: +limit });
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// GET /api/tasks/:id
router.get("/:id", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const task = await getDb()("tasks").where({ id: req.params.id }).first();
    if (!task) return res.status(404).json({ error: "Task not found" });
    res.json(task);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// POST /api/tasks
router.post("/", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const { title, description, priority = "medium", assignee_id, due_date } = req.body;
    if (!title?.trim()) return res.status(400).json({ error: "title is required" });

    const [task] = await getDb()("tasks")
      .insert({ title, description, priority, assignee_id, due_date })
      .returning("*");

    res.status(201).json(task);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// PATCH /api/tasks/:id — partial update + notify on completion
router.patch("/:id", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const db = getDb();
    const { id } = req.params;
    const { title, status, priority, assignee_id, due_date } = req.body;

    const before = await db("tasks").where({ id }).first();
    if (!before) return res.status(404).json({ error: "Task not found" });

    // Build the update payload — omit undefined fields so we don't overwrite with null
    const patch = Object.fromEntries(
      Object.entries({ title, status, priority, assignee_id, due_date })
        .filter(([, v]) => v !== undefined)
    );
    patch.updated_at = db.fn.now();

    const [updated] = await db("tasks")
      .where({ id })
      .update(patch)
      .returning("*");

    // If task just moved to done, push async notification via SQS
    if (status === "done" && before.status !== "done") {
      await notifyTaskComplete(updated);
    }

    res.json(updated);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// DELETE /api/tasks/:id
router.delete("/:id", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const deleted = await getDb()("tasks").where({ id: req.params.id }).delete();
    if (!deleted) return res.status(404).json({ error: "Task not found" });
    res.status(204).send();
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

export default router;
⚠️ Connection pool sizing

PostgreSQL on a db.t3.micro supports about 87 max connections. With a pool of max: 10 per Node.js process, you can run up to 8 EC2 instances before hitting the limit. For production, use RDS Proxy — it multiplexes thousands of app connections into a handful of real DB connections.

RDS Proxy

RDS Proxy is a fully managed database connection pooler that sits between your application and RDS. It maintains a warm pool of database connections and multiplexes thousands of application connections (from Lambda functions, EC2 instances, ECS containers) into a much smaller set of real PostgreSQL connections. This is critical for Lambda — each Lambda invocation opens its own connection, so 1,000 concurrent Lambdas would otherwise open 1,000 DB connections simultaneously and exhaust RDS's limit instantly.

With RDS Proxy — connection multiplexing
1,000 Lambda invocations
→ 1,000 connections →
RDS Proxy
pools & multiplexes
→ ~20 connections →
RDS PostgreSQL

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/rds/home#proxies:

Click Create proxy. Takes ~5 minutes. Once available, copy the Proxy endpoint and use it in your Knex config instead of the RDS endpoint.

Terminal — create RDS Proxybash
SECRET_ARN=$(aws secretsmanager describe-secret \
  --secret-id taskboard/prod/rds \
  --query 'ARN' --output text)

aws rds create-db-proxy \
  --db-proxy-name taskboard-proxy \
  --engine-family POSTGRESQL \
  --auth '[{"AuthScheme":"SECRETS","SecretArn":"'"$SECRET_ARN"'","IAMAuth":"DISABLED"}]' \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:role/rds-proxy-role \
  --vpc-subnet-ids $PRIV1 $PRIV2 \
  --vpc-security-group-ids $RDS_SG \
  --no-require-tls

# Register the DB with the proxy
aws rds register-db-proxy-targets \
  --db-proxy-name taskboard-proxy \
  --db-instance-identifiers taskboard-db

# Get proxy endpoint (use this instead of RDS endpoint in your app)
aws rds describe-db-proxies \
  --db-proxy-name taskboard-proxy \
  --query 'DBProxies[0].Endpoint' --output text

Point-in-Time Restore

RDS automatically takes daily snapshots and retains transaction logs, allowing you to restore to any second within your backup retention window (up to 35 days). This is your safety net for accidental data deletion, corrupted migrations, or application bugs that wrote bad data.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/rds/home#database:id=taskboard-db
AWS Console
1

Initiate a point-in-time restore

In RDS → Databases, select taskboard-db. Click Actions → Restore to point in time.

RDS › taskboard-db › Actions › Restore to point in time
Restore time
● Custom date and time → 2024-03-15 13:45:00 UTC
Enter the timestamp just before the incident occurred. RDS creates a new instance at that exact state — your original instance is left untouched.
DB instance identifier
taskboard-db-restored
Instance class, storage, VPC
Same as source instance

Click Restore to point in time. Takes 10–15 minutes. Connect to the restored instance, verify the data, then update your app's RDS_HOST to point at it if a full cutover is needed.

💡
Know this procedure before you need it. Test it on a staging environment. The worst time to learn point-in-time restore is during a production incident at 2 AM.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — restore to a point in timebash
# Restore to 13:45:00 UTC on 2024-03-15
aws rds restore-db-instance-to-point-in-time \
  --source-db-instance-identifier taskboard-db \
  --target-db-instance-identifier taskboard-db-restored \
  --restore-time 2024-03-15T13:45:00Z \
  --db-instance-class db.t3.micro \
  --no-multi-az \
  --no-publicly-accessible

# Wait for the restored instance to become available
aws rds wait db-instance-available \
  --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db-restored

# Get the new endpoint
aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db-restored \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].Endpoint.Address' --output text

RDS Performance Insights

Performance Insights is a database monitoring tool that shows which SQL statements are consuming the most DB time. It plots a database load graph over time and lets you drill into individual queries, wait states, and users. The free tier provides 7 days of history on db.t3.micro.

Enable and use it

console.aws.amazon.com/rds/home#performance-insights-v20206:/resourceId/db-XXXX
AWS Console
1

Enable Performance Insights on the instance

In RDS → Databases, click taskboard-db → Modify. Under Additional configuration → Performance Insights, toggle Enable Performance Insights. Set retention to 7 days (free tier). Click Continue → Modify DB instance.

RDS › taskboard-db › Modify → Performance Insights → Enable
2

Read the dashboard during load

Click taskboard-db → Performance Insights in the sidebar. The Database load graph shows vCPUs used. Below it, the Top SQL table lists the queries consuming the most DB time. Click any query to see the full SQL text, execution count, and average latency. Use this to identify missing indexes or N+1 query patterns.

✅
Look for queries with high Avg latency (ms) and high Calls/sec. A query that runs 1,000 times/second at 50ms each is consuming 50 vCPU-seconds/second — a common sign of a missing index. Run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on that query in psql to confirm.

Database Migration with pg_dump

When you need to move data between PostgreSQL instances — upgrading a major version, migrating to a larger instance class, or copying production data to staging — pg_dump and pg_restore are the standard tools. They work with RDS just like any PostgreSQL server.

Terminal — export from source RDS and import to targetbash
SOURCE_HOST="taskboard-db.xxxx.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com"
TARGET_HOST="taskboard-db-new.xxxx.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com"
DB_NAME="taskboard"
DB_USER="taskadmin"

# Export from source (custom format — faster and supports parallel restore)
pg_dump \
  --host=$SOURCE_HOST \
  --port=5432 \
  --username=$DB_USER \
  --dbname=$DB_NAME \
  --format=custom \
  --compress=9 \
  --file=taskboard_backup.dump

# Import to target (parallel restore with 4 workers)
pg_restore \
  --host=$TARGET_HOST \
  --port=5432 \
  --username=$DB_USER \
  --dbname=$DB_NAME \
  --jobs=4 \
  --verbose \
  taskboard_backup.dump

# Verify row counts match
psql --host=$TARGET_HOST --username=$DB_USER --dbname=$DB_NAME \
  --command="SELECT schemaname, tablename, n_live_tup FROM pg_stat_user_tables ORDER BY n_live_tup DESC;"
💡 pg_dump for staging data sync

To copy production data to staging (with PII scrubbed), pipe through sed or use pg_dump with --exclude-table to skip sensitive tables, then run a scrubbing SQL script on the target before opening access to developers.

Knex Seeding

Knex seeds populate the database with sample data — essential for local development, staging environments, and automated tests. Seeds are separate from migrations: migrations define schema, seeds define data.

Terminal — create a seed filebash
npx knex seed:make 01_users --knexfile src/config/db.js
npx knex seed:make 02_tasks --knexfile src/config/db.js
src/seeds/01_users.jsjavascript
export async function seed(knex) {
  // Truncate in reverse FK order, then re-insert
  await knex("attachments").truncate();
  await knex("tasks").truncate();
  await knex("users").truncate();

  await knex("users").insert([
    { id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001", email: "alice@example.com", name: "Alice" },
    { id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002", email: "bob@example.com",   name: "Bob"   },
  ]);
}
src/seeds/02_tasks.jsjavascript
export async function seed(knex) {
  await knex("tasks").insert([
    { title: "Set up VPC",       status: "done",        priority: "high",   assignee_id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001" },
    { title: "Configure RDS",    status: "in_progress", priority: "high",   assignee_id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001" },
    { title: "Deploy to EC2",    status: "todo",        priority: "medium", assignee_id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002" },
    { title: "Set up CloudWatch",status: "todo",        priority: "low",    assignee_id: "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002" },
  ]);
}
Terminal — run seedsbash
# Run all seed files (in alphabetical order)
npx knex seed:run --knexfile src/config/db.js

# Seeds are idempotent — safe to run repeatedly (truncate + re-insert)
# Useful in package.json scripts:
# "db:seed": "knex seed:run --knexfile src/config/db.js"
# "db:reset": "knex migrate:rollback --all && knex migrate:latest && knex seed:run"
Service 5 of 7
Lambda — Serverless Functions
Lambda runs your code without any server to manage. You upload a function; AWS executes it in response to events (HTTP, SQS messages, S3 uploads, schedules). You pay only for actual execution time, billed in 1ms increments. First 1M invocations per month are always free.
Event-drivenCold startsLayersEnvironment varsConcurrencySQS trigger

Lambda execution model

Understanding the Lambda lifecycle helps you write faster, cheaper functions:

  1. Init phase (runs once per container): Lambda downloads your code, initializes the runtime, and runs code outside the handler. Keep this fast — it's amortized over many invocations.
  2. Invoke phase (runs every invocation): Your handler function runs.
  3. Shutdown: After inactivity, AWS freezes and eventually recycles the container.
💡 Cold start vs warm start

Cold start: A new container is spun up — takes 200-500ms extra for Node.js. Warm start: The container is reused — only your handler runs. Initialize DB connections and AWS clients outside the handler so they're reused on warm invocations.

The email notifier Lambda

lambda/emailNotifier/index.mjsjavascript
// Initialize clients OUTSIDE the handler — reused on warm starts
import { SESClient, SendEmailCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-ses";

const ses = new SESClient({ region: "us-east-1" });
const FROM_EMAIL = process.env.FROM_EMAIL;   // verified in SES

/**
 * Triggered by SQS. Receives up to 10 messages per invocation.
 * Lambda automatically retries failed messages (up to maxReceiveCount).
 */
export const handler = async (event) => {
  console.log(`Processing ${event.Records.length} SQS messages`);

  const results = await Promise.allSettled(
    event.Records.map(record => processMessage(record))
  );

  // Report any failures so Lambda knows which messages to retry
  const failures = results
    .map((r, i) => r.status === "rejected" ? { itemIdentifier: event.Records[i].messageId } : null)
    .filter(Boolean);

  if (failures.length > 0) {
    console.error("Failed messages:", JSON.stringify(failures));
    // Return partial batch failure — only retries the failed ones
    return { batchItemFailures: failures };
  }

  return { batchItemFailures: [] };
};

async function processMessage(record) {
  const task = JSON.parse(record.body);
  console.log("Sending completion notification for task:", task.id);

  // Build and send the email via Amazon SES
  await ses.send(new SendEmailCommand({
    Source: FROM_EMAIL,
    Destination: { ToAddresses: [task.assigneeEmail] },
    Message: {
      Subject: { Data: `✅ Task completed: ${task.title}` },
      Body: {
        Html: {
          Data: `
            <h2>Task Completed</h2>
            <p>Hi ${task.assigneeName},</p>
            <p>Task <strong>"${task.title}"</strong> has been marked as done.</p>
            <p>Completed at: ${new Date().toLocaleString()}</p>
          `
        }
      }
    }
  }));

  console.log(`Email sent for task ${task.id} to ${task.assigneeEmail}`);
}

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

scripts/deploy-lambda.shbash
#!/bin/bash
FUNCTION_NAME="taskboard-email-notifier"
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
ROLE_ARN="arn:aws:iam::${ACCOUNT_ID}:role/TaskboardLambdaRole"

# Create Lambda execution role (one-time setup)
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name TaskboardLambdaRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
    "Version":"2012-10-17",
    "Statement":[{
      "Effect":"Allow",
      "Principal":{"Service":"lambda.amazonaws.com"},
      "Action":"sts:AssumeRole"
    }]
  }' 2>/dev/null || true

aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name TaskboardLambdaRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/service-role/AWSLambdaBasicExecutionRole
aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name TaskboardLambdaRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonSESFullAccess
aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name TaskboardLambdaRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonSQSFullAccess

sleep 10  # IAM propagation delay

# Package
cd lambda/emailNotifier
zip -r ../../function.zip index.mjs
cd ../..

# Deploy or update
if aws lambda get-function --function-name $FUNCTION_NAME &>/dev/null; then
  aws lambda update-function-code \
    --function-name $FUNCTION_NAME \
    --zip-file fileb://function.zip
  aws lambda update-function-configuration \
    --function-name $FUNCTION_NAME \
    --memory-size 256 \
    --timeout 30 \
    --environment Variables="{FROM_EMAIL=noreply@yourdomain.com}"
else
  aws lambda create-function \
    --function-name $FUNCTION_NAME \
    --runtime nodejs20.x \
    --handler index.handler \
    --role $ROLE_ARN \
    --zip-file fileb://function.zip \
    --memory-size 256 \
    --timeout 30 \
    --environment Variables="{FROM_EMAIL=noreply@yourdomain.com}"
fi

# Connect to SQS
QUEUE_ARN=$(aws sqs get-queue-attributes \
  --queue-url $SQS_NOTIFICATIONS_URL \
  --attribute-names QueueArn \
  --query 'Attributes.QueueArn' --output text)

aws lambda create-event-source-mapping \
  --function-name $FUNCTION_NAME \
  --event-source-arn $QUEUE_ARN \
  --batch-size 10 \
  --function-response-types ReportBatchItemFailures \
  2>/dev/null || echo "Event source mapping already exists"

echo "Lambda deployed and connected to SQS"
rm function.zip

Lambda for scheduled tasks (cron)

💡 Why .mjs files in Lambda?

Lambda functions in this tutorial use .mjs files (ES Modules) instead of .js (CommonJS). This lets us use the standard import/export syntax consistently with the rest of the Node.js app. If you prefer CommonJS, rename the file to index.js and replace import with require() and export const handler with exports.handler. You also need to add "type": "module" to package.json when using .js files with ES module syntax.

lambda/dueDateChecker/index.mjsjavascript
// Triggered by EventBridge Schedule (cron) — runs daily at 8AM UTC
// Finds tasks due today and sends reminder emails

import Knex from "knex";
import { SESClient, SendEmailCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-ses";
import { SecretsManagerClient, GetSecretValueCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

const ses = new SESClient({ region: "us-east-1" });
const sm  = new SecretsManagerClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// Initialise Knex once outside the handler — reused on warm starts
let db;

async function getDb() {
  if (db) return db;

  const { SecretString } = await sm.send(new GetSecretValueCommand({
    SecretId: process.env.SECRETS_ARN,
  }));
  const creds = JSON.parse(SecretString);

  db = Knex({
    client: "pg",
    connection: {
      host: creds.host, port: creds.port,
      database: creds.dbname, user: creds.username, password: creds.password,
      ssl: { rejectUnauthorized: false },
    },
    pool: { min: 1, max: 2 }, // Lambda: keep pool small
  });

  return db;
}

export const handler = async (event) => {
  console.log("Due date checker running at", new Date().toISOString());
  const db = await getDb();

  const today = new Date().toISOString().split("T")[0];

  // Knex join query — same result as the raw SQL, much more readable
  const dueTasks = await db("tasks as t")
    .join("users as u", "t.assignee_id", "u.id")
    .select("t.*", "u.email", "u.name as assignee_name")
    .where("t.due_date", today)
    .whereNot("t.status", "done");

  console.log(`Found ${dueTasks.length} tasks due today`);

  for (const task of dueTasks) {
    await ses.send(new SendEmailCommand({
      Source: process.env.FROM_EMAIL,
      Destination: { ToAddresses: [task.email] },
      Message: {
        Subject: { Data: `⏰ Task due today: ${task.title}` },
        Body: { Text: { Data: `Hi ${task.assignee_name}, your task "${task.title}" is due today!` } },
      },
    }));
  }

  return { processedTasks: dueTasks.length };
};

# Run daily at 8:00 AM UTC

  --name taskboard-due-date-check \

  --state ENABLED

  --function-name taskboard-due-date-checker \
  --statement-id eventbridge-invoke \
  --action lambda:InvokeFunction \
  --principal events.amazonaws.com \
  --source-arn arn:aws:events:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:rule/taskboard-due-date-check

Lambda Layers

A Lambda Layer is a zip archive containing shared code, dependencies, or data that multiple Lambda functions can reference. Instead of bundling knex, pg, and the AWS SDK into every function's deployment package, you put them in a layer once and reference it. Functions stay small (~5 KB vs ~30 MB), deployments are faster, and updates to shared code happen in one place.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/lambda/home#/layers
AWS Console
1

Build the layer zip locally, then create it in the console

Lambda Layers must follow a specific directory structure. Build the zip locally first:

mkdir -p layer/nodejs && cd layer/nodejs
npm init -y
npm install knex pg @aws-sdk/client-ses @aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager
cd ../.. && zip -r layer.zip layer/

Then go to Lambda → Layers → Create layer.

Lambda › Layers › Create layer
Name
taskboard-dependencies
Upload a .zip file
● Upload a .zip file → layer.zip
Compatible runtimes
☑ Node.js 20.x

Click Create. Copy the Layer version ARN from the detail page.

2

Attach the layer to a function

Open your function (Lambda → taskboard-email-notifier). Scroll to the bottom of the Code tab to find the Layers panel. Click Add a layer.

Layer source
● Custom layers
Custom layers
taskboard-dependencies
Version
1 (latest)

Click Add. The layer is now available at /opt/nodejs/node_modules/ inside your function at runtime.

✓
Remove node_modules/ from your function zip — it no longer needs to be bundled. The deployment package drops from ~30 MB to a few KB, making deployments significantly faster.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create and use a dependencies layerbash
# Build the layer — dependencies must be in nodejs/node_modules/
mkdir -p layer/nodejs
cd layer/nodejs
npm init -y
npm install knex pg @aws-sdk/client-ses @aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager
cd ../..
zip -r layer.zip layer/

# Publish the layer
LAYER_ARN=$(aws lambda publish-layer-version \
  --layer-name taskboard-dependencies \
  --description "knex, pg, AWS SDK clients" \
  --compatible-runtimes nodejs20.x \
  --zip-file fileb://layer.zip \
  --query 'LayerVersionArn' --output text)

echo "Layer ARN: $LAYER_ARN"

# Attach the layer to a function (replaces bundled deps)
aws lambda update-function-configuration \
  --function-name taskboard-email-notifier \
  --layers $LAYER_ARN

# Your function zip now only needs index.mjs — no node_modules
# Layer contents are available at /opt/nodejs/node_modules/ at runtime
💡 In the console

Go to Lambda → Layers → Create layer. Upload the zip, select compatible runtimes (Node.js 20.x). To attach to a function: open the function → Code → Scroll to Layers → Add a layer → Custom layers → select taskboard-dependencies.

Provisioned Concurrency

Lambda cold starts add 200–500ms to the first invocation after a period of inactivity. For user-facing endpoints where latency matters, Provisioned Concurrency pre-warms a set number of Lambda containers so they are always ready to respond in under 10ms. You pay for the pre-warmed containers even when idle.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/lambda/home#/functions/taskboard-email-notifier
AWS Console
1

Publish a version and add Provisioned Concurrency

Provisioned Concurrency requires a published version or an alias — it cannot be set on $LATEST. Open the function → Actions → Publish new version. Then click the Configuration tab → Provisioned concurrency → Add configuration.

Version or alias
1 (published version)
Provisioned concurrency
5
Keep 5 containers warm. With 5 instances pre-warmed, the first 5 simultaneous requests have zero cold-start latency. Additional requests beyond 5 still cold-start. Cost: ~$0.016/GB-hour for pre-warmed containers.

Click Save. Status changes from In progress to Ready within 2–3 minutes.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — publish version and enable Provisioned Concurrencybash
# Publish a version (snapshot of current $LATEST)
VERSION=$(aws lambda publish-version \
  --function-name taskboard-email-notifier \
  --description "v1 — with provisioned concurrency" \
  --query 'Version' --output text)

echo "Published version: $VERSION"

# Enable Provisioned Concurrency on the version
aws lambda put-provisioned-concurrency-config \
  --function-name taskboard-email-notifier \
  --qualifier $VERSION \
  --provisioned-concurrent-executions 5

# Check status
aws lambda get-provisioned-concurrency-config \
  --function-name taskboard-email-notifier \
  --qualifier $VERSION \
  --query 'Status'

Lambda Function URLs

Function URLs give a Lambda function a dedicated HTTPS endpoint — no API Gateway needed. The URL is permanent (it doesn't change between deployments) and supports both public and IAM-authenticated access. Ideal for webhooks, simple APIs with a single function, and Slack/GitHub integrations.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/lambda/home#/functions/taskboard-webhook-handler

Add a Function URL

Open the function → Configuration → Function URL → Create function URL.

Auth type
● NONE (public URL)
NONE = publicly accessible via HTTPS without signing. Use this for webhook endpoints (Stripe, GitHub) where the caller provides its own auth token in the request body. AWS_IAM = requires Signature V4 signing.
Configure CORS
● Enable if your function serves browser requests

Click Save. Copy the Function URL — e.g. https://abcd1234.lambda-url.us-east-1.on.aws/. This endpoint is immediately live.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create a Function URLbash
aws lambda create-function-url-config \
  --function-name taskboard-webhook-handler \
  --auth-type NONE \
  --cors '{"AllowOrigins":["*"],"AllowMethods":["POST"],"AllowHeaders":["content-type"]}'

# Get the URL
aws lambda get-function-url-config \
  --function-name taskboard-webhook-handler \
  --query 'FunctionUrl' --output text

# Test it
curl -X POST https://abcd1234.lambda-url.us-east-1.on.aws/ \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"event":"ping"}'

S3 Event Triggers

Lambda can be invoked automatically when files are uploaded, deleted, or modified in S3. The event payload contains the bucket name, object key, size, and event type. This enables fully serverless file processing pipelines with zero polling.

lambda/fileProcessor/index.mjs — process S3 upload eventsjavascript
import { S3Client, GetObjectCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";

const s3 = new S3Client({ region: "us-east-1" });

export const handler = async (event) => {
  for (const record of event.Records) {
    const bucket = record.s3.bucket.name;
    const key    = decodeURIComponent(record.s3.object.key.replace(/\+/g, " "));
    const size   = record.s3.object.size;

    console.log(`New upload: s3://${bucket}/${key} (${size} bytes)`);

    // Example: only process PDF attachments
    if (!key.endsWith(".pdf")) continue;

    // Fetch the file for processing
    const { Body } = await s3.send(new GetObjectCommand({ Bucket: bucket, Key: key }));

    // Stream Body for processing (e.g. extract text, scan for viruses, generate preview)
    // await extractPdfText(Body, key);

    console.log(`Processed: ${key}`);
  }
};
💡 Connect in the console

Go to S3 → your bucket → Properties → Event notifications → Create event notification. Set the prefix/suffix filter, choose s3:ObjectCreated:*, and select your Lambda function as the destination. The full console walkthrough is in the S3 section above (S3 Event Notifications).

Container Image Deployments

Lambda supports Docker container images up to 10 GB in size — far beyond the 50 MB zipped deployment limit. Use container images when your function needs heavy ML libraries (PyTorch, TensorFlow), complex native binaries, or a large number of npm packages that push the zip size over the limit.

lambda/Dockerfile — Node.js Lambda as a containerbash
# Use the AWS-provided Lambda base image
FROM public.ecr.aws/lambda/nodejs:20

# Copy and install dependencies
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci --production

# Copy function code
COPY index.mjs ./

# Set the handler
CMD ["index.handler"]
Terminal — build, push to ECR, and deploy as Lambda containerbash
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
REGION="us-east-1"
REPO="taskboard-lambda"

# Create ECR repository
aws ecr create-repository --repository-name $REPO --region $REGION

# Authenticate Docker to ECR
aws ecr get-login-password --region $REGION | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin \
  $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com

# Build and push the image
docker build -t $REPO lambda/fileProcessor/
docker tag $REPO:latest $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:latest
docker push $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:latest

# Deploy as Lambda (first time)
aws lambda create-function \
  --function-name taskboard-file-processor \
  --package-type Image \
  --code ImageUri=$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:latest \
  --role arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT_ID:role/TaskboardLambdaRole \
  --memory-size 512 \
  --timeout 60

# Update after code changes — just push new image and update
docker push $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:latest
aws lambda update-function-code \
  --function-name taskboard-file-processor \
  --image-uri $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:latest
Service 6 of 7
DynamoDB — NoSQL Database
DynamoDB is a fully managed, serverless NoSQL database. It delivers single-digit millisecond performance at any scale, with no servers to provision or manage. TaskBoard uses DynamoDB for user sessions (fast token validation) and an activity log (append-heavy, no joins needed).
Partition KeySort KeyGSITTLStreamsTransactions

DynamoDB data model

DynamoDB is a key-value and document store. Unlike SQL, you design the schema around your access patterns, not around normalized tables. The most important design decision is choosing your keys:

Key TypeDescriptionExample
Partition Key (PK)Determines which DynamoDB partition your data goes in. Queries must include the PK. Choose high-cardinality keys for even distribution.sessionId, userId
Sort Key (SK)Optional second dimension. Enables range queries: between, begins_with, order by. Items with the same PK are grouped and sorted by SK.timestamp, taskId
GSI (Global Secondary Index)An additional index with a different PK/SK. Allows querying on non-key attributes.Query all activity by userId
LSI (Local Secondary Index)Same PK, different SK. Must be defined at table creation time.Sort items by a different field

Create tables

We need two tables: a sessions table (simple key-value, TTL-based expiry) and an activity-logs table (partition + sort key with a GSI for querying by task).

Option A — AWS Console

Table 1 of 2 — Sessions Table

console.aws.amazon.com/dynamodbv2/home#create-table

Name the table and define the partition key

taskboard-sessions
