sessionId String
The partition key is the primary lookup dimension. Every getSession() call in the app provides a sessionId — this makes it a perfect partition key for O(1) lookups.
Sort key
— (leave empty for sessions)
Sessions only need a single-key lookup. No sort key required.
Table settings
3

Use on-demand (PAY_PER_REQUEST) capacity

Table settings
● Customize settings
Capacity mode
● On-demand
On-demand scales automatically with no capacity to provision or monitor. It costs slightly more per request than provisioned, but is zero-waste and the right default for a new project. Switch to provisioned once you have stable, predictable traffic.

Leave all other settings as default. Click Create table.

✓

Enable TTL on the sessions table
4

Turn on Time to Live

Click the taskboard-sessions table name to open it. Go to the Additional settings tab. Under Time to live (TTL), click Enable.

taskboard-sessions › Additional settings › TTL: Enable
TTL attribute name*
ttl
This must exactly match the attribute name your Node.js code writes. The createSession() function sets ttl to a Unix timestamp 24 hours in the future — DynamoDB automatically deletes the item when that time passes.

Click Enable TTL.

💡
TTL deletion is eventually consistent — items may remain for up to 48 hours after their TTL expires. The getSession() function in the app checks the TTL value manually and treats expired sessions as invalid before DynamoDB actually deletes them.

Table 2 of 2 — Activity Logs Table (with GSI)

console.aws.amazon.com/dynamodbv2/home#create-table
AWS Console
1

Create the activity-logs table with a composite key

Click Create table again.

Table name*
taskboard-activity-logs
Partition key*
userId String
Groups all log entries for a user together in the same DynamoDB partition.
Sort key
createdAt String
An ISO 8601 timestamp string (e.g. 2024-03-15T10:23:45.123Z). String sort of ISO timestamps is chronological, enabling createdAt >= "2024-03-01" range queries.

Select Customize settings and set capacity mode to On-demand again.

Global Secondary Index (GSI)
2

Add a GSI so you can query all activity for a specific task

Scroll down to Global secondary indexes and click Create global index. The GSI lets you query "give me all log entries for task #42" — impossible on the main table without a full scan.

Partition key*
taskId Number
Sort key
createdAt String
Index name
taskId-index
Attribute projections
All
All copies every attribute into the GSI. This costs more storage but means you never need a second lookup to get the full item. Use Include if storage is a concern and you only need specific fields.

Click Create index, then click Create table.

✓
Both DynamoDB tables are ready. The taskboard-activity-logs GSI will show Active status within a few seconds of table creation.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminalbash
# Sessions table — simple key-value lookup by sessionId
aws dynamodb create-table \
  --table-name taskboard-sessions \
  --attribute-definitions AttributeName=sessionId,AttributeType=S \
  --key-schema AttributeName=sessionId,KeyType=HASH \
  --billing-mode PAY_PER_REQUEST

# Enable TTL — sessions auto-delete when the 'ttl' timestamp passes
aws dynamodb update-time-to-live \
  --table-name taskboard-sessions \
  --time-to-live-specification "Enabled=true,AttributeName=ttl"

# Activity log table — PK=userId, SK=timestamp (range queries over time)
# With a GSI on taskId to get all activity for a task
aws dynamodb create-table \
  --table-name taskboard-activity-logs \
  --attribute-definitions \
    AttributeName=userId,AttributeType=S \
    AttributeName=createdAt,AttributeType=S \
    AttributeName=taskId,AttributeType=N \
  --key-schema \
    AttributeName=userId,KeyType=HASH \
    AttributeName=createdAt,KeyType=RANGE \
  --global-secondary-indexes '[{
    "IndexName": "taskId-index",
    "KeySchema": [
      {"AttributeName":"taskId","KeyType":"HASH"},
      {"AttributeName":"createdAt","KeyType":"RANGE"}
    ],
    "Projection": {"ProjectionType":"ALL"}
  }]' \
  --billing-mode PAY_PER_REQUEST

Complete DynamoDB service module

src/services/dynamoService.jsjavascript
import {
  DynamoDBClient,
  PutItemCommand,
  GetItemCommand,
  DeleteItemCommand,
  QueryCommand,
  UpdateItemCommand,
  TransactWriteItemsCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-dynamodb";
import { marshall, unmarshall } from "@aws-sdk/util-dynamodb";
import { dynamo } from "../config/aws.js";

const SESSIONS_TABLE = process.env.DYNAMO_SESSIONS_TABLE;
const LOGS_TABLE     = process.env.DYNAMO_LOGS_TABLE;

// ─── SESSIONS ────────────────────────────────────────────────

export async function createSession(sessionId, userId, userData) {
  const ttl = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) + (24 * 60 * 60);  // 24 hours

  await dynamo.send(new PutItemCommand({
    TableName: SESSIONS_TABLE,
    Item: marshall({
      sessionId,
      userId,
      userData,           // store user info to avoid a DB roundtrip on every request
      createdAt: new Date().toISOString(),
      ttl,
    }),
    ConditionExpression: "attribute_not_exists(sessionId)",  // prevent overwrite
  }));

  return { sessionId, expiresAt: new Date(ttl * 1000).toISOString() };
}

export async function getSession(sessionId) {
  const { Item } = await dynamo.send(new GetItemCommand({
    TableName: SESSIONS_TABLE,
    Key: marshall({ sessionId }),
  }));

  if (!Item) return null;
  const session = unmarshall(Item);

  // Check TTL manually (DynamoDB TTL deletion can lag by up to 48h)
  if (session.ttl < Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000)) return null;

  return session;
}

export async function refreshSession(sessionId) {
  const newTtl = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000) + (24 * 60 * 60);

  await dynamo.send(new UpdateItemCommand({
    TableName: SESSIONS_TABLE,
    Key: marshall({ sessionId }),
    UpdateExpression: "SET #ttl = :ttl",
    ExpressionAttributeNames: { "#ttl": "ttl" },  // 'ttl' is reserved — use alias
    ExpressionAttributeValues: marshall({ ":ttl": newTtl }),
  }));
}

export async function deleteSession(sessionId) {
  await dynamo.send(new DeleteItemCommand({
    TableName: SESSIONS_TABLE,
    Key: marshall({ sessionId }),
  }));
}

// ─── ACTIVITY LOG ────────────────────────────────────────────

export async function logActivity(userId, taskId, action, details = {}) {
  await dynamo.send(new PutItemCommand({
    TableName: LOGS_TABLE,
    Item: marshall({
      userId,
      createdAt: new Date().toISOString(),  // sort key — enables time-range queries
      taskId,
      action,  // e.g. "TASK_CREATED", "STATUS_CHANGED", "ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED"
      details,
    }),
  }));
}

// Get all activity for a user in the last N days
export async function getUserActivity(userId, days = 7) {
  const since = new Date(Date.now() - days * 86400000).toISOString();

  const { Items } = await dynamo.send(new QueryCommand({
    TableName: LOGS_TABLE,
    KeyConditionExpression: "userId = :uid AND createdAt >= :since",
    ExpressionAttributeValues: marshall({ ":uid": userId, ":since": since }),
    ScanIndexForward: false,  // most recent first
    Limit: 100,
  }));

  return (Items || []).map(unmarshall);
}

// Get all activity for a task (uses GSI)
export async function getTaskActivity(taskId) {
  const { Items } = await dynamo.send(new QueryCommand({
    TableName: LOGS_TABLE,
    IndexName: "taskId-index",
    KeyConditionExpression: "taskId = :tid",
    ExpressionAttributeValues: marshall({ ":tid": taskId }),
    ScanIndexForward: false,
  }));

  return (Items || []).map(unmarshall);
}

Auth middleware using DynamoDB sessions

src/middleware/auth.jsjavascript
import { getSession, refreshSession } from "../services/dynamoService.js";

export async function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  const token = req.headers.authorization?.replace("Bearer ", "");
  if (!token) return res.status(401).json({ error: "No token provided" });

  const session = await getSession(token);
  if (!session) return res.status(401).json({ error: "Invalid or expired session" });

  // Attach user to request for downstream handlers
  req.user = session.userData;

  // Refresh session TTL on every request (sliding expiration)
  refreshSession(token).catch(console.error);  // fire-and-forget

  next();
}
✅ DynamoDB vs RDS — when to use each

Use DynamoDB when: you have simple, predictable access patterns; you need sub-millisecond latency; you expect massive scale; or data doesn't need complex SQL queries. Use RDS when: you need JOINs, complex transactions, ad-hoc queries, or full SQL capabilities.

DynamoDB Transactions

TransactWriteItems executes up to 100 write operations atomically across one or more tables — all succeed or all fail, with no partial state. This is the DynamoDB equivalent of a SQL BEGIN ... COMMIT transaction. Use it when you need consistency across multiple items: create a task AND write an activity log entry atomically.

Node.js — atomic task create + activity log using TransactWriteItemsjavascript
import {
  DynamoDBClient,
  TransactWriteItemsCommand,
  TransactGetItemsCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-dynamodb";
import { marshall } from "@aws-sdk/util-dynamodb";

const dynamo = new DynamoDBClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// Write a session AND a log entry atomically
// If either fails (e.g. session already exists), BOTH are rolled back
export async function createSessionWithLog(sessionId, userId, action) {
  await dynamo.send(new TransactWriteItemsCommand({
    TransactItems: [
      {
        // Condition: fail the whole transaction if sessionId already exists
        Put: {
          TableName: "taskboard-sessions",
          Item: marshall({ sessionId, userId, createdAt: new Date().toISOString(), ttl: Math.floor(Date.now()/1000)+86400 }),
          ConditionExpression: "attribute_not_exists(sessionId)",
        },
      },
      {
        Put: {
          TableName: "taskboard-activity-logs",
          Item: marshall({ userId, createdAt: new Date().toISOString(), action, taskId: 0 }),
        },
      },
    ],
  }));
}

// Read two items atomically (consistent point-in-time snapshot)
export async function getSessionAndUser(sessionId, userId) {
  const { Responses } = await dynamo.send(new TransactGetItemsCommand({
    TransactItems: [
      { Get: { TableName: "taskboard-sessions", Key: marshall({ sessionId }) } },
      { Get: { TableName: "taskboard-activity-logs", Key: marshall({ userId, createdAt: "2024-01-01" }) } },
    ],
  }));
  return Responses.map(r => r.Item ? unmarshall(r.Item) : null);
}

DynamoDB Streams

Streams capture a time-ordered log of every item-level change in a DynamoDB table — inserts, updates, and deletes. A Lambda function can be triggered automatically for each batch of changes. This enables real-time sync to OpenSearch, CDC (Change Data Capture) pipelines, cross-region replication, and audit trails.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/dynamodbv2/home#table?name=taskboard-activity-logs
AWS Console
1

Enable DynamoDB Streams on the table

Open the table → Exports and streams tab → DynamoDB stream details → Enable.

DynamoDB › taskboard-activity-logs › Exports and streams › Enable DynamoDB stream
View type
● New and old images
NEW_IMAGE: only the item after the change. OLD_IMAGE: only before. NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES: both — gives you a diff of exactly what changed. Best for auditing.

Click Enable. Then click Create trigger to connect a Lambda function to this stream.

2

Connect a Lambda trigger

Lambda function
taskboard-stream-processor
Batch size
100
Process up to 100 stream records per Lambda invocation for efficiency.
Starting position
Latest

Click Create trigger.

lambda/streamProcessor/index.mjs — handle DynamoDB stream eventsjavascript
import { unmarshall } from "@aws-sdk/util-dynamodb";

export const handler = async (event) => {
  for (const record of event.Records) {
    const { eventName, dynamodb } = record;

    if (eventName === "INSERT") {
      const newItem = unmarshall(dynamodb.NewImage);
      console.log("New activity logged:", newItem);
      // Sync to OpenSearch, send webhook, etc.
    }

    if (eventName === "MODIFY") {
      const before = unmarshall(dynamodb.OldImage);
      const after  = unmarshall(dynamodb.NewImage);
      console.log("Item changed from:", before, "to:", after);
    }

    if (eventName === "REMOVE") {
      const deleted = unmarshall(dynamodb.OldImage);
      console.log("Item deleted:", deleted);
    }
  }
};

Single-Table Design

Single-table design stores multiple entity types — users, tasks, sessions, activity logs — in one DynamoDB table using composite key patterns. This avoids multiple GetItem calls to assemble a single view, reduces costs (one table vs many), and takes advantage of DynamoDB's fast partition key lookups. It is an advanced pattern that requires deliberate key design.

PKSKEntity typeData
USER#alicePROFILEUser profileemail, name, createdAt
USER#aliceTASK#42Task membershiprole, joinedAt
TASK#42METADATATask detailstitle, status, priority
TASK#42LOG#2024-03-15T10:00:00ZActivity logaction, userId
SESSION#abc123DATASessionuserId, ttl
💡 Where to learn more

Single-table design has a steep learning curve. The definitive reference is "The DynamoDB Book" by Alex DeBrie (dynamodbbook.com). AWS's Rick Houlihan talks are also essential — search "DynamoDB Advanced Design Patterns re:Invent".

DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX)

DAX is a fully managed, in-memory cache for DynamoDB. It reduces read latency from single-digit milliseconds to microseconds by serving cached results directly — without hitting the underlying DynamoDB table. The DAX API is compatible with the DynamoDB API, so switching requires changing only the client initialization.

Node.js — swap DynamoDB client for DAX clientjavascript
// Without DAX (standard DynamoDB)
import { DynamoDBClient } from "@aws-sdk/client-dynamodb";
const dynamo = new DynamoDBClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// With DAX — install: npm install amazon-dax-client
import AmazonDaxClient from "amazon-dax-client";
const dax = new AmazonDaxClient({
  endpoints: ["taskboard-dax-cluster.xxxxx.dax-clusters.us-east-1.amazonaws.com"],
  region: "us-east-1",
});
// dax exposes the same GetItem, PutItem, Query API as DynamoDBClient
// Read hits are served from in-memory cache; writes go through to DynamoDB
💡 In the console

Create a DAX cluster at DynamoDB → DAX → Clusters → Create cluster. Select the node type (dax.r5.large is the smallest non-free option), VPC, and subnets. DAX must be in the same VPC as the EC2 or Lambda that connects to it.

Provisioned Capacity with Auto-Scaling

On-demand billing is convenient but more expensive per request at steady, predictable traffic. Provisioned capacity lets you commit to a specific number of Read/Write Capacity Units (RCUs/WCUs) per second at a lower price — with auto-scaling to handle spikes automatically.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/dynamodbv2/home#table?name=taskboard-sessions
AWS Console
1

Switch from On-demand to Provisioned with auto-scaling

Open the table → Additional settings tab → Read/write capacity → Edit.

Capacity mode
● Provisioned
Read capacity — Auto scaling
Enabled
Set minimum: 5 RCU, maximum: 100 RCU, target utilisation: 70%. DynamoDB scales between min and max automatically.
Write capacity — Auto scaling
Enabled — Min: 5 WCU, Max: 50 WCU, Target: 70%

Click Save changes. Auto-scaling adjusts capacity within 1–2 minutes based on consumed units.

Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR) and Backups

PITR continuously backs up your DynamoDB table and lets you restore to any second within the last 35 days. It is free of charge for the backup storage during the retention window. Enable it immediately on any table holding production data.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/dynamodbv2/home#table?name=taskboard-sessions&tab=backups
AWS Console
1

Enable PITR

Open the table → Backups tab → Enable under Point-in-time recovery. Click Enable PITR.

DynamoDB › table › Backups › Enable PITR

To restore: click Actions → Restore to point in time, select a target date/time, enter a new table name, and click Restore. The original table is untouched.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — enable PITR and restorebash
# Enable PITR on a table
aws dynamodb update-continuous-backups \
  --table-name taskboard-sessions \
  --point-in-time-recovery-specification PointInTimeRecoveryEnabled=true

# Restore to a specific time (creates a new table)
aws dynamodb restore-table-to-point-in-time \
  --source-table-name taskboard-sessions \
  --target-table-name taskboard-sessions-restored \
  --restore-date-time 2024-03-15T13:45:00Z \
  --use-latest-restorable-time  # or remove this to use --restore-date-time

# Check status
aws dynamodb describe-table \
  --table-name taskboard-sessions-restored \
  --query 'Table.TableStatus' --output text
Service 7 of 7
SQS — Simple Queue Service
SQS is a fully managed message queue. It decouples producers (your API) from consumers (Lambda workers). Messages are stored durably — if a consumer fails, the message stays in the queue and gets retried. TaskBoard uses SQS to hand off slow operations (email, report generation) from the API.
Standard QueueFIFO QueueDead Letter QueueVisibility TimeoutLong Polling

SQS queue types

Standard Queue

  • Unlimited throughput
  • At-least-once delivery — occasionally delivers a message more than once. Your consumer must be idempotent.
  • Best-effort ordering (mostly in order, not guaranteed)
  • Use for: notifications, log processing, anything where duplicates are tolerable

FIFO Queue

  • 300 transactions/sec (3,000 with batching)
  • Exactly-once processing — deduplication ID prevents duplicate delivery
  • Strict ordering within a message group
  • Use for: financial transactions, order processing, anything requiring strict sequencing

Dead Letter Queue (DLQ) — handling failures

A DLQ receives messages that couldn't be processed after N attempts. This prevents bad messages from blocking the main queue indefinitely. We create the DLQ first, then the main queue that references it.

Option A — AWS Console

Queue 1 of 2 — Create the Dead Letter Queue

The DLQ type must match the main queue's type. Since our main queue is Standard, the DLQ must also be Standard.
Name*

Naming convention: append -dlq to the main queue name. Makes it easy to identify in the SQS list and in CloudWatch alarms.

3

Set a longer retention period for the DLQ

Message retention period
14 days
DLQ messages are failed messages you need time to investigate and reprocess. Keep them longer than the main queue (which uses 1 day). 14 days gives you ample time to debug without paying much extra.

Leave all other settings as default. Click Create queue.

Type
● Standard
Name*
taskboard-notifications
Configuration
2

Set visibility timeout and enable long polling

Visibility timeout
60 seconds
When Lambda picks up a message, it hides it for 60 seconds while processing. If Lambda finishes and deletes it, great. If Lambda crashes, the message reappears after 60s for retry. Must be greater than your Lambda timeout (30s).
Message retention period
1 day
How long to keep unprocessed messages. 1 day is enough for notification emails — stale notifications older than 24h are not worth delivering.
Receive message wait time
20 seconds
Sets long polling. When nothing is in the queue, SQS waits up to 20 seconds before returning an empty response. This dramatically reduces the number of empty API calls and cuts cost by up to 20×.
Dead-letter queue

Dead-letter queue
Enabled
Choose queue
taskboard-notifications-dlq
Maximum receives*
3
After a message is received (and not deleted) 3 times, it moves to the DLQ. This means Lambda failed to process it 3 times — the message is likely malformed or the downstream service is broken.

Click Create queue.

✓
Main queue created with the DLQ attached. Copy the queue URL from the detail page — this is your SQS_NOTIFICATIONS_URL environment variable value for your Node.js app and Lambda.
💡 Set a CloudWatch alarm on the DLQ

Once both queues are created, go to the CloudWatch → Alarms section of this tutorial and create an alarm on ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible ≥ 1 for taskboard-notifications-dlq. This fires an email the moment any message fails 3 processing attempts — exactly when you need to investigate. The full alarm creation walkthrough is in the CloudWatch section (Part 4).

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create queue with DLQbash
# 1. Create the Dead Letter Queue first
DLQ_URL=$(aws sqs create-queue \
  --queue-name taskboard-notifications-dlq \
  --query 'QueueUrl' --output text)

DLQ_ARN=$(aws sqs get-queue-attributes \
  --queue-url $DLQ_URL \
  --attribute-names QueueArn \
  --query 'Attributes.QueueArn' --output text)

# 2. Create the main queue with redrive policy
# maxReceiveCount=3: after 3 failed processing attempts, move to DLQ
QUEUE_URL=$(aws sqs create-queue \
  --queue-name taskboard-notifications \
  --attributes '{
    "VisibilityTimeout": "60",
    "MessageRetentionPeriod": "86400",
    "ReceiveMessageWaitTimeSeconds": "20",
    "RedrivePolicy": "{\"deadLetterTargetArn\":\"'"${DLQ_ARN}"'\",\"maxReceiveCount\":\"3\"}"
  }' \
  --query 'QueueUrl' --output text)

echo "Main queue: $QUEUE_URL"
echo "DLQ: $DLQ_URL"

# Next step: create a CloudWatch alarm on the DLQ.
# See the CloudWatch section (Part 4 — Alarms) for the full alarm setup.
# The alarm to create watches ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible >= 1
# on taskboard-notifications-dlq and notifies the taskboard-alerts SNS topic.

Complete SQS service module

src/services/sqsService.jsjavascript
import {
  SQSClient,
  SendMessageCommand,
  SendMessageBatchCommand,
  ReceiveMessageCommand,
  DeleteMessageCommand,
  DeleteMessageBatchCommand,
  ChangeMessageVisibilityCommand,
  GetQueueAttributesCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-sqs";
import { sqs } from "../config/aws.js";

const QUEUE_URL = process.env.SQS_NOTIFICATIONS_URL;

/**
 * Send a single notification message.
 * Each message type has its own structure so Lambda can route them.
 */
export async function notifyTaskComplete(task) {
  const message = {
    type: "TASK_COMPLETED",
    taskId: task.id,
    title: task.title,
    assigneeEmail: task.assignee_email,
    assigneeName: task.assignee_name,
    completedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
  };

  const { MessageId } = await sqs.send(new SendMessageCommand({
    QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
    MessageBody: JSON.stringify(message),
    MessageAttributes: {
      "messageType": {
        DataType: "String",
        StringValue: "TASK_COMPLETED",
      }
    },
    // Add a 5s delay before Lambda receives it (optional)
    DelaySeconds: 5,
  }));

  console.log("Queued notification:", MessageId);
  return MessageId;
}

/**
 * Send multiple messages in a single API call (cheaper — 1 request vs N).
 * Max 10 messages per batch, total payload <256KB.
 */
export async function batchNotify(messages) {
  const entries = messages.map((msg, i) => ({
    Id: String(i),
    MessageBody: JSON.stringify(msg),
    MessageAttributes: {
      "messageType": { DataType: "String", StringValue: msg.type }
    }
  }));

  const { Failed } = await sqs.send(new SendMessageBatchCommand({
    QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
    Entries: entries,
  }));

  if (Failed?.length) console.error("Batch send failures:", Failed);
  return { sent: entries.length - (Failed?.length || 0), failed: Failed?.length || 0 };
}

/**
 * Manual polling — used in non-Lambda consumer scripts.
 * Lambda-triggered queues don't need this — Lambda polls automatically.
 */
export async function pollAndProcess(processor, options = {}) {
  const { batchSize = 10, waitSeconds = 20, maxIterations = Infinity } = options;
  let iterations = 0;

  while (iterations++ < maxIterations) {
    const { Messages } = await sqs.send(new ReceiveMessageCommand({
      QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
      MaxNumberOfMessages: batchSize,
      WaitTimeSeconds: waitSeconds,    // long polling — reduces empty responses & cost
      MessageAttributeNames: ["All"],
      AttributeNames: ["ApproximateReceiveCount"],
    }));

    if (!Messages?.length) continue;

    const toDelete = [];

    for (const msg of Messages) {
      try {
        await processor(JSON.parse(msg.Body), msg);
        toDelete.push({ Id: msg.MessageId, ReceiptHandle: msg.ReceiptHandle });
      } catch (err) {
        console.error("Processing failed:", err.message);
        // Extend visibility timeout so message returns to queue faster
        await sqs.send(new ChangeMessageVisibilityCommand({
          QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
          ReceiptHandle: msg.ReceiptHandle,
          VisibilityTimeout: 0,  // make it immediately visible to retry
        }));
      }
    }

    // Batch-delete all successfully processed messages
    if (toDelete.length) {
      await sqs.send(new DeleteMessageBatchCommand({
        QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
        Entries: toDelete,
      }));
    }
  }
}

// Monitor queue depth
export async function getQueueDepth() {
  const { Attributes } = await sqs.send(new GetQueueAttributesCommand({
    QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL,
    AttributeNames: ["ApproximateNumberOfMessages", "ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible"],
  }));
  return {
    visible: parseInt(Attributes.ApproximateNumberOfMessages),
    inFlight: parseInt(Attributes.ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible),
  };
}
✅ Visibility timeout explained

When Lambda receives a message, SQS hides it from other consumers for the visibility timeout duration (e.g. 60s). If Lambda finishes, it deletes the message. If Lambda crashes or times out, the message becomes visible again and gets retried. Set the timeout to at least 6x your Lambda timeout.

Message Deduplication (FIFO queues)

FIFO queues guarantee exactly-once processing using a MessageDeduplicationId. If you send two messages with the same deduplication ID within a 5-minute window, SQS silently discards the duplicate. This is critical for financial transactions, inventory updates, and any idempotency-sensitive operation.

Terminal — create a FIFO queue and send a deduplicated messagebash
# FIFO queues must end in .fifo
FIFO_URL=$(aws sqs create-queue \
  --queue-name taskboard-payments.fifo \
  --attributes '{
    "FifoQueue": "true",
    "ContentBasedDeduplication": "false",
    "VisibilityTimeout": "60"
  }' \
  --query 'QueueUrl' --output text)

# Send with explicit deduplication ID — sending twice has no effect
aws sqs send-message \
  --queue-url $FIFO_URL \
  --message-body '{"amount":99.99,"orderId":"order-123","action":"CHARGE"}' \
  --message-group-id "payments" \
  --message-deduplication-id "charge-order-123"  # duplicate within 5 min is silently dropped
Node.js — sending to a FIFO queue with deduplicationjavascript
import { SQSClient, SendMessageCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-sqs";
import { createHash } from "crypto";

const sqs = new SQSClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

export async function sendPayment(orderId, amount) {
  const body = JSON.stringify({ orderId, amount, action: "CHARGE" });

  // Deterministic deduplication ID — same order ID always produces the same ID
  const deduplicationId = createHash("sha256").update(`charge-${orderId}`).digest("hex");

  await sqs.send(new SendMessageCommand({
    QueueUrl: process.env.FIFO_QUEUE_URL,
    MessageBody: body,
    MessageGroupId: "payments",           // orders FIFO within this group
    MessageDeduplicationId: deduplicationId,
  }));
}

Message Filtering with SNS Fan-out

For complex event routing, place SNS above SQS. One publish to an SNS topic delivers to multiple SQS queues, each filtered by message attributes. A single TASK_COMPLETED event can simultaneously trigger an email notification queue, a reporting queue, and a webhook queue — without the publisher knowing any of them exist.

💡 Console note — SNS subscription filter policies

Creating an SNS topic and subscribing SQS queues can be done in the console (SNS → Topics → Create topic and Create subscription), but subscription filter policies cannot currently be set through the console UI — they must be added via the CLI or SDK after the subscription is created. For this reason, the CLI approach below is the recommended path for fan-out with filtering.

Terminal (CLI)

SNS fan-out to filtered SQS queues
Publisher (API)
sns.publish(event)
→
SNS Topic
taskboard-events
→ filter: TASK_COMPLETED →
SQS: email-queue
→ Lambda notifier
→ filter: ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED →
SQS: scan-queue
→ Lambda virus scanner
→ filter: all events →
SQS: audit-queue
→ Lambda audit logger
Terminal — create SNS topic, subscribe SQS queues with filtersbash
# Create the SNS topic
TOPIC_ARN=$(aws sns create-topic --name taskboard-events --query 'TopicArn' --output text)

EMAIL_QUEUE_ARN="arn:aws:sqs:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:taskboard-notifications"
SCAN_QUEUE_ARN="arn:aws:sqs:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:taskboard-scan"
AUDIT_QUEUE_ARN="arn:aws:sqs:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:taskboard-audit"

# Allow SNS to send to each SQS queue (add to each queue's policy)
# Then subscribe with attribute filters:

# Email queue — only TASK_COMPLETED events
aws sns subscribe \
  --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN \
  --protocol sqs \
  --notification-endpoint $EMAIL_QUEUE_ARN \
  --attributes '{"FilterPolicy":"{\"eventType\":[\"TASK_COMPLETED\"]}"}'

# Scan queue — only ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED events
aws sns subscribe \
  --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN \
  --protocol sqs \
  --notification-endpoint $SCAN_QUEUE_ARN \
  --attributes '{"FilterPolicy":"{\"eventType\":[\"ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED\"]}"}'

# Audit queue — all events (no filter)
aws sns subscribe \
  --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN --protocol sqs \
  --notification-endpoint $AUDIT_QUEUE_ARN
Node.js — publish to SNS with message attributes for filteringjavascript
import { SNSClient, PublishCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-sns";

const sns = new SNSClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

export async function publishEvent(eventType, payload) {
  await sns.send(new PublishCommand({
    TopicArn: process.env.SNS_TOPIC_ARN,
    Message: JSON.stringify(payload),
    MessageAttributes: {
      eventType: {
        DataType: "String",
        StringValue: eventType,  // e.g. "TASK_COMPLETED" or "ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED"
      },
    },
  }));
}

// Usage:
// await publishEvent("TASK_COMPLETED",   { taskId: 42, userId: "usr-1" });
// await publishEvent("ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED", { taskId: 42, key: "tasks/42/file.pdf" });

Large Message Payloads

SQS has a hard 256 KB message size limit. For larger payloads — file processing results, large JSON documents, base64-encoded content — use the Extended Client pattern: store the payload in S3 and put only the S3 reference in the SQS message. The consumer fetches from S3 to get the full payload.

Node.js — Extended Client pattern for payloads over 256 KBjavascript
import { SQSClient, SendMessageCommand, ReceiveMessageCommand, DeleteMessageCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-sqs";
import { S3Client, PutObjectCommand, GetObjectCommand, DeleteObjectCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";
import { v4 as uuid } from "uuid";

const sqs = new SQSClient({ region: "us-east-1" });
const s3  = new S3Client({ region: "us-east-1" });
const PAYLOAD_BUCKET = process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME;
const QUEUE_URL      = process.env.SQS_NOTIFICATIONS_URL;
const SIZE_LIMIT     = 200 * 1024;  // offload anything over 200 KB

// Producer — automatically offloads large payloads to S3
export async function sendLargeMessage(payload) {
  const body = JSON.stringify(payload);

  if (Buffer.byteLength(body) <= SIZE_LIMIT) {
    // Small enough — send directly
    await sqs.send(new SendMessageCommand({ QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL, MessageBody: body }));
    return;
  }

  // Too large — store in S3, send pointer
  const s3Key = `sqs-payloads/${uuid()}.json`;
  await s3.send(new PutObjectCommand({
    Bucket: PAYLOAD_BUCKET, Key: s3Key,
    Body: body, ContentType: "application/json",
  }));

  const pointer = JSON.stringify({ s3Offload: true, bucket: PAYLOAD_BUCKET, key: s3Key });
  await sqs.send(new SendMessageCommand({ QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL, MessageBody: pointer }));
}

// Consumer — transparently fetches from S3 when needed
export async function receiveLargeMessage() {
  const { Messages } = await sqs.send(new ReceiveMessageCommand({
    QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL, MaxNumberOfMessages: 1,
  }));
  if (!Messages?.length) return null;

  const msg = Messages[0];
  let parsed = JSON.parse(msg.Body);

  if (parsed.s3Offload) {
    // Fetch real payload from S3
    const { Body } = await s3.send(new GetObjectCommand({ Bucket: parsed.bucket, Key: parsed.key }));
    const text = await Body.transformToString();
    parsed = JSON.parse(text);
    // Clean up S3 after processing
    await s3.send(new DeleteObjectCommand({ Bucket: PAYLOAD_BUCKET, Key: msg.Body.key }));
  }

  await sqs.send(new DeleteMessageCommand({ QueueUrl: QUEUE_URL, ReceiptHandle: msg.ReceiptHandle }));
  return parsed;
}

Fetch secrets in Node.js

src/services/secretsService.jsjavascript
import { SecretsManagerClient, GetSecretValueCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

const sm = new SecretsManagerClient({ region: process.env.AWS_REGION });
const cache = new Map();  // in-memory cache to reduce API calls

export async function getSecret(secretId) {
  // Cache for 5 minutes — balances freshness vs. API call cost
  if (cache.has(secretId)) {
    const { value, fetchedAt } = cache.get(secretId);
    if (Date.now() - fetchedAt < 5 * 60 * 1000) return value;
  }

  const { SecretString, SecretBinary } = await sm.send(
    new GetSecretValueCommand({ SecretId: secretId })
  );

  const value = SecretString
    ? JSON.parse(SecretString)
    : JSON.parse(Buffer.from(SecretBinary, "base64").toString());

  cache.set(secretId, { value, fetchedAt: Date.now() });
  return value;
}
💡 Parameter Store vs Secrets Manager

Secrets Manager: Costs ~$0.40/secret/month. Has automatic rotation built in. Best for passwords and API keys. Parameter Store (SSM): Free for standard parameters. No automatic rotation. Best for config values, feature flags, and non-sensitive settings.

SSM Parameter Store — when to use it instead

For non-sensitive configuration that doesn't need rotation, Parameter Store is the right choice — it's free for standard parameters and integrates with the same AWS SDK. Use it for feature flags, environment names, API base URLs, and other config that changes between environments.

Use caseRecommended serviceReason
Database password, API secret keySecrets ManagerAutomatic rotation, audit trail, encryption at rest
Feature flag (ENABLE_DARK_MODE=true)Parameter Store (Standard)Free, no rotation needed, changes via CLI or console
API base URL, S3 bucket nameParameter Store (Standard)Free, not sensitive, easy to update without a code deploy
TLS certificates, SSH keysSecrets ManagerBinary secret support, rotation, cross-account access

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/systems-manager/parameters/create
AWS Console
1

Open Systems Manager → Parameter Store → Create parameter

Search for Systems Manager in the console. In the left sidebar click Parameter Store → Create parameter.

Systems Manager › Parameter Store › Create parameter
Name*
/taskboard/prod/FEATURE_DARK_MODE
The leading slash creates a hierarchical path. You can retrieve all parameters under /taskboard/prod/ in one API call using GetParametersByPath.
Tier
● Standard (free)
Type
● String
Use SecureString for sensitive values — it encrypts the value with your KMS key. Use String for non-sensitive config like feature flags and URLs.
Value*
true

Click Create parameter. Repeat for each config value. To update a value later: open the parameter and click Edit — no code deploy required.

💡
Your EC2 instance or Lambda must have the ssm:GetParameter and ssm:GetParametersByPath permissions in its IAM role to read parameters at runtime.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — store and retrieve config in Parameter Storebash
# Store a parameter (free — Standard tier)
aws ssm put-parameter \
  --name "/taskboard/prod/FEATURE_DARK_MODE" \
  --value "true" \
  --type String

# Store a secret (encrypted with KMS — still cheaper than Secrets Manager)
aws ssm put-parameter \
  --name "/taskboard/prod/THIRD_PARTY_API_KEY" \
  --value "sk-live-xxxxx" \
  --type SecureString  # encrypted with your default KMS key

# Read in your app
aws ssm get-parameter \
  --name "/taskboard/prod/FEATURE_DARK_MODE" \
  --query 'Parameter.Value' --output text

# Read decrypted SecureString
aws ssm get-parameter \
  --name "/taskboard/prod/THIRD_PARTY_API_KEY" \
  --with-decryption \
  --query 'Parameter.Value' --output text
Node.js — fetch config from Parameter Store at startupjavascript
import { SSMClient, GetParametersByPathCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-ssm";

const ssm = new SSMClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// Fetch all /taskboard/prod/ parameters at once
export async function loadConfig(path = "/taskboard/prod/") {
  const params = {};
  let nextToken;

  do {
    const { Parameters, NextToken } = await ssm.send(
      new GetParametersByPathCommand({
        Path: path, WithDecryption: true,
        NextToken: nextToken, MaxResults: 10,
      })
    );
    Parameters.forEach(p => {
      const key = p.Name.replace(path, "");  // strip prefix
      params[key] = p.Value;
    });
    nextToken = NextToken;
  } while (nextToken);

  return params;
}

// Usage at server startup:
// const config = await loadConfig();
// console.log(config.FEATURE_DARK_MODE); // "true"

Secret Versioning and Rotation Window

Secrets Manager keeps multiple versions of a secret simultaneously during rotation. When a rotation happens, the new credential is staged as AWSPENDING, tested, then promoted to AWSCURRENT. The old credential moves to AWSPREVIOUS and is retained for a brief overlap period. Applications must handle this correctly to avoid outages during rotation.

Node.js — correctly handle version labels during rotationjavascript
import { SecretsManagerClient, GetSecretValueCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

const sm = new SecretsManagerClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// Always fetch AWSCURRENT — the active credential
// Secrets Manager rotation automatically promotes AWSPENDING → AWSCURRENT
export async function getCurrentSecret(secretId) {
  const { SecretString } = await sm.send(new GetSecretValueCommand({
    SecretId: secretId,
    VersionStage: "AWSCURRENT",  // default — always use the live credential
  }));
  return JSON.parse(SecretString);
}

// In the rotation Lambda — stages to handle:
// AWSPENDING: new credential created, not yet validated
// AWSCURRENT: currently active credential
// AWSPREVIOUS: previous credential — kept briefly for in-flight connections
Terminal — check which versions exist for a secretbash
aws secretsmanager list-secret-version-ids \
  --secret-id taskboard/prod/rds \
  --query 'Versions[*].[VersionId,VersionStages]' \
  --output table

# During rotation you will see:
# AWSPENDING  — new password created, Lambda testing it
# AWSCURRENT  — password currently in use by app
# AWSPREVIOUS — old password, still valid briefly

Resource-Based Policies on Secrets

Secrets Manager supports resource-based policies directly on individual secrets — similar to S3 bucket policies. This lets you grant another AWS account or a specific IAM role access to a secret without modifying IAM policies in the consumer account. The canonical use case is sharing a database password from a central secrets account with multiple application accounts.

💡 Console — Secrets Manager → Resource permissions

You can set a resource-based policy in the console by opening the secret and clicking the Resource permissions tab → Edit permissions. Paste the JSON policy directly into the text editor. There is no visual policy builder for resource-based policies — write the JSON manually (example below) and paste it in, or use the CLI for automation and repeatability.

Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — grant cross-account access to a secretbash
# Allow the staging account (111111111111) to read this secret
aws secretsmanager put-resource-policy \
  --secret-id taskboard/prod/rds \
  --resource-policy '{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
      "Sid": "AllowStagingAccountRead",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {
        "AWS": "arn:aws:iam::111111111111:root"
      },
      "Action": [
        "secretsmanager:GetSecretValue",
        "secretsmanager:DescribeSecret"
      ],
      "Resource": "*"
    }]
  }'

# The staging account can now call:
# aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --secret-id arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:secret:taskboard/prod/rds
# No changes needed in the staging account's IAM policies

Rotation for Non-RDS Secrets

AWS provides a pre-built rotation Lambda for RDS credentials. For everything else — Stripe API keys, GitHub tokens, third-party service credentials — you write a custom rotation Lambda. AWS provides templates, but each one needs customisation for the specific secret type and the external service's key-rotation API.

lambda/rotateStripeKey/index.mjs — custom rotation Lambda structurejavascript
import { SecretsManagerClient, GetSecretValueCommand, PutSecretValueCommand, UpdateSecretVersionStageCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

const sm = new SecretsManagerClient({ region: "us-east-1" });

// Secrets Manager calls this Lambda with four distinct steps during rotation
export const handler = async (event) => {
  const { SecretId, ClientRequestToken, Step } = event;

  switch (Step) {
    case "createSecret":
      // Generate a new key using the external service's API
      // Store it as AWSPENDING
      await sm.send(new PutSecretValueCommand({
        SecretId, ClientRequestToken,
        SecretString: JSON.stringify({ apiKey: await generateNewStripeKey() }),
        VersionStages: ["AWSPENDING"],
      }));
      break;

    case "setSecret":
      // Apply the new AWSPENDING key to the service (activate it at Stripe)
      const { SecretString } = await sm.send(new GetSecretValueCommand({
        SecretId, VersionStage: "AWSPENDING",
      }));
      // await activateStripeKey(JSON.parse(SecretString).apiKey);
      break;

    case "testSecret":
      // Validate the AWSPENDING key works before promoting
      // await testStripeConnection(JSON.parse(SecretString).apiKey);
      break;

    case "finishSecret":
      // Promote AWSPENDING → AWSCURRENT
      await sm.send(new UpdateSecretVersionStageCommand({
        SecretId, VersionStage: "AWSCURRENT",
        MoveToVersionId: ClientRequestToken,
        RemoveFromVersionId: await getCurrentVersionId(SecretId),
      }));
      break;
  }
};

async function getCurrentVersionId(secretId) {
  const { VersionIdsToStages } = await sm.send(
    new GetSecretValueCommand({ SecretId: secretId })
  );
  return Object.entries(VersionIdsToStages)
    .find(([, stages]) => stages.includes("AWSCURRENT"))?.[0];
}

async function generateNewStripeKey() {
  // Call Stripe API to roll the key and return the new value
  // return await stripe.apiKeys.create({ type: "restricted" });
  return "sk-live-new-key-placeholder";
}
Advanced Topic 2
CloudWatch — Logging, Metrics & Monitoring
CloudWatch is AWS's full observability platform. It covers three pillars: Logs (what happened), Metrics (how the system is behaving numerically), and Alarms (automated responses when things go wrong). Every production AWS app must have all three configured from day one.
Logs Metrics Alarms Dashboards Logs Insights Metric Filters CloudWatch Agent Embedded Metrics

CloudWatch architecture overview

Understanding how the pieces fit together is critical before writing any code:

CloudWatch Data Flow — TaskBoard

→ PutLogEvents →
Log Group

→ contains →
Log Streams
per instance/day

→ auto →
Log Group
/aws/lambda/fn-name
Log Groups
→ Metric Filter →
Custom Metrics
→ threshold →

SNS → Email/Slack
All Metrics

Dashboard

Log Groups
→
Logs Insights
CloudWatch ConceptDefinitionAnalogy
Log GroupA named container for log streams. One per application or Lambda function. Retention policies apply at this level.A folder on disk
Log StreamA sequence of log events from a single source (one EC2 instance, one Lambda container). Streams are created automatically per instance.A single log file inside the folder
Log EventA single timestamped log entry. Max 256KB per event.One line in a log file
MetricA time-series numerical value (e.g. CPUUtilization = 43%). Grouped by namespace and identified by dimensions.A gauge reading
NamespaceA grouping for metrics. AWS services use AWS/EC2, AWS/Lambda etc. Your custom metrics go in your own namespace.A category
DimensionA name/value pair that uniquely identifies a metric (e.g. InstanceId=i-xxx). Up to 30 dimensions per metric.A label/tag
AlarmWatches a metric over time. Transitions between OK, ALARM, and INSUFFICIENT_DATA states. Triggers actions (SNS, Auto Scaling).A smoke detector
Metric FilterScans log events with a pattern and increments a custom metric counter. Bridge between logs and metrics.A grep | wc -l on a live log
Logs InsightsSQL-like query engine for log data. Can query across multiple log groups simultaneously.SQL for your logs
DashboardA visual canvas of widgets (metric graphs, alarms, log queries). Shareable URL, auto-refresh.Grafana, but AWS-native

Part 1 — CloudWatch Logs

How log retention works

By default, CloudWatch Logs retains data forever, which costs money. Always set a retention policy. Common choices:

Log typeRecommended retentionReason
Application debug/info logs7–14 daysHigh volume, short-lived utility
Error logs30–90 daysNeed history to spot regressions
Security / audit logs1–7 yearsCompliance (PCI, HIPAA, SOC2)
Lambda logs7 daysHigh volume, usually ephemeral

Installing the CloudWatch Agent on EC2

The CloudWatch Agent is a daemon that runs on EC2 and ships logs and system-level metrics (RAM, disk) to CloudWatch automatically — without any SDK calls in your app code. This is the recommended approach for EC2: install the agent, point it at your log files, and let it handle the rest.

EC2 shell — install and configure the CloudWatch Agentbash
# Install the agent (Amazon Linux 2023)
sudo dnf install -y amazon-cloudwatch-agent

# Create the agent config file
sudo tee /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/etc/amazon-cloudwatch-agent.json <<'EOF'
{
  "agent": {
    "metrics_collection_interval": 60,
    "run_as_user": "cwagent"
  },
  "logs": {
    "logs_collected": {
      "files": {
        "collect_list": [
          {
            "file_path": "/home/appuser/app/logs/app.log",
            "log_group_name": "/taskboard/api",
            "log_stream_name": "{instance_id}-app",
            "timestamp_format": "%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S",
            "multi_line_start_pattern": "^\\{",
            "retention_in_days": 14
          },
          {
            "file_path": "/var/log/userdata.log",
            "log_group_name": "/taskboard/ec2-init",
            "log_stream_name": "{instance_id}",
            "retention_in_days": 7
          }
        ]
      }
    }
  },
  "metrics": {
    "namespace": "TaskBoard/EC2",
    "metrics_collected": {
      "mem": {
        "measurement": ["mem_used_percent"],
        "metrics_collection_interval": 60
      },
      "disk": {
        "measurement": ["disk_used_percent"],
        "resources": ["/"],
        "metrics_collection_interval": 60
      },
      "cpu": {
        "measurement": ["cpu_usage_idle", "cpu_usage_user", "cpu_usage_system"],
        "metrics_collection_interval": 60
      }
    },
    "append_dimensions": {
      "InstanceId": "${aws:InstanceId}",
      "InstanceType": "${aws:InstanceType}"
    }
  }
}
EOF

# Start the agent and enable on boot
sudo /opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/bin/amazon-cloudwatch-agent-ctl \
  -a fetch-config \
  -m ec2 \
  -c file:/opt/aws/amazon-cloudwatch-agent/etc/amazon-cloudwatch-agent.json \
  -s

sudo systemctl enable amazon-cloudwatch-agent
sudo systemctl status amazon-cloudwatch-agent
💡 Agent vs SDK logging

The CloudWatch Agent tails log files on disk — your app writes to a file and the agent ships it. This is simpler and decoupled from your application code. SDK logging (PutLogEvents in code) gives you more control and works in environments without agents (like Lambda, which has its own built-in log shipping via stdout). Use the agent on EC2, rely on stdout for Lambda.

Structured JSON logging — the right way

CloudWatch Logs Insights can extract JSON fields automatically. Always log in JSON so you can filter, group, and aggregate log data by any field. Here is a complete, production-grade logger for your Node.js app:

src/utils/logger.js — structured loggerjavascript
import fs from "fs";
import path from "path";

// Log levels in order of severity
const LEVELS = { DEBUG: 0, INFO: 1, WARN: 2, ERROR: 3 };
const MIN_LEVEL = LEVELS[process.env.LOG_LEVEL?.toUpperCase() || "INFO"];

// On EC2: write to a file that the CloudWatch Agent tails.
// On Lambda: write to stdout — Lambda runtime ships stdout to CloudWatch automatically.
const IS_LAMBDA = !!process.env.LAMBDA_TASK_ROOT;
const logFile = IS_LAMBDA
  ? null
  : fs.createWriteStream(path.join(process.cwd(), "logs/app.log"), { flags: "a" });

function log(level, message, context = {}) {
  if (LEVELS[level] < MIN_LEVEL) return;

  const entry = {
    timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),
    level,
    message,
    service: "taskboard-api",
    environment: process.env.NODE_ENV,
    instanceId: process.env.EC2_INSTANCE_ID || "local",
    ...context,  // spread caller-provided context (requestId, userId, etc.)
  };

  const line = JSON.stringify(entry) + "\n";

  if (logFile) {
    logFile.write(line);  // write to file for CloudWatch Agent to pick up
  } else {
    process.stdout.write(line);  // Lambda: stdout is auto-shipped to CloudWatch
  }
}

export const logger = {
  debug: (msg, ctx) => log("DEBUG", msg, ctx),
  info:  (msg, ctx) => log("INFO",  msg, ctx),
  warn:  (msg, ctx) => log("WARN",  msg, ctx),
  error: (msg, ctx) => log("ERROR", msg, ctx),

  // Convenience: log an error with stack trace
  exception: (err, ctx = {}) => log("ERROR", err.message, {
    ...ctx,
    errorName: err.name,
    stack: err.stack,
  }),
};
src/middleware/requestLogger.js — HTTP request tracingjavascript
import { randomUUID } from "crypto";
import { logger } from "../utils/logger.js";

/**
 * Attaches a unique requestId to every request.
 * Logs when the request arrives and when it completes, with full context.
 * The requestId lets you correlate all log lines for a single request
 * across your app, Lambda functions, and databases.
 */
export function requestLogger(req, res, next) {
  const requestId = randomUUID();
  const startAt   = process.hrtime.bigint();

  // Make requestId available to all downstream handlers
  req.requestId = requestId;
  res.setHeader("X-Request-Id", requestId);

  // Log the incoming request
  logger.info("request_received", {
    requestId,
    method: req.method,
    path:   req.path,
    query:  req.query,
    ip:     req.ip,
    userAgent: req.get("User-Agent"),
  });

  // Log when the response is sent
  res.on("finish", () => {
    const durationMs = Number(process.hrtime.bigint() - startAt) / 1_000_000;

    const level =
      res.statusCode >= 500 ? "ERROR" :
      res.statusCode >= 400 ? "WARN"  : "INFO";

    logger[level.toLowerCase()]("request_completed", {
      requestId,
      method:     req.method,
      path:       req.path,
      statusCode: res.statusCode,
      durationMs: Math.round(durationMs * 100) / 100,
      userId:     req.user?.id || null,
    });
  });

  next();
}

// Use it in route handlers like this:
// logger.info("task_created", { requestId: req.requestId, taskId: task.id });
Usage example in a route handlerjavascript
router.post("/", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const { title, priority } = req.body;

    logger.info("task_create_attempt", {
      requestId: req.requestId,
      userId: req.user.id,
      title, priority,
    });

    const task = await createTask({ title, priority, userId: req.user.id });

    logger.info("task_created", {
      requestId: req.requestId,
      userId: req.user.id,
      taskId: task.id,
    });

    res.status(201).json(task);
  } catch (err) {
    logger.exception(err, { requestId: req.requestId, path: req.path });
    next(err);
  }
});

The resulting log output looks like this — every line is valid JSON that Logs Insights can parse:

Sample structured log output (pretty-printed for clarity)json
{ "timestamp":"2024-03-15T10:23:45.123Z", "level":"INFO",  "message":"request_received",  "requestId":"a1b2c3d4", "method":"POST", "path":"/api/tasks", "ip":"41.80.1.5", "service":"taskboard-api" }
{ "timestamp":"2024-03-15T10:23:45.145Z", "level":"INFO",  "message":"task_create_attempt","requestId":"a1b2c3d4", "userId":"usr-99", "title":"Fix login bug" }

Sending logs directly from SDK (for non-agent environments)

When the CloudWatch Agent isn't available — e.g. when running locally for development or in a container without an agent — you can push logs directly using the SDK. The key technique is batching: never send one log event per API call; buffer them and flush every 1–2 seconds.

src/utils/cwTransport.js — SDK-based log transportjavascript
import {
  CloudWatchLogsClient,
  CreateLogGroupCommand,
  CreateLogStreamCommand,
  PutLogEventsCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-cloudwatch-logs";
import os from "os";

const cwl = new CloudWatchLogsClient({ region: process.env.AWS_REGION || "us-east-1" });

const LOG_GROUP  = "/taskboard/api";
const LOG_STREAM = `${new Date().toISOString().split("T")[0]}/${os.hostname()}-${process.pid}`;

const MAX_BATCH_SIZE  = 10_000;   // CloudWatch limit: 10,000 events per PutLogEvents
const MAX_BATCH_BYTES = 1_048_576; // 1MB limit per batch
const FLUSH_INTERVAL  = 2_000;    // flush every 2 seconds

let buffer         = [];
let bufferBytes    = 0;
let flushTimer     = null;
let initialized    = false;

async function ensureLogStream() {
  if (initialized) return;
  try { await cwl.send(new CreateLogGroupCommand({ logGroupName: LOG_GROUP })); }
  catch (e) { if (e.name !== "ResourceAlreadyExistsException") throw e; }

  try { await cwl.send(new CreateLogStreamCommand({ logGroupName: LOG_GROUP, logStreamName: LOG_STREAM })); }
  catch (e) { if (e.name !== "ResourceAlreadyExistsException") throw e; }

  initialized = true;
}

export function enqueue(message) {
  const event = { timestamp: Date.now(), message };
  const eventBytes = Buffer.byteLength(message, "utf8") + 26; // 26 bytes overhead per event

  buffer.push(event);
  bufferBytes += eventBytes;

  // Flush immediately if we've hit the size limits
  if (buffer.length >= MAX_BATCH_SIZE || bufferBytes >= MAX_BATCH_BYTES) {
    flush();
    return;
  }

  // Schedule a timed flush if not already pending
  if (!flushTimer) {
    flushTimer = setTimeout(flush, FLUSH_INTERVAL);
  }
}

export async function flush() {
  clearTimeout(flushTimer);
  flushTimer = null;

  if (!buffer.length) return;

  const batch = buffer.splice(0, buffer.length);
  bufferBytes = 0;

  try {
    await ensureLogStream();
    await cwl.send(new PutLogEventsCommand({
      logGroupName:  LOG_GROUP,
      logStreamName: LOG_STREAM,
      logEvents:     batch,
    }));
  } catch (err) {
    // Don't crash the app if logging fails — put events back
    console.error("[CW Transport] PutLogEvents failed:", err.message);
    buffer = [...batch, ...buffer];
  }
}

// Graceful shutdown: flush before the process exits
async function onShutdown(signal) {
  console.log(`[CW Transport] ${signal} received — flushing logs...`);
  await flush();
  process.exit(0);
}
process.on("SIGTERM", () => onShutdown("SIGTERM"));
process.on("SIGINT",  () => onShutdown("SIGINT"));

Part 2 — CloudWatch Metrics

Built-in AWS metrics — what you get for free

AWS automatically publishes metrics for managed services. You don't write any code — they just appear in CloudWatch:

ServiceNamespaceKey Metrics
EC2AWS/EC2CPUUtilization, NetworkIn, NetworkOut, DiskReadOps, StatusCheckFailed. ⚠️ RAM and disk are NOT included — use CloudWatch Agent.
RDSAWS/RDSCPUUtilization, DatabaseConnections, FreeStorageSpace, ReadLatency, WriteLatency, FreeableMemory
LambdaAWS/LambdaInvocations, Errors, Duration, Throttles, ConcurrentExecutions, IteratorAge (SQS lag)
SQSAWS/SQSApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible, ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage, NumberOfMessagesSent, NumberOfMessagesDeleted
S3AWS/S3BucketSizeBytes, NumberOfObjects, AllRequests, GetRequests, 4xxErrors, 5xxErrors
DynamoDBAWS/DynamoDBConsumedReadCapacityUnits, ConsumedWriteCapacityUnits, SuccessfulRequestLatency, SystemErrors

Custom metrics — publishing your own

Beyond AWS-managed metrics, publish your own business-level metrics: tasks created per minute, active users, payment errors, SLA breaches, etc. These are what matter most for your application.

src/utils/metrics.js — custom metric publisherjavascript
import {
  CloudWatchClient,
  PutMetricDataCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-cloudwatch";

const cw = new CloudWatchClient({ region: process.env.AWS_REGION || "us-east-1" });
const NAMESPACE = "TaskBoard/Application";

/**
 * Publish one or more metrics in a single API call.
 * Batch up to 1000 data points per PutMetricData call (max 20 per call recommended).
 *
 * @param {Array} data - array of { name, value, unit, dimensions }
 */
export async function publishMetrics(data) {
  const MetricData = data.map(({ name, value, unit = "Count", dimensions = {} }) => ({
    MetricName: name,
    Value: value,
    Unit: unit,
    Timestamp: new Date(),
    Dimensions: Object.entries(dimensions).map(([Name, Value]) => ({ Name, Value })),
  }));

  await cw.send(new PutMetricDataCommand({ Namespace: NAMESPACE, MetricData }));
}

// ─── Convenience wrappers ─────────────────────────────────────

/** Track task lifecycle events */
export async function recordTaskEvent(event, priority = "medium") {
  await publishMetrics([
    { name: "TaskEvents",   value: 1, dimensions: { Event: event, Priority: priority } },
    { name: `Tasks${event}`, value: 1, dimensions: { Priority: priority } },
  ]);
}

/** Track API endpoint latency */
export async function recordApiLatency(endpoint, method, durationMs, statusCode) {
  await publishMetrics([
    {
      name: "ApiLatency",
      value: durationMs,
      unit: "Milliseconds",
      dimensions: { Endpoint: endpoint, Method: method },
    },
    {
      name: "ApiRequests",
      value: 1,
      dimensions: { Endpoint: endpoint, StatusCode: String(statusCode) },
    },
  ]);
}

/** Track S3 upload sizes */
export async function recordUpload(fileSizeBytes, mimeType) {
  await publishMetrics([
    { name: "AttachmentUploads", value: 1, dimensions: { MimeType: mimeType } },
    { name: "AttachmentBytes",   value: fileSizeBytes, unit: "Bytes" },
  ]);
}

/** Track session events */
export async function recordSessionEvent(event) {
  await publishMetrics([{ name: "SessionEvents", value: 1, dimensions: { Event: event } }]);
}

// Example usage in route handlers:
// await recordTaskEvent("Created", task.priority);
// await recordTaskEvent("Completed");
// await recordUpload(req.file.size, req.file.mimetype);

Embedded Metric Format (EMF) — metrics from logs, zero extra API calls

EMF is a special JSON log format that CloudWatch automatically extracts as metrics. You write structured logs and get metrics for free — no separate PutMetricData call. This is the recommended approach for Lambda functions where minimizing API calls reduces cost and latency.

src/utils/emf.js — Embedded Metric Format helperjavascript
/**
 * Embedded Metric Format (EMF) — a special CloudWatch log line structure
 * that is both a human-readable log AND a metric data point.
 * CloudWatch automatically extracts the metrics without a separate API call.
 *
 * Docs: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/CloudWatch_Embedded_Metric_Format_Specification.html
 */
export function emf(namespace, metricName, value, unit = "Count", dimensions = {}, properties = {}) {
  const entry = {
    // EMF metadata — tells CloudWatch which fields are metrics
    _aws: {
      Timestamp: Date.now(),
      CloudWatchMetrics: [{
        Namespace: namespace,
        Dimensions: [Object.keys(dimensions)],  // array of dimension name arrays
        Metrics: [{ Name: metricName, Unit: unit }],
      }],
    },
    // Dimension values (must match keys in Dimensions above)
    ...dimensions,
    // The metric value itself
    [metricName]: value,
    // Extra context (logged but not extracted as metrics)
    ...properties,
  };

  // Write to stdout — Lambda ships this to CloudWatch automatically
  process.stdout.write(JSON.stringify(entry) + "\n");
}

// ─── Usage in Lambda functions ────────────────────────────────
// This single log line creates BOTH a log record AND a CloudWatch metric.

// In your Lambda handler:
// emf(
//   "TaskBoard/Lambda",          // namespace
//   "EmailsSent",                // metric name
//   1,                           // value
//   "Count",                     // unit
//   { FunctionName: "emailNotifier", Environment: "prod" },  // dimensions
//   { taskId: 42, recipient: "user@example.com" }  // extra log context
// );
lambda/emailNotifier/index.mjs — using EMFjavascript
import { emf } from "./emf.mjs";

export const handler = async (event) => {
  const startTime = Date.now();
  let successCount = 0;
  let failCount    = 0;

  for (const record of event.Records) {
    try {
      const task = JSON.parse(record.body);
      await sendEmail(task);
      successCount++;

      // This log line is ALSO a metric — no extra API call
      emf("TaskBoard/Lambda", "EmailSent", 1, "Count",
        { Environment: process.env.NODE_ENV },
        { taskId: task.id, recipient: task.assigneeEmail }
      );

    } catch (err) {
      failCount++;
      emf("TaskBoard/Lambda", "EmailFailed", 1, "Count",
        { Environment: process.env.NODE_ENV, ErrorType: err.name },
        { error: err.message }
      );
    }
  }

  // Record total duration as a metric
  emf("TaskBoard/Lambda", "BatchDuration", Date.now() - startTime, "Milliseconds",
    { Environment: process.env.NODE_ENV },
    { batchSize: event.Records.length, successCount, failCount }
  );

  return { batchItemFailures: [] };
};

Part 3 — Metric Filters

Metric Filters bridge Logs and Metrics. They scan log streams for a pattern (a JSON field value, a string, a regex) and increment a custom CloudWatch metric every time a match is found. This lets you turn log events into actionable metrics without changing your application code.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#logsV2:log-groups
AWS Console
1

Open the log group and go to Metric filters

In CloudWatch, click Log groups. Click /taskboard/api to open it. Click the Metric filters tab, then click Create metric filter.

CloudWatch › Log groups › /taskboard/api › Metric filters › Create metric filter
Filter 1 — Count ERROR log lines
2

Define the filter pattern

Filter pattern*
{ $.level = "ERROR" }
Curly-brace syntax matches JSON fields. $.level means the top-level level field in your structured log. Click Test pattern and paste a sample log line to verify it matches before saving.

Click Next.

3

Define the metric to emit

Filter name*
ErrorCount
Metric namespace*
TaskBoard/Application
Use your own namespace — never AWS/* which is reserved. Grouping all app metrics under one namespace makes them easy to find in the alarms and dashboards metric browsers.
Metric name*
ApplicationErrors
Metric value
1
Each matching log line increments the metric by 1. For latency metrics, enter $.durationMs here to extract the actual value from the JSON field.
Default value
0
Emit 0 when no log lines match in the period. Without this, periods with no errors produce no data point — alarms can't distinguish "zero errors" from "no data".

Click Next → Create metric filter.

💡
Repeat this flow for the other three filters (ApiLatency, Http5xxErrors, TaskCompletions) from the CLI section below. Each filter is created on the same log group via the same tab.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create metric filters from logsbash
# Filter 1: Count ERROR-level log lines
# Pattern: JSON field "level" equals "ERROR"
aws logs put-metric-filter \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-name ErrorCount \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.level = "ERROR" }' \
  --metric-transformations \
    metricName=ApplicationErrors,metricNamespace=TaskBoard/Application,metricValue=1,defaultValue=0

# Filter 2: Measure API latency (extract a numeric field value)
aws logs put-metric-filter \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-name ApiLatencyFilter \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.message = "request_completed" && $.durationMs = * }' \
  --metric-transformations \
    metricName=ApiLatency,metricNamespace=TaskBoard/Application,metricValue='$.durationMs',unit=Milliseconds,defaultValue=0

# Filter 3: Count 5xx responses specifically
aws logs put-metric-filter \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-name Http5xxErrors \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.statusCode >= 500 }' \
  --metric-transformations \
    metricName=Http5xxErrors,metricNamespace=TaskBoard/Application,metricValue=1,defaultValue=0

# Filter 4: Count task completions (business metric from logs)
aws logs put-metric-filter \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-name TaskCompletions \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.message = "task_created" && $.status = "done" }' \
  --metric-transformations \
    metricName=TaskCompletions,metricNamespace=TaskBoard/Business,metricValue=1,defaultValue=0

# List all metric filters on a log group
aws logs describe-metric-filters --log-group-name /taskboard/api
✅ Metric Filter patterns reference

{ $.field = "value" } — match a JSON string field. { $.field > 100 } — numeric comparison. [w1="ERROR", ...] — space-delimited log format. "[ERROR]" — literal string match anywhere in the line. Multiple conditions: { $.level = "ERROR" && $.statusCode >= 500 }.

Part 4 — Alarms

Alarms watch a single metric over a time window and transition between three states: OK, ALARM, and INSUFFICIENT_DATA. When the state changes, it can trigger an SNS notification, an Auto Scaling action, or an EC2 action.

Alarm math — understanding evaluation periods

Alarms don't fire on a single data point — they evaluate over a rolling window. The key parameters are period (how often to sample, in seconds) and evaluationPeriods (how many consecutive periods must breach the threshold). Example: period=60, evaluationPeriods=5 means the alarm fires only if the threshold is breached for 5 consecutive minutes — reducing false positives from momentary spikes.

Option A — AWS Console

Option B — Terminal (CLI) — comprehensive alarm setup

Terminal — comprehensive alarm setupbash
# ─── SNS Topic for all alerts ───────────────────────────────────
TOPIC_ARN=$(aws sns create-topic --name taskboard-alerts --query 'TopicArn' --output text)
aws sns subscribe --topic-arn $TOPIC_ARN --protocol email --notification-endpoint you@example.com
# Confirm the email subscription — check your inbox!

# ─── EC2 Alarms ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

# CPU high — 5 consecutive 1-minute periods above 80%
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-ec2-high-cpu \
  --alarm-description "EC2 CPU sustained above 80%" \
  --namespace AWS/EC2 --metric-name CPUUtilization \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=$INSTANCE_ID \
  --statistic Average \
  --period 60 --evaluation-periods 5 \
  --threshold 80 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --treat-missing-data notBreaching \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN --ok-actions $TOPIC_ARN

# RAM high — from CloudWatch Agent custom metric (not available by default!)
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-ec2-high-memory \
  --alarm-description "EC2 memory above 85%" \
  --namespace TaskBoard/EC2 --metric-name mem_used_percent \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=$INSTANCE_ID \
  --statistic Average \
  --period 60 --evaluation-periods 3 \
  --threshold 85 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

# Disk low — from CloudWatch Agent
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-ec2-low-disk \
  --alarm-description "EC2 disk above 80% full" \
  --namespace TaskBoard/EC2 --metric-name disk_used_percent \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=$INSTANCE_ID Name=path,Value=/ \
  --statistic Average \
  --period 300 --evaluation-periods 1 \
  --threshold 80 --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

# EC2 status check failure — instance unreachable
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-ec2-status-check \
  --alarm-description "EC2 instance failing status checks" \
  --namespace AWS/EC2 --metric-name StatusCheckFailed \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=$INSTANCE_ID \
  --statistic Maximum \
  --period 60 --evaluation-periods 2 \
  --threshold 1 --comparison-operator GreaterThanOrEqualToThreshold \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

# ─── RDS Alarms ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

# RDS connections approaching limit (t3.micro max ~87)

Composite Alarms — reduce alert noise

Composite alarms combine multiple child alarms using boolean logic. Instead of getting 5 separate pages when one EC2 instance has issues, create one composite "EC2 unhealthy" alarm that groups them all. This dramatically reduces alert fatigue.

Terminal — composite alarmbash
# Fires only when BOTH CPU is high AND memory is high
# (avoids false alarms from momentary CPU spikes alone)
aws cloudwatch put-composite-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-ec2-resource-pressure \
  --alarm-description "EC2 is under sustained resource pressure" \
  --alarm-rule "ALARM(taskboard-ec2-high-cpu) AND ALARM(taskboard-ec2-high-memory)" \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

# A "critical" alarm that fires if ANY critical component fails
aws cloudwatch put-composite-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-system-critical \
  --alarm-description "A critical TaskBoard component has failed" \
  --alarm-rule "ALARM(taskboard-ec2-status-check) OR ALARM(taskboard-rds-low-storage) OR ALARM(taskboard-sqs-message-age)" \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

4

Export results or add to a Dashboard

After running a query, click Export results to download as CSV, or click Add to dashboard to pin the query result as a widget on a CloudWatch Dashboard for ongoing monitoring.

Query results › Add to dashboard
✓
Adding a Logs Insights query to a dashboard turns it into a live widget that re-runs automatically at the dashboard refresh interval — no need to manually run the query during incidents.

Logs Insights query language reference

CommandPurposeExample
fieldsSelect which log fields to displayfields @timestamp, level, message, userId
filterFilter log events by conditionfilter level = "ERROR"
statsAggregate with functions: count, sum, avg, min, max, pNstats count() by path
sortSort resultssort @timestamp desc
limitLimit the number of resultslimit 100
parseExtract fields from unstructured log text using glob or regexparse @message "userId=*" as userId
dedupRemove duplicate values for a fielddedup requestId
ispresent()Check if a field existsfilter ispresent(userId)
CloudWatch Logs Insights — production query playbooksql
-- ─── Debugging ────────────────────────────────────────────────

-- Trace a single request end-to-end by requestId
fields @timestamp, level, message, taskId, userId, durationMs
| filter requestId = "a1b2c3d4-e5f6-..."
| sort @timestamp asc

-- All errors in the last hour with stack traces
fields @timestamp, message, errorName, stack, requestId, path
| filter level = "ERROR"
| sort @timestamp desc
| limit 50

-- Find all 5xx responses and group by path (which endpoints are broken?)
fields path, statusCode
| filter statusCode >= 500
| stats count() as errorCount by path
| sort errorCount desc

-- ─── Performance ───────────────────────────────────────────────

-- API latency percentiles per endpoint (last 24h)
fields path, durationMs
| filter message = "request_completed" and ispresent(durationMs)
| stats
    count()                   as requests,
    avg(durationMs)           as avg_ms,
    pct(durationMs, 50)       as p50_ms,
    pct(durationMs, 95)       as p95_ms,
    pct(durationMs, 99)       as p99_ms,
    max(durationMs)           as max_ms
  by path
| sort p99_ms desc

-- Slowest individual requests ever (last 7 days)
fields @timestamp, method, path, durationMs, requestId, userId
| filter message = "request_completed" and durationMs > 1000
| sort durationMs desc
| limit 20

-- Request rate over time (bucketed by 5 minutes)
fields @timestamp
| filter message = "request_completed"
| stats count() as requestsPerWindow
  by bin(5m)
| sort @timestamp asc

-- ─── Security ───────────────────────────────────────────────────

-- Find IPs making the most failed auth requests (brute force detection)
fields ip
| filter statusCode = 401
| stats count() as failedAttempts by ip
| sort failedAttempts desc
| limit 20

-- Requests with no authenticated userId (public or unauthorized)
fields @timestamp, method, path, statusCode, ip
| filter not ispresent(userId)
| filter path not like /health/
| stats count() as unauthRequests by ip
| sort unauthRequests desc

-- ─── Business ───────────────────────────────────────────────────

-- Tasks created per day over the past week
fields @timestamp
| filter message = "task_created"
| stats count() as tasksCreated by datefloor(@timestamp, 1d)
| sort @timestamp asc

-- Most active users by task operations
fields userId
| filter ispresent(userId) and message in ["task_created","task_updated","task_completed"]
| stats count() as operations by userId
| sort operations desc
| limit 10

-- ─── Lambda ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────

-- Lambda cold starts (INIT_START lines)
filter @type = "INIT_START"
| stats count() as coldStarts by bin(1h)
| sort @timestamp desc

-- Lambda error details from all functions
fields @timestamp, @logStream, @message
| filter @message like /ERROR/
| sort @timestamp desc
| limit 30

-- Lambda duration distribution (are functions timing out?)
filter @type = "REPORT"
| parse @message "Duration: * ms" as duration
| stats
    avg(duration) as avg_duration,
    pct(duration, 95) as p95_duration,
    max(duration) as max_duration
  by @logStream
Terminal — run Insights queries from the CLIbash
# Start a query (returns a queryId immediately)
QUERY_ID=$(aws logs start-query \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --start-time $(date -d "1 hour ago" +%s) \
  --end-time $(date +%s) \
  --query-string 'fields @timestamp, level, message | filter level = "ERROR" | sort @timestamp desc | limit 20' \
  --query 'queryId' --output text)

# Poll for results (queries run asynchronously)
aws logs get-query-results --query-id $QUERY_ID

# Keep polling until status = "Complete"
# (or use the CloudWatch Console for an interactive experience)
while true; do
  STATUS=$(aws logs get-query-results --query-id $QUERY_ID --query 'status' --output text)
  echo "Status: $STATUS"
  [ "$STATUS" = "Complete" ] && break
  sleep 2
done

aws logs get-query-results --query-id $QUERY_ID \
  --query 'results' --output json

Part 6 — CloudWatch Dashboards

Dashboards are shareable, real-time visual panels. Build one dashboard per application or team, containing all the metrics that matter for that context. They auto-refresh and can be shared via URL.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#dashboards
AWS Console
1

Create a new dashboard

In CloudWatch, click Dashboards in the sidebar. Click Create dashboard. Name it TaskBoard-Operations and click Create dashboard.

CloudWatch › Dashboards → Create dashboard
2

Add an Alarm Status widget (system health overview)

In the Add widget dialog, select Alarm status and click Configure. Search for and add all four taskboard alarms: taskboard-ec2-status-check, taskboard-rds-high-cpu, taskboard-lambda-errors, taskboard-sqs-message-age. Set the title to System Health. Click Update widget.

💡
The alarm status widget shows a colour-coded grid — green for OK, red for ALARM, grey for insufficient data. This is the first thing you look at when you get an alert email.
3

Add a Line chart for EC2 CPU & Memory

Click + Add widget → Line → Configure. In the metric browser, navigate to EC2 → Per-Instance Metrics → CPUUtilization for your instance. Then also add TaskBoard/EC2 → mem_used_percent from the custom namespace. Set the title to EC2 CPU & Memory. Add a horizontal annotation at y = 80 labelled "Warning".

Widget type
Line
Auto-refresh
1 minute
Set this on the dashboard toolbar (top right) — not per widget. 1-minute refresh is sufficient for operational monitoring.
4

Add widgets for RDS, Lambda, and SQS — then save

Repeat the Add widget → Line flow for:

  • RDS Connections — AWS/RDS → DatabaseConnections, dimension DBInstanceIdentifier = taskboard-db. Add annotation at y=70.
  • Lambda Errors & Duration — AWS/Lambda → Errors + Duration (p99), FunctionName = taskboard-email-notifier.
  • SQS Queue Depth — AWS/SQS → ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible + NotVisible, QueueName = taskboard-notifications.

Drag the widgets to arrange them. Click Save dashboard at the top.

✓
Your dashboard URL is shareable. Click the Actions → Share dashboard button to get a public link your team can bookmark without needing an AWS account — useful for on-call engineers checking status on their phone.
