Part 7 — Live log tailing

During development and incident response, you often need to watch logs in real time. Both the console and CLI support this — the console is more visual, the CLI is faster for filtering.

Option A — AWS Console (CloudWatch Live Tail)

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#logsV2:live-tail
AWS Console
1

Open Live Tail

In CloudWatch, click Live Tail in the left sidebar under Logs. This is a real-time log stream — events appear within 1–2 seconds of being written.

CloudWatch › Logs › Live Tail
💡
Live Tail costs $0.01 per minute of streaming. Stop the session when you're done — click Stop or close the browser tab. A 30-minute debugging session costs $0.30.
2

Select log groups and apply a filter

Select log groups*
/taskboard/api
You can add multiple log groups. Events from all selected groups are interleaved in real time, colour-coded by source log group.
Filter events (optional)
{ $.level = "ERROR" }
Uses the same filter pattern syntax as metric filters. Leave blank to see all events. Use { $.requestId = "abc123" } to trace a single request in real time.
Highlight terms (optional)
ERROR, WARN, taskId
Words in this list are highlighted in yellow in the stream — makes it easy to spot important values in dense JSON logs.

Click Start. Log events begin streaming immediately. Click any event row to expand the full JSON.

✓
Use Live Tail during deployments — watch for errors in real time as the new version starts. If errors appear, roll back immediately rather than waiting for an alarm to fire.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — live log tailing and searchbash
# Live tail all streams in a log group (like tail -f)
# Requires AWS CLI v2.13+
aws logs tail /taskboard/api --follow

# Tail with a filter pattern — only show ERROR lines
aws logs tail /taskboard/api \
  --follow \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.level = "ERROR" }'

# Tail Lambda logs for a specific function
aws logs tail /aws/lambda/taskboard-email-notifier --follow

# Tail multiple log groups at once (new feature)
aws logs tail /taskboard/api /aws/lambda/taskboard-email-notifier --follow

# Get last 100 lines (no --follow, exits immediately)
aws logs tail /taskboard/api --since 1h | head -100

# Search historically — find errors in a time range
aws logs filter-log-events \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-pattern '{ $.level = "ERROR" }' \
  --start-time $(date -d "2 hours ago" +%s)000 \
  --end-time $(date +%s)000 \
  --query 'events[*].message' \
  --output json | python3 -m json.tool
✅ Observability checklist for production

Before going live, verify all of the following: structured JSON logs on every service ✓ · log groups with retention policies ✓ · CloudWatch Agent running on EC2 (RAM + disk metrics) ✓ · metric filters for errors and latency ✓ · alarms for CPU, memory, disk, RDS connections, Lambda errors, SQS age ✓ · SNS email subscription confirmed ✓ · dashboard with health overview ✓ · billing alarm at $5 ✓.

Anomaly Detection Alarms

Instead of a fixed threshold, Anomaly Detection uses ML to learn the normal range for a metric — including daily and weekly patterns — and alarms when actual values deviate significantly. A request rate alarm based on anomaly detection fires when traffic is unusually low (potential outage) OR unusually high (DDoS), without you having to know the "normal" number in advance.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#alarmsV2:create
AWS Console
1

Create an Anomaly Detection alarm

Go to CloudWatch → Alarms → Create alarm → Select metric. Select your metric (e.g. TaskBoard/Application → ApiRequests). On the conditions page:

Threshold type
● Anomaly detection
Anomaly detection threshold
2
The band width in standard deviations. A value of 2 means "alarm if the metric is more than 2 standard deviations above or below the predicted band". Higher = less sensitive.
Alarm when metric is
● Outside the band (above or below)

CloudWatch shows a graph with the predicted band and historical metric overlaid. The band widens at times of historically higher traffic (e.g. weekday afternoons) and narrows when traffic is consistently low (weekends). Click Next and attach to your SNS topic.

💡
Anomaly detection requires at least 3 days of metric data before the model is accurate. During the training period it uses a default flat band.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create an anomaly detection alarmbash
# First create the anomaly detector for the metric
aws cloudwatch put-anomaly-detector \
  --namespace TaskBoard/Application \
  --metric-name ApiRequests \
  --stat Sum

# Create the alarm using the anomaly band as the threshold
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name taskboard-api-request-anomaly \
  --alarm-description "API request rate deviates from normal pattern" \
  --metrics '[{
    "Id": "m1",
    "MetricStat": {
      "Metric": {"Namespace":"TaskBoard/Application","MetricName":"ApiRequests"},
      "Period": 300, "Stat": "Sum"
    }
  },{
    "Id": "ad1",
    "Expression": "ANOMALY_DETECTION_BAND(m1, 2)",
    "Label": "ApiRequests (expected)"
  }]' \
  --comparison-operator GreaterThanUpperThreshold \
  --threshold-metric-id ad1 \
  --evaluation-periods 2 \
  --alarm-actions $TOPIC_ARN

Contributor Insights

Contributor Insights analyses CloudWatch Logs and identifies which specific values — IP addresses, user IDs, API paths, error codes — are the top contributors to a pattern. Use it to find which user is making the most API calls, which IP is hammering your server, or which endpoint generates the most errors — all without writing a Logs Insights query manually.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#contributor-insights:create
AWS Console
1

Create a Contributor Insights rule

In CloudWatch sidebar click Contributor Insights → Create rule.

CloudWatch › Insights › Contributor Insights › Create rule
Rule name
top-api-users
Log group
/taskboard/api
Rule body (JSON)
{ "Schema": { "Name": "CloudWatchLogRule", "Version": 1 },
  "LogGroupNames": ["/taskboard/api"],
  "LogFormat": "JSON",
  "Fields": { "$.userId": "userId" },
  "Contribution": { "Keys": ["userId"], "CountedFields": ["userId"] },
  "AggregateOn": "Count" }
This finds the top users by request count. Change $.userId to $.ip for top IPs, or $.path for top endpoints.

Click Create. The rule processes new log events in real time and displays a ranked list of top contributors in the console.

CloudWatch Synthetics (Canaries)

Synthetics runs canary scripts — simulated user journeys in a headless browser or HTTP client — on a configurable schedule. A canary calls your API or loads your web page, checks the response, and reports availability and latency to CloudWatch. It detects outages before real users report them, and it monitors from outside your VPC (real user perspective).

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/home#synthetics:canary/create
AWS Console
1

Create a canary that monitors the health endpoint

In CloudWatch sidebar click Application monitoring → Synthetics canaries → Create canary.

CloudWatch › Synthetics canaries › Create canary
Name
taskboard-health-check
Blueprint
● API canary
Use the API canary blueprint for REST endpoints. It makes an HTTP request and checks the status code. Use the Heartbeat monitor for a simple availability check.
URL
http://your-alb-dns.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com/health
Schedule
Run continuously — every 1 minute
CloudWatch alarms
● Enable — alarm if success rate drops below 90%

Click Create canary. Results appear within 2 minutes. Each run stores a screenshot (for browser canaries) and an HTTP response artifact in the S3 bucket Synthetics creates automatically.

✓
Your TaskBoard API is now monitored from outside AWS every minute. If the health endpoint doesn't respond with 200 OK, the canary alarms and you get an SNS email — before any user experiences the outage.

Container Insights

Container Insights provides enhanced, per-container metrics for ECS and EKS — CPU, memory, network, disk, and pod/task counts — with automatic dashboards. Standard EC2 CloudWatch metrics don't track individual container resource usage; Container Insights fills that gap when you move from EC2 to containers.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/ecs/v2/clusters
AWS Console
1

Enable Container Insights on an existing cluster

Go to ECS → Clusters → taskboard-cluster. Click the Update cluster button (top right).

Container Insights
Enabled
Enabling this causes ECS to push per-container CPU, memory, and network metrics to CloudWatch under the ECS/ContainerInsights namespace.

Click Update. Metrics and automatic dashboards appear in CloudWatch within 5 minutes.

💡
View the dashboards at CloudWatch → Container Insights → ECS. Select your cluster from the dropdown to see per-service and per-task metrics.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — enable Container Insights on an ECS clusterbash
# Enable on a new ECS cluster
aws ecs create-cluster \
  --cluster-name taskboard-cluster \
  --settings name=containerInsights,value=enabled

# Enable on an existing cluster
aws ecs update-cluster-settings \
  --cluster taskboard-cluster \
  --settings name=containerInsights,value=enabled

# Metrics become available in CloudWatch under AWS/ECS namespace:
# CpuUtilized, MemoryUtilized, NetworkRxBytes, NetworkTxBytes
# grouped by ClusterName, ServiceName, TaskDefinitionFamily

Cross-Account Observability

When you have multiple AWS accounts (dev, staging, prod), cross-account observability lets you view logs, metrics, and traces from all accounts in a single monitoring account's CloudWatch dashboard. One engineer can see the full system state without logging into each account separately.

💡 Console — CloudWatch → Settings → Cross-account observability

The console does provide a cross-account observability setup wizard at CloudWatch → Settings → Cross-account observability → Configure. However, the underlying OAM (Observability Access Manager) API configuration — creating sinks, setting resource policies, and creating links — is more reliably done through the CLI shown below, especially when configuring multiple accounts. The console wizard covers the common case but may not expose all filtering options.

Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — set up cross-account observability (monitoring account and source accounts)bash
MONITORING_ACCOUNT="111111111111"  # central monitoring account
SOURCE_ACCOUNT="222222222222"       # prod account with the actual resources

# ─── In the MONITORING account ───────────────────────────────────
aws cloudwatch put-sink \
  --name taskboard-monitoring-sink \
  --policy '{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::'"$SOURCE_ACCOUNT"':root"},
      "Action": ["oam:CreateLink"],
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "ForAllValues:StringEquals": {
          "oam:ResourceTypes": ["AWS::Logs::LogGroup","AWS::CloudWatch::Metric"]
        }
      }
    }]
  }'

SINK_ARN=$(aws cloudwatch list-sinks --query 'Items[0].Arn' --output text)

# ─── In the SOURCE (prod) account ────────────────────────────────
aws cloudwatch create-link \
  --label-template "$AccountName" \
  --resource-types "AWS::Logs::LogGroup" "AWS::CloudWatch::Metric" \
  --sink-identifier $SINK_ARN

# From the monitoring account, /taskboard/api logs from the prod account
# now appear in CloudWatch Logs Insights when you select the source account filter
Advanced Topic 3
CI/CD & Deployment Pipeline
Automate your deployment so every git push triggers a test, build, and deploy to EC2. We use GitHub Actions as the CI/CD runner — it's free and integrates perfectly with AWS.

Setup — Configure GitHub Secrets

The GitHub Actions workflow needs three AWS values stored as encrypted secrets in your repository. These are injected into the workflow at runtime and never exposed in logs.

github.com/YOUR_USERNAME/taskboard-api/settings/secrets/actions
GitHub
1

Create a deployment IAM user in AWS

Before setting GitHub Secrets, create a dedicated IAM user for CI/CD. Go to IAM → Users → Add users. Name it taskboard-github-deploy, attach the policy below, and create an access key with the Application running outside AWS use case.

iam/github-deploy-policy.jsonjson
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "SSMRunCommand",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["ssm:SendCommand", "ssm:GetCommandInvocation", "ssm:ListCommandInvocations"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:ec2:us-east-1:*:instance/*",
        "arn:aws:ssm:us-east-1::document/AWS-RunShellScript"
      ]
    },
    {
      "Sid": "WaitForCommand",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "ssm:DescribeInstanceInformation",
      "Resource": "*"
    }
  ]
}
✅
This policy is intentionally narrow — it can only run SSM commands on EC2 instances. It cannot create, delete, or modify any AWS resource. This is the principle of least privilege applied to CI/CD.
2

Open your GitHub repository settings

In your GitHub repository, click Settings → Secrets and variables → Actions → New repository secret. Add all three secrets below.

GitHub repo › Settings › Secrets and variables › Actions › New repository secret
Secret nameValueWhere to find it
AWS_ACCESS_KEY_IDAKIA…IAM → taskboard-github-deploy → Security credentials → Access keys
AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEYwJal…Same page — only shown once at creation time
EC2_INSTANCE_IDi-0abc123…EC2 → Instances → taskboard-server → Instance ID column
EC2_PUBLIC_IP54.123.45.67EC2 → Instances → taskboard-server → Elastic IP or Public IPv4
3

Verify the SSM agent is ready on EC2

The deployment workflow uses SSM to run commands on EC2 without SSH. Verify the SSM agent is running and the EC2 role has the required policy.

# From your laptop — check instance is visible to SSM
aws ssm describe-instance-information \
  --query 'InstanceInformationList[*].[InstanceId,PingStatus]' \
  --output table
# Should show your instance ID with PingStatus: Online
⚠️
If the instance doesn't appear, attach AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore to the TaskboardEC2Role. Go to IAM → Roles → TaskboardEC2Role → Attach policies, search for the policy and attach it. The instance appears in SSM within 1–2 minutes.

GitHub Actions deployment workflow

.github/workflows/deploy.ymlyaml
name: Deploy to EC2

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: '20' }
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm test

  deploy:
    needs: test
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: production
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Configure AWS credentials
        uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          aws-access-key-id:     ${{ secrets.AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID }}
          aws-secret-access-key: ${{ secrets.AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY }}
          aws-region: us-east-1

      - name: Deploy to EC2 via SSM (no SSH key needed!)
        run: |
          COMMAND_ID=$(aws ssm send-command \
            --instance-ids ${{ secrets.EC2_INSTANCE_ID }} \
            --document-name "AWS-RunShellScript" \
            --parameters 'commands=[
              "cd /home/appuser/app",
              "git pull origin main",
              "npm ci --production",
              "pm2 restart taskboard --update-env"
            ]' \
            --query "Command.CommandId" --output text)

          # Wait for command to finish
          aws ssm wait command-executed \
            --command-id $COMMAND_ID \
            --instance-id ${{ secrets.EC2_INSTANCE_ID }}

          # Check exit code
          STATUS=$(aws ssm get-command-invocation \
            --command-id $COMMAND_ID \
            --instance-id ${{ secrets.EC2_INSTANCE_ID }} \
            --query "Status" --output text)

          echo "Deploy status: $STATUS"
          [ "$STATUS" = "Success" ] || exit 1

      - name: Verify deployment
        run: |
          sleep 5
          curl -f http://${{ secrets.EC2_PUBLIC_IP }}:3000/health || exit 1
          echo "Deployment verified!"
✅ SSM vs SSH

AWS Systems Manager (SSM) lets you run commands on EC2 without opening SSH port 22 to the internet. Much more secure. Requires the SSM agent (pre-installed on Amazon Linux) and the AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore policy on your EC2 role.

Blue-green deployment strategy

For zero-downtime deployments, use blue-green: run two identical environments (blue = live, green = new version). Switch traffic at the load balancer level when green is ready.

Blue-Green Deployment Flow
ALB (Load Balancer)
→ 100% →
Blue (v1.0 — LIVE)
Deploy v1.1 to Green, run tests...
ALB
→ 100% →
Green (v1.1 — LIVE)
Blue kept as rollback

OIDC for GitHub Actions — no stored credentials

Instead of storing long-lived AWS access keys in GitHub Secrets, configure AWS to trust GitHub's OIDC provider. GitHub Actions can then assume an IAM role directly using a short-lived token — no access keys anywhere, no credential rotation, and if a repository is compromised the token expires in minutes.

Option A — AWS Console

  --url https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
  --client-id-list sts.amazonaws.com \
  --thumbprint-list 6938fd4d98bab03faadb97b34396831e3780aea1

# Create a role that only YOUR repo can assume
aws iam create-role \
  --role-name TaskboardGitHubActionsRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": {
        "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::'"$ACCOUNT_ID"':oidc-provider/token.actions.githubusercontent.com"
      },
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud": "sts.amazonaws.com"
        },
        "StringLike": {
          "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub": "repo:yourname/taskboard-api:*"
        }
      }
    }]
  }'

aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name TaskboardGitHubActionsRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::$ACCOUNT_ID:policy/TaskboardDeployPolicy
.github/workflows/deploy.yml — updated to use OIDC (no stored keys)yaml
permissions:
  id-token: write   # required for OIDC
  contents: read

steps:
  - uses: actions/checkout@v4

  - name: Configure AWS credentials (OIDC — no stored secrets)
    uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
    with:
      role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT_ID:role/TaskboardGitHubActionsRole
      aws-region: us-east-1

  - name: Deploy
    run: aws ssm send-command ...

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create an ECR repository and push an imagebash
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
REGION="us-east-1"
REPO="taskboard-api"

# Create the repository with vulnerability scanning on push
aws ecr create-repository \
  --repository-name $REPO \
  --region $REGION \
  --image-scanning-configuration scanOnPush=true \
  --image-tag-mutability IMMUTABLE  # prevents overwriting existing tags — use for prod

# Authenticate Docker
aws ecr get-login-password --region $REGION | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin \
  $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com

# Build, tag, and push
docker build -t $REPO .
docker tag $REPO:latest $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)
docker push $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)

# Set a lifecycle policy — keep only last 10 images to control storage costs
aws ecr put-lifecycle-policy \
  --repository-name $REPO \
  --lifecycle-policy-text '{
    "rules":[{
      "rulePriority":1,"description":"Keep last 10 images",
      "selection":{"tagStatus":"any","countType":"imageCountMoreThan","countNumber":10},
      "action":{"type":"expire"}
    }]
  }'

Infrastructure as Code (IaC)

Everything you have built in this tutorial through the console and CLI should ultimately be defined as code. IaC makes environments reproducible, changes reviewable in pull requests, and eliminates the "works in dev but broke in prod" problem caused by manual configuration drift. This is the most important next step after mastering the console.

AWS CDK (TypeScript) — recommended for Node.js teams

Terminal — bootstrap and create a CDK projectbash
npm install -g aws-cdk
cdk bootstrap aws://ACCOUNT_ID/us-east-1
mkdir taskboard-infra && cd taskboard-infra
cdk init app --language typescript
lib/taskboard-stack.ts — TaskBoard infrastructure in TypeScriptjavascript
import * as cdk  from "aws-cdk-lib";
import * as ec2  from "aws-cdk-lib/aws-ec2";
import * as s3   from "aws-cdk-lib/aws-s3";
import * as sqs  from "aws-cdk-lib/aws-sqs";
import * as rds  from "aws-cdk-lib/aws-rds";

export class TaskboardStack extends cdk.Stack {
  constructor(scope: cdk.App, id: string, props?: cdk.StackProps) {
    super(scope, id, props);

    // VPC with public and private subnets across 2 AZs
    const vpc = new ec2.Vpc(this, "TaskboardVpc", {
      maxAzs: 2,
      subnetConfiguration: [
        { name: "public",  subnetType: ec2.SubnetType.PUBLIC,          cidrMask: 24 },
        { name: "private", subnetType: ec2.SubnetType.PRIVATE_WITH_EGRESS, cidrMask: 24 },
      ],
    });

    // S3 bucket with versioning and encryption
    const bucket = new s3.Bucket(this, "Attachments", {
      versioned: true,
      encryption: s3.BucketEncryption.S3_MANAGED,
      blockPublicAccess: s3.BlockPublicAccess.BLOCK_ALL,
      lifecycleRules: [{ transitions: [{ storageClass: s3.StorageClass.INFREQUENT_ACCESS, transitionAfter: cdk.Duration.days(30) }] }],
    });

    // SQS queue with DLQ
    const dlq = new sqs.Queue(this, "NotificationsDlq", { retentionPeriod: cdk.Duration.days(14) });
    const queue = new sqs.Queue(this, "Notifications", {
      visibilityTimeout: cdk.Duration.seconds(60),
      deadLetterQueue: { queue: dlq, maxReceiveCount: 3 },
    });

    // RDS PostgreSQL in private subnets
    const db = new rds.DatabaseInstance(this, "Database", {
      engine: rds.DatabaseInstanceEngine.postgres({ version: rds.PostgresEngineVersion.VER_15 }),
      instanceType: ec2.InstanceType.of(ec2.InstanceClass.T3, ec2.InstanceSize.MICRO),
      vpc, vpcSubnets: { subnetType: ec2.SubnetType.PRIVATE_WITH_EGRESS },
      databaseName: "taskboard",
      deletionProtection: true,
      backupRetention: cdk.Duration.days(7),
    });

    new cdk.CfnOutput(this, "BucketName",   { value: bucket.bucketName });
    new cdk.CfnOutput(this, "QueueUrl",     { value: queue.queueUrl });
    new cdk.CfnOutput(this, "DbEndpoint",   { value: db.dbInstanceEndpointAddress });
  }
}

// Deploy: cdk deploy
Service 8 — Compute
API Gateway
API Gateway is AWS's fully managed API layer that sits between clients and your backend, handling routing, authentication, throttling, request transformation, and SSL termination. It replaces the need for an Express.js server entirely when building serverless architectures — routing HTTP requests directly to Lambda handlers. Every function you've written in this tutorial can be exposed via API Gateway without running any servers.
HTTP APIREST APIWebSocketLambda ProxyJWT AuthThrottlingCustom Domains

HTTP API vs REST API

API Gateway offers two API types. Choose HTTP API for new projects — it is 70% cheaper, has lower latency, and covers 90% of use cases. Use REST API only if you specifically need response caching, usage plans with API keys, or AWS service integrations beyond Lambda.

FeatureHTTP APIREST API
Cost per million requests$1.00$3.50
Latency overhead~1ms~5ms
JWT authorisers (Cognito, Auth0)Native — no Lambda neededVia custom Lambda authoriser
Response cachingNot supportedSupported — cache per stage/route
Usage plans & API keysNot supportedFull support — rate and quota per key
Request/response transformationBasic mapping onlyFull Velocity Template Language transforms
CORSAuto-configuredMust configure OPTIONS integration manually
WebSocket APIsNot supportedSupported (separate API type)
Lambda payload formatv2.0 (simpler)v1.0
Private integrations (VPC Link)SupportedSupported

How the Lambda proxy integration works

When API Gateway receives an HTTP request, it packages the entire request — headers, query string, body, path parameters, stage variables — into a single JSON event object and passes it to your Lambda function. Your Lambda returns a structured response object and API Gateway converts it back to an HTTP response. This is called proxy integration.

Lambda handler — HTTP API payload v2.0 formatjavascript
// event shape for HTTP API (payload format version 2.0)
export const handler = async (event) => {
  const { routeKey, pathParameters, queryStringParameters, body, requestContext } = event;

  // routeKey = "POST /tasks" or "GET /tasks/{id}"
  // requestContext.authorizer.jwt.claims = decoded JWT claims (if JWT authoriser attached)
  const userId = requestContext?.authorizer?.jwt?.claims?.sub;

  console.log(`${routeKey} called by user ${userId}`);

  let parsed;
  try {
    parsed = JSON.parse(body || '{}');
  } catch {
    return { statusCode: 400, body: JSON.stringify({ error: 'Invalid JSON' }) };
  }

  // Return must include statusCode; body must be a string
  return {
    statusCode: 200,
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
    body: JSON.stringify({ message: 'ok', received: parsed }),
  };
};

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — create HTTP API with routes and JWT authoriserbash
LAMBDA_ARN="arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:function:taskboard-email-notifier"

# Create the HTTP API
API_ID=$(aws apigatewayv2 create-api \
  --name taskboard-api \
  --protocol-type HTTP \
  --query 'ApiId' --output text)

# Create Lambda proxy integration
INTEGRATION_ID=$(aws apigatewayv2 create-integration \
  --api-id $API_ID \
  --integration-type AWS_PROXY \
  --integration-uri $LAMBDA_ARN \
  --payload-format-version 2.0 \
  --query 'IntegrationId' --output text)

# Catch-all route — Lambda handles all methods and paths
aws apigatewayv2 create-route \
  --api-id $API_ID \
  --route-key 'ANY /{proxy+}' \
  --target "integrations/$INTEGRATION_ID"

# Grant API Gateway permission to invoke Lambda
aws lambda add-permission \
  --function-name taskboard-email-notifier \
  --statement-id apigw-invoke \
  --action lambda:InvokeFunction \
  --principal apigateway.amazonaws.com \
  --source-arn "arn:aws:execute-api:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:$API_ID/*"

# Deploy a stage
aws apigatewayv2 create-stage \
  --api-id $API_ID \
  --stage-name prod \
  --auto-deploy

# Get the invoke URL
echo "https://$API_ID.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/prod"

# Add JWT authoriser
AUTH_ID=$(aws apigatewayv2 create-authorizer \
  --api-id $API_ID \
  --name taskboard-jwt \
  --authorizer-type JWT \
  --identity-source '$request.header.Authorization' \
  --jwt-configuration '{
    "Issuer": "https://cognito-idp.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/us-east-1_XXXXXX",
    "Audience": ["your-app-client-id"]
  }' \
  --query 'AuthorizerId' --output text)

Throttling and usage plans

Without throttling, a single client can overwhelm your Lambda functions with thousands of requests per second. API Gateway provides two layers of throttling:

LevelSettingDefaultPurpose
Account-levelBurst: 5,000 / Steady: 10,000 rpsApplied to all APIs in the regionProtect your AWS account from runaway traffic
Stage-levelSet under Stage → EditInherits account defaultLimit a specific API/stage (e.g. staging vs prod)
Route-levelSet on individual routesInherits stageRate-limit expensive endpoints (e.g. file upload)

Custom domain names

The default API Gateway URL (https://abcdef.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/prod) is not suitable for production. Map a custom domain like api.taskboard.io using ACM and Route 53.

Terminal — attach a custom domain to API Gatewaybash
CERT_ARN="arn:aws:acm:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:certificate/xxxxx"

# Create the custom domain (ACM cert must be in same region for HTTP APIs)
DOMAIN_NAME_ARN=$(aws apigatewayv2 create-domain-name \
  --domain-name api.taskboard.io \
  --domain-name-configurations CertificateArn=$CERT_ARN \
  --query 'DomainNameConfigurations[0].ApiGatewayDomainName' \
  --output text)

# Map the API to the domain
aws apigatewayv2 create-api-mapping \
  --domain-name api.taskboard.io \
  --api-id $API_ID \
  --stage prod

# Create Route 53 alias record pointing to the API Gateway domain
aws route53 change-resource-record-sets \
  --hosted-zone-id $ZONE_ID \
  --change-batch '{
    "Changes": [{"Action":"CREATE","ResourceRecordSet":{
      "Name":"api.taskboard.io","Type":"A",
      "AliasTarget":{"HostedZoneId":"Z1UJRXOUMOOFQ8","DNSName":"'"$DOMAIN_NAME_ARN"'","EvaluateTargetHealth":false}
    }}]
  }'
Service 9 — Networking
CloudFront
CloudFront is AWS's global CDN with 400+ edge locations worldwide. It caches responses close to users, terminates HTTPS via ACM certificates, provides DDoS protection through AWS Shield Standard (free), and integrates with WAF for application-layer filtering. It's not just for static assets — CloudFront sits in front of the TaskBoard ALB too, providing consistent latency, SSL offloading, and one place to enforce security policies across all origins.
CDN400+ Edge LocationsHTTPS / ACMOACCache PoliciesWAFSPA Hosting

How CloudFront works

When a user requests a resource, CloudFront routes the request to the nearest of 400+ edge locations worldwide. If the response is cached there (a cache hit), CloudFront returns it immediately — no round-trip to your origin. If it's a cache miss, CloudFront fetches the response from your origin (S3, ALB, API Gateway, or any HTTP server), caches it at the edge, and returns it. Subsequent users at that edge location get the cached copy.

CloudFront request flow
User in London
→
CloudFront Edge (London)
→ cache miss only →
Origin (S3 / ALB in us-east-1)
Cache hit: ~5ms. Cache miss: full round-trip to origin, but cached afterwards.

Origins and cache behaviours

Origin typeUse caseKey setting
S3 bucketStatic website hosting, SPA (React/Vue)Use Origin Access Control (OAC) — never make the S3 bucket public
ALBDynamic API served by EC2 or ECSMatch Host header; configure HTTPS between CloudFront and ALB
API GatewayServerless APICache-Control headers from your Lambda control TTLs
Custom HTTPOn-premise server, third-party serviceCloudFront connects via HTTPS to the custom origin

Origin Access Control (OAC) — secure S3 access

Never make your S3 bucket public for CloudFront. Instead, use Origin Access Control (OAC) — CloudFront signs requests to S3 with its own IAM identity. The S3 bucket policy allows only CloudFront to read objects. Users cannot bypass CloudFront to access S3 directly.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — deploy SPA with cache-busting strategybash
DISTRIBUTION_ID="E1XXXXXXXX"
SITE_BUCKET="app.taskboard.io"

npm run build

# Hashed assets (app.abc123.js) — cache forever
aws s3 sync dist/ s3://$SITE_BUCKET --delete \
  --exclude "index.html" \
  --cache-control "max-age=31536000,immutable,public"

# index.html — never cache (ensures users always get the latest entry point)
aws s3 cp dist/index.html s3://$SITE_BUCKET/index.html \
  --cache-control "no-cache,no-store,must-revalidate" \
  --content-type "text/html"

# Invalidate only index.html — hashed files are already new URLs
aws cloudfront create-invalidation \
  --distribution-id $DISTRIBUTION_ID \
  --paths "/index.html"

# View distribution status
aws cloudfront get-distribution \
  --id $DISTRIBUTION_ID \
  --query 'Distribution.Status' --output text

Cache policies — controlling what gets cached

CloudFront's cache key determines whether two requests share the same cached response. By default, only the URL path is in the cache key. You can add headers, query strings, and cookies — but every addition reduces cache hit rate. Keep the cache key as small as possible.

Terminal — create a custom cache policy for API responsesbash
# Cache API responses for 5 minutes; include Authorization header in cache key
aws cloudfront create-cache-policy --cache-policy-config '{
  "Name": "taskboard-api-cache",
  "DefaultTTL": 300, "MaxTTL": 300, "MinTTL": 0,
  "ParametersInCacheKeyAndForwardedToOrigin": {
    "EnableAcceptEncodingGzip": true, "EnableAcceptEncodingBrotli": true,
    "HeadersConfig": {"HeaderBehavior": "whitelist", "Headers": {"Quantity": 1, "Items": ["Authorization"]}},
    "CookiesConfig": {"CookieBehavior": "none"},
    "QueryStringsConfig": {"QueryStringBehavior": "all"}
  }
}'
Service 10 — Data
ElastiCache (Redis)
ElastiCache Redis is the in-memory layer that keeps your PostgreSQL and DynamoDB from being the bottleneck. It sits in your VPC alongside your EC2 and Lambda resources, accessible in under 1ms. Every high-traffic feature in TaskBoard can use Redis: task list caching, session storage, rate limiting per user, real-time notifications via pub/sub, and distributed locks to prevent duplicate job processing.
Redis 7Cache-asideSessionsRate LimitingPub/SubDistributed LockSorted Sets

When to use ElastiCache

PatternProblem solvedRedis mechanism
Cache-aside (read-through)Slow PostgreSQL queries under loadGET → miss → DB query → SET EX 300
Session storeDynamoDB session latency too highSET session:{id} with TTL
Rate limitingAPI abuse / per-user throttlingINCR + EXPIRE per minute window
LeaderboardsRanked lists (tasks by priority score)ZADD sorted set, ZRANGE
Pub/SubReal-time notifications to connected clientsPUBLISH / SUBSCRIBE
Distributed lockPrevent duplicate job processing across Lambda invocationsSET NX EX (atomic lock)
Job queueLightweight background task queueLPUSH / BRPOP

Serverless vs cluster mode

ElastiCache now offers two deployment models. Serverless (launched 2023) automatically scales capacity and charges per GB-hour of data stored and per ECU (ElastiCache Compute Unit) consumed — no cluster sizing required. Cluster mode uses fixed node types and is cheaper at predictable, sustained load.

Create a Redis cluster

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/elasticache/home#/create
AWS Console
1

Choose Serverless (simplest) or Cluster

Search ElastiCache → Create serverless cache for the simplest option. For a fixed-size cluster, choose Create cluster → Redis OSS.

Cache name
taskboard-cache
Engine version
Redis 7.x (latest)
Node type (cluster mode)
cache.t3.micro (~$0.017/hr for dev)
For production under load, use cache.r7g.large — memory-optimised, graviton-based, significantly faster. Right-size with Compute Optimizer after running in production for 2 weeks.
Replicas
1 (production) or 0 (dev)
1 replica enables automatic failover — if the primary node fails, the replica is promoted within ~60 seconds. For multi-AZ high availability, place primary and replica in different AZs.
VPC
taskboard-vpc
Subnets
☑ taskboard-private-1a   ☑ taskboard-private-1b
ElastiCache must be in private subnets — it has no public endpoint and should only be reachable from within your VPC.

Click Create. Copy the Primary endpoint from the cluster details once status is Available.

  --replication-group-id taskboard-cache \
  --query 'ReplicationGroups[0].NodeGroups[0].PrimaryEndpoint.Address' \
  --output text

Node.js integration — connection management and patterns

src/services/cacheService.js — production-grade Redis clientjavascript
import { createClient } from "redis";  // npm install redis

let client;

export async function getRedis() {
  if (client?.isReady) return client;

  client = createClient({
    url: `redis://${process.env.REDIS_HOST}:6379`,
    socket: {
      reconnectStrategy: retries => Math.min(retries * 100, 3000), // exponential backoff
      connectTimeout: 5000,
    },
  });

  client.on("error", err => console.error("Redis error:", err));
  client.on("reconnecting", () => console.log("Redis reconnecting..."));
  await client.connect();
  return client;
}

// ── Cache-aside: check cache → fallback to DB → cache result ──
export async function withCache(key, ttlSeconds, fetchFn) {
  const redis = await getRedis();
  const cached = await redis.get(key);
  if (cached) return JSON.parse(cached);

  const value = await fetchFn();
  await redis.setEx(key, ttlSeconds, JSON.stringify(value));
  return value;
}

// ── Invalidate a single key or a key pattern ──
export async function invalidate(key) {
  const redis = await getRedis();
  await redis.del(key);
}

// ── Rate limiting: 100 req/min per user ──
export async function checkRateLimit(userId, limit = 100, windowSecs = 60) {
  const redis = await getRedis();
  const key = `ratelimit:${userId}:${Math.floor(Date.now() / (windowSecs * 1000))}`;
  const count = await redis.incr(key);
  if (count === 1) await redis.expire(key, windowSecs);
  return { allowed: count <= limit, count, limit };
}

// ── Distributed lock: prevent duplicate processing ──
export async function withLock(lockKey, ttlMs, fn) {
  const redis = await getRedis();
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  // SET NX = only set if key does not exist (atomic)
  const acquired = await redis.set(lockKey, token, { NX: true, PX: ttlMs });
  if (!acquired) throw new Error(`Lock ${lockKey} already held`);

  try {
    return await fn();
  } finally {
    // Only release if we still own the lock
    const current = await redis.get(lockKey);
    if (current === token) await redis.del(lockKey);
  }
}

// ── Pub/Sub: publish task updates to websocket clients ──
export async function publishUpdate(channel, payload) {
  const redis = await getRedis();
  await redis.publish(channel, JSON.stringify(payload));
}

// Usage in routes:
// const task = await withCache(`task:${id}`, 300, () => db('tasks').where({id}).first());
// await invalidate(`task:${id}`); // on update/delete
// const { allowed } = await checkRateLimit(req.user.id);
// if (!allowed) return res.status(429).json({ error: 'Rate limit exceeded' });
Service 11 — Compute
ECS, EKS & Fargate
Containers are the next step after EC2 — you package your app and all its dependencies into a Docker image, and AWS runs it on managed infrastructure. ECS is AWS's own orchestrator, deeply integrated with IAM, ALB, CloudWatch, and Secrets Manager — and significantly simpler than Kubernetes for most teams. Fargate is the recommended compute option: AWS provisions and patches the underlying servers, and you only pay per vCPU-second your containers actually consume.
DockerECS ServicesFargateTask DefinitionsAuto-scalingRolling DeploymentsEKS

ECS concepts

ConceptEC2 equivalentDescription
Task DefinitionLaunch TemplateVersioned blueprint: Docker image URI, CPU/memory allocation, environment variables, IAM task role, port mappings, log configuration, health check. Immutable after creation — update by creating a new revision.
TaskEC2 instanceA running instance of a task definition. One or more containers share the same network namespace, storage volumes, and lifecycle.
ServiceAuto Scaling GroupEnsures N tasks are always running. Automatically replaces unhealthy tasks, integrates with the ALB target group, supports rolling and blue/green deployments.
ClusterVPC + ASGA logical grouping of tasks and services. With Fargate, the cluster is just a namespace — no EC2 infrastructure to manage.
Fargate—Serverless compute for containers. AWS provisions, scales, and patches the underlying hosts. You pay per vCPU-second and GB-second consumed by running tasks.
EC2 launch typeYour own ASGYou manage the EC2 instances in the cluster. More control and cheaper at sustained load, but more operational overhead.
Task roleEC2 instance profileIAM role assumed by the task's containers. Grants permissions to call AWS services (S3, DynamoDB, SQS) — equivalent to the EC2 instance role you set up earlier.
Execution role—IAM role used by the ECS agent to pull images from ECR and write logs to CloudWatch. Separate from the task role.

EC2 launch type vs Fargate — when to use each

FactorFargateEC2 launch type
Operational overheadMinimal — no instances to manageHigher — patch AMIs, manage ASG, right-size instance types
Cost at low/variable loadLower — pay only when tasks runHigher — EC2 instances billed whether idle or not
Cost at sustained high loadHigher (~30% premium vs EC2)Lower — use Reserved Instances for committed capacity
Startup time~30–60 seconds per task cold start~5–10 seconds once instances are running
GPU workloadsNot supportedSupported (p3, g4 instance types)
RecommendationDefault for most teamsHigh-throughput, cost-sensitive, or GPU workloads

Build and push the Docker image

Dockerfile — production Node.js containerbash
# Multi-stage build — keep the final image small
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci --only=production

FROM node:20-alpine AS runtime
WORKDIR /app
# Create non-root user (security best practice)
RUN addgroup -S appgroup && adduser -S appuser -G appgroup

COPY --from=builder /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --chown=appuser:appgroup src/ ./src/
COPY --chown=appuser:appgroup package.json ./

USER appuser

EXPOSE 3000
# Use exec form so Node receives SIGTERM for graceful shutdown
CMD ["node", "src/index.js"]
Terminal — build, push to ECR, and verifybash
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
REGION="us-east-1"
REPO="taskboard-api"
TAG=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)  # use git commit SHA as image tag — never :latest in production

# Authenticate Docker to ECR
aws ecr get-login-password --region $REGION | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin \
  $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com

# Build for the correct architecture (ECS Fargate runs on x86_64 or arm64)
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64 -t $REPO:$TAG .

# Tag and push
docker tag $REPO:$TAG $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$TAG
docker push $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$TAG

echo "Image: $ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$TAG"

# Create service with rolling update deployment

  --cluster taskboard-cluster \
  --service-name taskboard-api \
  --task-definition taskboard-api \

  --launch-type FARGATE \
  --network-configuration "awsvpcConfiguration={
    subnets=[$PRIV1,$PRIV2],
    securityGroups=[$ECS_SG],
    assignPublicIp=DISABLED
  }" \
  --load-balancers "targetGroupArn=$TG_ARN,containerName=taskboard-api,containerPort=3000" \
  --deployment-configuration "minimumHealthyPercent=100,maximumPercent=200"

Service auto-scaling

ECS services can scale the number of running tasks automatically based on CloudWatch metrics. Pair CPU/memory target tracking with SQS queue depth scaling to handle both API traffic spikes and async processing load.

Terminal — configure auto-scaling for the ECS servicebash
SERVICE_ARN="arn:aws:ecs:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:service/taskboard-cluster/taskboard-api"

# Register the service as a scalable target (min 2, max 10 tasks)
aws application-autoscaling register-scalable-target \
  --service-namespace ecs \
  --resource-id service/taskboard-cluster/taskboard-api \
  --scalable-dimension ecs:service:DesiredCount \
  --min-capacity 2 \
  --max-capacity 10

# Scale in/out when average CPU exceeds 70%
aws application-autoscaling put-scaling-policy \
  --service-namespace ecs \
  --resource-id service/taskboard-cluster/taskboard-api \
  --scalable-dimension ecs:service:DesiredCount \
  --policy-name cpu-target-tracking \
  --policy-type TargetTrackingScaling \
  --target-tracking-scaling-policy-configuration '{
    "TargetValue": 70.0,
    "PredefinedMetricSpecification": {"PredefinedMetricType": "ECSServiceAverageCPUUtilization"},
    "ScaleOutCooldown": 60,
    "ScaleInCooldown": 300
  }'

Deploying a new version

Terminal — CI/CD deploy: push image and force new deploymentbash
NEW_TAG=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)
NEW_IMAGE="$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/$REPO:$NEW_TAG"

# 1. Build and push new image
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64 -t $NEW_IMAGE .
docker push $NEW_IMAGE

# 2. Register a new task definition revision with the updated image
TASK_DEF=$(aws ecs describe-task-definition \
  --task-definition taskboard-api --query 'taskDefinition' | \
  jq --arg image "$NEW_IMAGE" '.containerDefinitions[0].image = $image |
    del(.taskDefinitionArn, .revision, .status, .requiresAttributes, .compatibilities, .registeredAt, .registeredBy)')

NEW_TASK_DEF_ARN=$(aws ecs register-task-definition \
  --cli-input-json "$TASK_DEF" \
  --query 'taskDefinition.taskDefinitionArn' --output text)

# 3. Update the service — ECS rolls out the new revision with zero downtime
aws ecs update-service \
  --cluster taskboard-cluster \
  --service taskboard-api \
  --task-definition $NEW_TASK_DEF_ARN \
  --force-new-deployment

# 4. Wait for deployment to complete
aws ecs wait services-stable \
  --cluster taskboard-cluster \
  --services taskboard-api

echo "Deployment complete: $NEW_IMAGE"

EKS — when to use Kubernetes instead

Use EKS when your team already knows Kubernetes, you need to run the same workload across multiple cloud providers, or you need advanced Kubernetes features: custom controllers, CRDs, Helm charts, or a large ecosystem of operators (e.g. Argo CD, Prometheus, Istio). EKS costs $0.10/hr for the managed control plane plus node costs.

Terminal — create an EKS cluster with Fargate nodesbash
# Install eksctl: https://eksctl.io
brew install eksctl

# Create a cluster with Fargate (no EC2 worker nodes to manage)
eksctl create cluster \
  --name taskboard-eks \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --fargate \
  --without-nodegroup   # managed Fargate profiles only

# Merge kubeconfig
aws eks update-kubeconfig --name taskboard-eks --region us-east-1

# Create a Fargate profile for the taskboard namespace
eksctl create fargateprofile \
  --cluster taskboard-eks \
  --name taskboard-profile \
  --namespace taskboard

# Deploy the app using standard kubectl
kubectl apply -f k8s/deployment.yaml
kubectl apply -f k8s/service.yaml
kubectl rollout status deployment/taskboard-api
Service 12 — Networking
Route 53
Route 53 is AWS's authoritative DNS service and the glue that ties your domain name to every other service in this tutorial. Without it, users access your app via ugly ALB DNS names. With it, api.taskboard.io resolves to your ALB, app.taskboard.io resolves to CloudFront, and health checks automatically reroute traffic away from unhealthy endpoints — making Route 53 a critical part of your application's availability story.
DNSHosted ZonesAlias RecordsACM CertificatesHealth ChecksLatency RoutingFailover

Key concepts

ConceptDescription
Hosted ZoneContainer for all DNS records for a domain. Public zones route internet traffic ($0.50/zone/month). Private zones resolve inside your VPC only.
Alias RecordMaps a domain to an AWS resource by DNS name (ALB, CloudFront, API Gateway). Free query charges. Auto-follows IP changes. Use at the zone apex (taskboard.io) where CNAME is not allowed.
A RecordMaps a domain directly to an IPv4 address. Use for Elastic IPs; prefer Alias for all AWS-managed resources.
CNAMEMaps a subdomain to another DNS name. Cannot be used at the zone apex — use Alias instead. Costs per query.
Health CheckRoute 53 pings your endpoint every 30 seconds. 3 consecutive failures = unhealthy. Powers automatic failover routing.
Routing policiesSimple, Weighted (A/B), Latency-based (multi-region), Failover (active-passive DR), Geolocation, Multivalue. Controlled per record set.

SSL/TLS certificates with ACM

Before pointing your domain at the ALB, request a free certificate in AWS Certificate Manager. ACM certificates auto-renew and attach to ALBs, CloudFront distributions, and API Gateway at no charge.

💡 ACM region rule

Certificates for CloudFront must be requested in us-east-1 regardless of where your resources are. Certificates for ALBs and API Gateway must be in the same region as the resource. You may need two certificates — one in us-east-1 for CloudFront, one in your primary region for the ALB.

Point your domain at the ALB with HTTPS

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/route53/v2/hostedzones
AWS Console
1

Request an ACM certificate

Go to Certificate Manager → Request certificate → Request public certificate. Enter *.taskboard.io and taskboard.io. Choose DNS validation. Click Request. On the certificate detail page, click Create records in Route 53 — ACM adds the validation CNAME records automatically. Certificate is issued within 1–5 minutes.

💡
If you haven't created the hosted zone yet, create it first (step 2 below), then come back to request the certificate so ACM can auto-add the validation records.
2

Create a hosted zone and update your registrar

Go to Route 53 → Hosted zones → Create hosted zone. Enter taskboard.io, leave type as Public. Click Create. Copy the 4 NS values shown and paste them into your domain registrar's nameserver settings.

3

Add HTTPS listener to the ALB

Go to EC2 → Load Balancers → taskboard-alb → Listeners → Add listener. Protocol HTTPS, port 443, forward to taskboard-tg, attach the ACM certificate. Then add a second listener on port 80 that redirects to HTTPS.

Route traffic to

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — request ACM cert, create hosted zone and alias recordbash
ALB_DNS="taskboard-alb-xxxx.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com"
ALB_HOSTED_ZONE_ID="Z35SXDOTRQ7X7K"  # hosted zone ID for ALBs in us-east-1

# Request wildcard certificate (DNS validation)
CERT_ARN=$(aws acm request-certificate \
  --domain-name "*.taskboard.io" \
  --subject-alternative-names "taskboard.io" \
  --validation-method DNS \
  --query 'CertificateArn' --output text)
echo "Certificate ARN: $CERT_ARN"
# Go to ACM console → click 'Create records in Route 53' to validate

# Create hosted zone
ZONE_ID=$(aws route53 create-hosted-zone \
  --name taskboard.io --caller-reference $(date +%s) \
  --query 'HostedZone.Id' --output text | cut -d/ -f3)

# Alias A record → ALB (with health check evaluation)
aws route53 change-resource-record-sets \
  --hosted-zone-id $ZONE_ID \
  --change-batch '{
    "Changes": [{"Action":"CREATE","ResourceRecordSet":{
      "Name":"api.taskboard.io","Type":"A",
      "AliasTarget":{
        "HostedZoneId":"'"$ALB_HOSTED_ZONE_ID"'",
        "DNSName":"'"$ALB_DNS"'",
        "EvaluateTargetHealth":true
      }
    }}]
  }'

Latency-based routing for multi-region

Once you have ALBs in two regions, Route 53 latency routing sends each user to whichever region has the lowest measured latency from their location — not geographic proximity. Combined with health checks, failed regions are automatically removed from DNS within ~30 seconds.

Terminal — latency routing across two regionsbash
# Primary record: us-east-1
aws route53 change-resource-record-sets --hosted-zone-id $ZONE_ID \
  --change-batch '{"Changes":[{"Action":"CREATE","ResourceRecordSet":{
    "Name":"api.taskboard.io","Type":"A",
    "Region":"us-east-1","SetIdentifier":"primary-us",
    "AliasTarget":{"HostedZoneId":"Z35SXDOTRQ7X7K","DNSName":"'"$ALB_US"'","EvaluateTargetHealth":true}
  }}]}'

# Secondary record: eu-west-1 (users in Europe served from here)
aws route53 change-resource-record-sets --hosted-zone-id $ZONE_ID \
  --change-batch '{"Changes":[{"Action":"CREATE","ResourceRecordSet":{
    "Name":"api.taskboard.io","Type":"A",
    "Region":"eu-west-1","SetIdentifier":"secondary-eu",
    "AliasTarget":{"HostedZoneId":"Z32O12XQLNTSW2","DNSName":"'"$ALB_EU"'","EvaluateTargetHealth":true}
  }}]}'
Service 13 — Messaging
SES — Simple Email Service
SES powers every notification in the TaskBoard tutorial — the due date checker Lambda, the task completion notifier, and the SNS fan-out pipeline all ultimately send through SES. At scale it handles billions of emails per month for companies like Reddit and Netflix. Getting SES right means understanding DKIM, SPF, DMARC, bounce handling, and the suppression list — all of which directly affect whether your emails land in inboxes or spam folders.
SESv2DKIM / SPF / DMARCConfiguration SetsBounce HandlingSuppression ListEvent TrackingProduction Access

Email authentication — SPF, DKIM, DMARC

Modern email providers (Gmail, Outlook) reject or spam-folder messages that fail authentication checks. SES sets up DKIM automatically, but you must also add SPF and DMARC records to your DNS to maximise deliverability.

StandardWhat it doesDNS record type
SPFLists which mail servers are authorised to send email for your domain. Receiving servers check the sender's IP against this list.TXT record on your domain: v=spf1 include:amazonses.com ~all
DKIMCryptographically signs each email with a private key. Receiving servers verify the signature using your public key in DNS. SES generates and manages the key pair.3 CNAME records provided by SES after domain verification
DMARCTells receiving servers what to do when SPF/DKIM fail (none, quarantine, or reject). Also sends aggregate reports of authentication failures to your specified email.TXT record: v=DMARC1; p=quarantine; rua=mailto:dmarc@taskboard.io

Verify your domain and move out of the sandbox

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/ses/home#/verified-identities
AWS Console
1

Verify your sending domain

Search Amazon SES → Verified identities → Create identity.

Identity type
● Domain
Domain
taskboard.io
DKIM signing
● Easy DKIM (recommended)
SES generates a 2048-bit RSA key pair and provides 3 CNAME records to add to Route 53. Verification completes within minutes of adding the records.
DKIM signatures
● Enabled
Custom MAIL FROM domain
● Enabled → mail.taskboard.io
Setting a custom MAIL FROM domain aligns SPF with your sending domain, improving deliverability and sender reputation scores at receiving mail servers.

Click Create identity. Add all the DNS records SES shows you to Route 53.

2

Create a Configuration Set for tracking

Go to SES → Configuration sets → Create configuration set. Name it taskboard-tracking. Add an Event destination → SNS → select or create taskboard-alerts. Tick Sends, Deliveries, Bounces, Complaints, Opens, Clicks.

💡
Configuration sets let you track every email event — delivery, bounce, complaint, open, click — and route them to SNS, CloudWatch, Kinesis, or S3. Use the bounce and complaint events to automatically add addresses to your suppression list and protect your sender reputation.
3

Request production access

Go to Account dashboard → Request production access. Fill in: mail type (Transactional), website URL, and a clear description: "TaskBoard sends task completion notifications and due date reminders to registered users who opted in at signup. Users can unsubscribe at any time via a link in every email."

⚠️
Keep your bounce rate below 5% and complaint rate below 0.1% at all times. AWS monitors these metrics and will pause your sending account if they are breached. Always implement a suppression list: remove bounced and complained-about addresses immediately.

Option B — Terminal (CLI) and Node.js

src/services/emailService.js — production email service with config set and error handlingjavascript
import { SESv2Client, SendEmailCommand, CreateSuppressedDestinationCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-sesv2";

// Use SESv2 — newer API with more features than SESv1
const ses = new SESv2Client({ region: "us-east-1" });

export async function sendTaskNotification(to, taskTitle, assigneeName) {
  try {
    await ses.send(new SendEmailCommand({
      FromEmailAddress: "noreply@taskboard.io",
      Destination: { ToAddresses: [to] },
      // Attach configuration set for tracking opens, clicks, bounces
      ConfigurationSetName: "taskboard-tracking",
      Content: {
        Simple: {
          Subject: { Data: `Task completed: ${taskTitle}`, Charset: "UTF-8" },
          Body: {
            Html: {
              Data: `
                
                  
Hi ${assigneeName},

                  
Your task ${taskTitle} was just marked complete.

                  
View all tasks →

                  

                  

                    
                      Unsubscribe from notifications
                    
                  

                `,
              Charset: "UTF-8",
            },
            Text: {
              Data: `Hi ${assigneeName}, "${taskTitle}" was completed. View tasks: https://app.taskboard.io/tasks`,
              Charset: "UTF-8",
            },
          },
        },
      },
    }));
  } catch (err) {
    // MessageRejected = address on suppression list; don't retry
    if (err.name === "MessageRejected" || err.name === "SendingPausedException") {
      console.warn(`Email to ${to} rejected:`, err.message);
      return;
    }
    throw err;
  }
}

// Add an address to the account-level suppression list
// (call this when SES notifies you of a bounce or complaint via SNS)
export async function suppressAddress(email, reason = "BOUNCE") {
  await ses.send(new CreateSuppressedDestinationCommand({
    EmailAddress: email,
    Reason: reason, // "BOUNCE" | "COMPLAINT"
  }));
}

Service 14 — Data
Amazon OpenSearch Service
OpenSearch Service is where you go when PostgreSQL's LIKE queries and full-text search become too slow or too limited. For TaskBoard, it enables fuzzy search across task titles and descriptions (tolerating typos), faceted filtering by status and priority, relevance ranking, and autocomplete — all returning results in under 50ms at scale. It also serves as the analytics backend for a log dashboard fed by CloudWatch Logs.
Full-text SearchFuzzy MatchingAggregationsFacetsHighlightsAutocompleteOpenSearch Dashboards

When to use OpenSearch vs PostgreSQL full-text search

CapabilityPostgreSQL tsvectorOpenSearch
Fuzzy matching (typos)NoYes — fuzziness: AUTO
Relevance scoringBasic (frequency-based)Full BM25 + field boosting
Multi-field searchYes but complexNative multi_match
Facets & aggregationsSlow at scaleFast — designed for it
Highlight matching termsNoYes — highlight block
ScaleSingle node limitsHorizontally scalable
Cost (small dataset)Free (already have RDS)$25+/month minimum
When to switchTasks < 100k, basic searchTasks > 100k, or need fuzzy/faceted/autocomplete

  -d '{

        "priority":    {"type": "keyword"},
        "assigneeId":  {"type": "keyword"},

src/services/searchService.js — production search servicejavascript
import { Client } from "@opensearch-project/opensearch";
import { AwsSigv4Signer } from "@opensearch-project/opensearch/aws";
import { defaultProvider } from "@aws-sdk/credential-provider-node";

// npm install @opensearch-project/opensearch @aws-sdk/credential-provider-node

const client = new Client({
  ...AwsSigv4Signer({ region: "us-east-1", service: "es", getCredentials: defaultProvider() }),
  node: `https://${process.env.OPENSEARCH_ENDPOINT}`,
});

// Index or update a task — call this after every create/update
export async function indexTask(task) {
  await client.index({
    index: "tasks",
    id: String(task.id),
    body: {
      title:       task.title,
      description: task.description || "",
      status:      task.status,
      priority:    task.priority,
      assigneeId:  task.assignee_id,
      createdAt:   task.created_at,
    },
    refresh: "wait_for",  // ensure indexed before returning (dev only; omit in prod)
  });
}

// Delete from index when a task is removed
export async function deleteTask(taskId) {
  await client.delete({ index: "tasks", id: String(taskId) });
}

// Full-text search with fuzzy matching, filters, facets, and highlights
export async function searchTasks(query, { status, priority, assigneeId, page = 1, size = 20 } = {}) {
  const must = [];
  const filter = [];

  // Full-text match across title (boosted 2×) and description
  if (query?.trim()) {
    must.push({
      multi_match: {
        query,
        fields: ["title^2", "description"],
        fuzziness: "AUTO",         // tolerates 1-2 character typos
        operator: "and",           // all terms must match
        type: "best_fields",
      },
    });
  }

  // Keyword filters (exact match — no text analysis)
  if (status)     filter.push({ term: { status } });
  if (priority)   filter.push({ term: { priority } });
  if (assigneeId) filter.push({ term: { assigneeId } });

  const { body } = await client.search({
    index: "tasks",
    body: {
      query: { bool: { must: must.length ? must : [{ match_all: {} }], filter } },
      // Highlight matching terms in title and description
      highlight: {
        fields: { title: {}, description: { fragment_size: 150, number_of_fragments: 2 } },
        pre_tags: [""], post_tags: [""],
      },
      // Aggregations for filter facets (how many tasks per status/priority)
      aggs: {
        by_status:   { terms: { field: "status" } },
        by_priority: { terms: { field: "priority" } },
      },
      sort: query?.trim()
        ? [{ _score: "desc" }]              // relevance sort when searching
        : [{ createdAt: "desc" }],          // recency sort when browsing
      from: (page - 1) * size,
      size,
    },
  });

  const hits = body.hits;
  return {
    total:    hits.total.value,
    page,
    results:  hits.hits.map(h => ({
      ...h._source,
      id:         h._id,
      score:      h._score,
      highlights: h.highlight || {},
    })),
    facets: {
      status:   body.aggregations.by_status.buckets,
      priority: body.aggregations.by_priority.buckets,
    },
  };
}

// Autocomplete — suggest task titles as the user types
export async function autocomplete(prefix) {
  const { body } = await client.search({
    index: "tasks",
    body: {
      query: { match_phrase_prefix: { title: { query: prefix, max_expansions: 10 } } },
      _source: ["title", "status"],
      size: 5,
    },
  });
  return body.hits.hits.map(h => ({ id: h._id, ...h._source }));
}

Keeping OpenSearch in sync with PostgreSQL

OpenSearch is a secondary index — PostgreSQL remains the source of truth. You must keep them in sync. The simplest approach is to call indexTask() and deleteTask() from your Express routes after every successful database write. For more resilience, use a DynamoDB Stream or a PostgreSQL trigger → Lambda → OpenSearch pipeline.

src/routes/tasks.js — sync to OpenSearch on writejavascript
import { indexTask, deleteTask } from "../services/searchService.js";

// After creating a task in PostgreSQL, index it in OpenSearch
router.post("/", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const [task] = await db("tasks").insert({ ...req.body }).returning("*");
    await indexTask(task).catch(err => console.error("Search index failed:", err));
    res.status(201).json(task);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});

// Full-text search endpoint
router.get("/search", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const { q, status, priority, page } = req.query;
    const results = await searchTasks(q, { status, priority, page: Number(page) || 1 });
    res.json(results);
  } catch (err) { next(err); }
});
Service 15 — Security
Amazon Cognito
Cognito replaces the custom DynamoDB session store built earlier in this tutorial with a production-grade auth system that handles every auth concern: sign-up with email verification, sign-in, password reset, MFA, OAuth 2.0 / OIDC, and social login via Google or GitHub. It issues short-lived JWTs that your Express middleware verifies locally in under 1ms — no round-trip to Cognito on every API request.

Option B — Node.js integration

src/middleware/cognitoAuth.js — verify tokens + extract user infojavascript
import { CognitoJwtVerifier } from "aws-jwt-verify";  // npm install aws-jwt-verify

// The verifier fetches and caches the User Pool's public keys (JWKS) on first use
const verifier = CognitoJwtVerifier.create({
  userPoolId:  process.env.COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID,   // us-east-1_XXXXXXXXX
  tokenUse:    "access",                            // verify access tokens (use "id" for ID tokens)
  clientId:    process.env.COGNITO_CLIENT_ID,
});

export async function cognitoAuth(req, res, next) {
  const authHeader = req.headers.authorization;
  if (!authHeader?.startsWith("Bearer ")) {
    return res.status(401).json({ error: "Missing Bearer token" });
  }

  const token = authHeader.slice(7);
  try {
    // Verifies: signature, expiry, issuer, audience — no Cognito API call needed
    const payload = await verifier.verify(token);

    req.user = {
      id:     payload.sub,          // stable unique identifier — use this as the user ID
      email:  payload.email,
      groups: payload["cognito:groups"] || [],
    };

    next();
  } catch (err) {
    const status = err.name === "JwtExpiredError" ? 401 : 403;
    return res.status(status).json({ error: "Invalid or expired token", detail: err.message });
  }
}

// Admin-only middleware — requires the user to be in the "admins" Cognito group
export function requireAdmin(req, res, next) {
  if (!req.user?.groups?.includes("admins")) {
    return res.status(403).json({ error: "Admin access required" });
  }
  next();
}
src/services/cognitoAdminService.js — manage users via Admin APIjavascript
import { CognitoIdentityProviderClient, AdminCreateUserCommand, AdminAddUserToGroupCommand, AdminDisableUserCommand } from "@aws-sdk/client-cognito-identity-provider";

const cognito = new CognitoIdentityProviderClient({ region: "us-east-1" });
const USER_POOL_ID = process.env.COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID;

// Invite a user by email — Cognito sends a welcome email with a temp password
export async function inviteUser(email, role = "member") {
  const user = await cognito.send(new AdminCreateUserCommand({
    UserPoolId: USER_POOL_ID,
    Username:   email,
    UserAttributes: [
      { Name: "email",          Value: email },
      { Name: "email_verified", Value: "true" },
    ],
    DesiredDeliveryMediums: ["EMAIL"],
  }));

  // Assign to a group based on role
  await cognito.send(new AdminAddUserToGroupCommand({
    UserPoolId: USER_POOL_ID,
    Username:   email,
    GroupName:  role === "admin" ? "admins" : "members",
  }));

  return user.User;
}

// Disable a user (e.g. they left the team)
export async function disableUser(email) {
  await cognito.send(new AdminDisableUserCommand({
    UserPoolId: USER_POOL_ID,
    Username:   email,
  }));
}
✅ Replacing the custom DynamoDB session store

This tutorial's sessionService.js stores sessions in DynamoDB. With Cognito, you can remove it entirely. The Cognito access token carries the user identity — your API middleware just verifies it. Refresh tokens (valid 30 days by default) give clients a way to get new access tokens without re-authenticating. No session table, no TTL management, no session ID generation needed.

Service 16 — IaC
Terraform
Terraform codifies everything you have built through the console in this tutorial into version-controlled HCL files. Every resource — VPC, subnets, RDS, S3, SQS, Lambda, ECS service, CloudFront distribution — becomes a .tf file you can diff, review in pull requests, reproduce in any AWS account, and tear down with a single command. This is the final step that turns ad-hoc infrastructure into a repeatable, auditable system.
HCLterraform plan / applyRemote State (S3)DynamoDB LockingWorkspacesVariablesVPC Module

The Terraform workflow

Terraform works in three stages on every change: Write HCL in .tf files, Plan to see a preview of what will be created/updated/destroyed, Apply to execute the changes. The state file tracks what Terraform has provisioned — it is the single source of truth for what exists.

CommandWhat it does
terraform initDownload providers and modules. Run once per project and after adding new providers.
terraform validateCheck HCL syntax without connecting to AWS. Fast — run before every plan.
terraform planShow what would change: + create, ~ update, - destroy. No changes made.
terraform applyExecute the plan. Prompts for confirmation. Pass -auto-approve for CI/CD.
terraform destroyTear down all resources managed by this state. Use with extreme caution in production.
terraform state listList all resources in the state file.
terraform importImport an existing AWS resource into Terraform state (for resources created manually).
terraform workspaceSwitch between named workspaces (dev/staging/prod) — each has its own state file.

Remote state — required for teams

By default, Terraform stores state locally in terraform.tfstate. This is fine for solo projects, but breaks for teams — two engineers running apply simultaneously can corrupt state. The solution is remote state in S3 with a DynamoDB lock table that prevents concurrent applies.

Terminal — bootstrap remote state (run once)bash
ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
STATE_BUCKET="taskboard-terraform-state-$ACCOUNT_ID"

# Create the state bucket (versioned for rollback)
aws s3api create-bucket --bucket $STATE_BUCKET --region us-east-1
aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket $STATE_BUCKET \
  --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled
aws s3api put-bucket-encryption --bucket $STATE_BUCKET \
  --server-side-encryption-configuration '{
    "Rules":[{"ApplyServerSideEncryptionByDefault":{"SSEAlgorithm":"AES256"}}]
  }'

# DynamoDB table for state locking
aws dynamodb create-table \
  --table-name taskboard-terraform-locks \
  --attribute-definitions AttributeName=LockID,AttributeType=S \
  --key-schema AttributeName=LockID,KeyType=HASH \
  --billing-mode PAY_PER_REQUEST

main.tf — complete TaskBoard infrastructurebash
terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.6"
  required_providers { aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.0" } }

  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "taskboard-terraform-state-ACCOUNT_ID"
    key            = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "taskboard-terraform-locks"
    encrypt        = true
  }
}

provider "aws" { region = var.aws_region }

locals {
  tags = { Project = "taskboard", Environment = var.environment, ManagedBy = "terraform" }
}

# ── VPC ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
module "vpc" {
  source          = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"
  version         = "5.0"
  name            = "taskboard-vpc"
  cidr            = "10.0.0.0/16"
  azs             = ["${var.aws_region}a", "${var.aws_region}b"]
  public_subnets  = ["10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]
  private_subnets = ["10.0.3.0/24", "10.0.4.0/24"]
  enable_nat_gateway   = true
  single_nat_gateway   = var.environment != "production"  # save cost in non-prod
  enable_flow_log      = true
  flow_log_destination_type = "cloud-watch-logs"
  tags = local.tags
}

# ── S3 — attachments bucket ──────────────────────────────────
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "attachments" {
  bucket = "taskboard-attachments-${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}"
  tags   = local.tags
}
resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "attachments" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.attachments.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}
resource "aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration" "attachments" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.attachments.id
  rule { apply_server_side_encryption_by_default { sse_algorithm = "AES256" } }
}
resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "attachments" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.attachments.id
  rule {
    id = "transition-old-objects"
    status = "Enabled"
    transition { days = 30; storage_class = "STANDARD_IA" }
    transition { days = 90; storage_class = "GLACIER" }
  }
}

# ── SQS — notifications + DLQ ────────────────────────────────
resource "aws_sqs_queue" "dlq" {
  name                      = "taskboard-notifications-dlq"
  message_retention_seconds = 1209600
  tags = local.tags
}
resource "aws_sqs_queue" "notifications" {
  name                       = "taskboard-notifications"
  visibility_timeout_seconds = 60
  receive_wait_time_seconds  = 20
  redrive_policy = jsonencode({
    deadLetterTargetArn = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.arn
    maxReceiveCount     = 3
  })
  tags = local.tags
}

# ── RDS PostgreSQL ────────────────────────────────────────────
resource "aws_db_subnet_group" "taskboard" {
  name       = "taskboard-db-subnet"
  subnet_ids = module.vpc.private_subnets
  tags = local.tags
}
resource "aws_db_instance" "taskboard" {
  identifier              = "taskboard-db"
  engine                  = "postgres"
  engine_version          = "15.4"
  instance_class          = var.environment == "production" ? "db.t3.small" : "db.t3.micro"
  allocated_storage       = 20
  max_allocated_storage   = 100   # auto-scale up to 100 GB
  db_name                 = "taskboard"
  username                = "taskadmin"
  password                = var.db_password
  skip_final_snapshot     = var.environment != "production"
  deletion_protection     = var.environment == "production"
  backup_retention_period = 7
  performance_insights_enabled = true
  db_subnet_group_name    = aws_db_subnet_group.taskboard.name
  vpc_security_group_ids  = [aws_security_group.rds.id]
  tags = local.tags
}

data "aws_caller_identity" "current" {}

output "bucket_name"  { value = aws_s3_bucket.attachments.bucket }
output "queue_url"    { value = aws_sqs_queue.notifications.url }
output "db_endpoint"  { value = aws_db_instance.taskboard.endpoint }
output "vpc_id"       { value = module.vpc.vpc_id }
