Service 17 — Operations
AWS Compute Optimizer
Compute Optimizer applies machine learning to 14–93 days of CloudWatch utilisation data and recommends right-sized alternatives for every EC2 instance, Lambda function, EBS volume, and ECS task in your account. In practice the first review typically identifies 30–60% in savings — the equivalent of paying for several months of CloudWatch costs back in the first recommendation report alone. It is free to enable.
Right-sizingEC2Lambda MemoryEBS gp2→gp3ECS TasksEnhanced MetricsLambda Power Tuning

How Compute Optimizer works

Compute Optimizer applies machine learning to 14 days of CloudWatch utilisation data (CPU, memory, network, disk I/O) for each resource. It compares current utilisation patterns against the specifications of every available instance type or configuration in your region, then ranks alternatives by performance risk and cost savings. Resources are categorised as:

FindingMeaningTypical causeAction
Over-provisionedResource is larger than your workload needsProvisioned for peak that never came; dev box in prodDownsize — e.g. m5.large → t3.medium. Saves money, same performance.
Under-provisionedResource is too small for your workloadTraffic grew; memory leak; CPU-bound queriesUpsize. Prevents latency spikes and OOM crashes.
OptimizedWell-matched to current workload—No action needed. Re-check after major traffic changes.
Insufficient dataLess than 14 days of metricsNew resource; CloudWatch metrics not enabledWait, or enable enhanced metrics and re-check in 2 weeks.
Not applicableResource type not supportedGPU instances, bare metalManual review needed.

Enable and act on recommendations

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/compute-optimizer/home
AWS Console
1

Enable Compute Optimizer

Search Compute Optimizer → Get started → Enable Compute Optimizer. For AWS Organizations accounts, enable it at the management account level to see recommendations across all member accounts in one place.

💡
Enable Enhanced infrastructure metrics (under Preferences → Update) to extend the look-back window from 14 to 93 days at ~$0.0003360 per resource per hour. Worth it for workloads with weekly or monthly traffic cycles — a 14-day window may miss a pattern that repeats monthly.
2

Review EC2 instance recommendations

Click EC2 instances in the left sidebar. Click any instance to see the recommendation detail page.

FindingMeaningAction
Over-provisionedCPU/memory consistently below 40%Downgrade — e.g. m5.large → t3.medium. Saves ~30%.
Under-provisionedCPU or memory consistently above 90%Upgrade. Prevents performance degradation.
OptimizedWell-matched to current workloadNo change needed.

The recommendation detail page shows a CPU utilisation chart for the past 14 days with the recommended instance type overlaid — letting you see exactly how much headroom you'd have. It also shows the projected monthly savings and the performance risk (Low / Medium / High). Only act on Low or Medium risk recommendations.

3

Review Lambda memory recommendations

Click Lambda functions. The Lambda cost model is memory × duration — so the optimal memory setting is rarely the default 128 MB. More memory often means shorter execution time, resulting in the same or lower cost.

💡
Run the open-source AWS Lambda Power Tuning tool (find it in the Serverless Application Repository — search "Lambda Power Tuning"). It runs your function at 10+ memory settings in parallel and produces a cost/performance graph. Compute Optimizer and Power Tuning together give you a complete picture.
4

Review EBS volume recommendations

Click EBS volumes. Compute Optimizer analyses IOPS and throughput utilisation — not just storage size. It often recommends switching from gp2 to gp3 (always cheaper and better performing) or downsizing volumes with consistently low IOPS utilisation.

Common recommendationWhySavings
gp2 → gp3gp3 is always cheaper than gp2 at the same size and has better baseline performance~20%
Reduce provisioned IOPSio1/io2 volumes provisioned for peak that rarely occursUp to 70%
Reduce volume sizeLarge volumes with low actual utilisationProportional
✓
The first Compute Optimizer review after 2 weeks of production traffic typically identifies 30–60% cost savings across the fleet. Apply Low-risk recommendations immediately. Re-review every quarter as traffic patterns evolve.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — enable, query, and export recommendationsbash
# Enable for this account (or all org accounts with --include-member-accounts)
aws compute-optimizer update-enrollment-status --status Active

# Enable enhanced metrics (93-day look-back, costs ~$0.0003/resource/hr)
aws compute-optimizer put-recommendation-preferences \
  --resource-type Ec2Instance \
  --enhanced-infrastructure-metrics Active

# EC2 recommendations — filter for over-provisioned only
aws compute-optimizer get-ec2-instance-recommendations \
  --filters Name=finding,Values=Overprovisioned \
  --query 'instanceRecommendations[*].{
    Instance:instanceArn,
    Current:currentInstanceType,
    Recommended:recommendationOptions[0].instanceType,
    Savings:recommendationOptions[0].estimatedMonthlySavings.value
  }' \
  --output table

# Lambda recommendations
aws compute-optimizer get-lambda-function-recommendations \
  --query 'lambdaFunctionRecommendations[*].{
    Function:functionArn,
    CurrentMem:currentMemorySize,
    RecommendedMem:memorySizeRecommendationOptions[0].memorySize,
    Risk:memorySizeRecommendationOptions[0].performanceRisk
  }' \
  --output table

# EBS volume recommendations
aws compute-optimizer get-ebs-volume-recommendations \
  --query 'volumeRecommendations[*].{
    Volume:volumeArn,
    CurrentType:currentConfiguration.volumeType,
    RecommendedType:volumeRecommendationOptions[0].configuration.volumeType
  }' \
  --output table

# Export all EC2 recommendations to S3 for spreadsheet analysis
aws compute-optimizer export-ec2-instance-recommendations \
  --s3-destination-config bucket=$BUCKET,keyPrefix=optimizer/ \
  --fields AccountId,InstanceArn,InstanceName,Finding,CurrentInstanceType,RecommendationOptions
Advanced Topic 4
Billing & Cost Control
AWS bills you for exactly what you use — which is great for scale, but dangerous without guardrails. An unmonitored EC2 instance left running, a forgotten RDS instance, or an accidental data transfer can generate hundreds of dollars in unexpected charges. This section teaches you how AWS billing works, how to stay inside the free tier, and how to set hard limits before anything gets out of hand.
Free Tier Billing Alerts Cost Explorer Budgets Pricing Calculator Cost Allocation Tags

How AWS billing works

AWS charges are calculated per service, per region, and billed monthly. The bill is the sum of every resource's usage multiplied by the unit price. Key concepts:

ConceptWhat it meansExample
On-demand pricingPay for what you use, by the second or hour. No upfront commitment. Most expensive per unit.EC2 t3.micro at $0.0104/hr
Reserved InstancesCommit to 1 or 3 years, save 30–60% vs on-demand. Good for stable baseline workloads.RDS db.t3.micro 1yr saves ~40%
Savings PlansFlexible commitment to a $ spend per hour (not a specific instance type). Covers EC2, Lambda, Fargate.Compute Savings Plan at $0.10/hr
Spot InstancesSpare EC2 capacity at up to 90% discount. AWS can reclaim with 2-min notice. Use for batch jobs.c5.large at $0.03/hr vs $0.17/hr
Data transferInbound to AWS is free. Outbound to the internet costs money. Transfer between AZs also costs.$0.09/GB outbound from us-east-1
Free TierGenerous always-free and 12-month trial quotas for most services. Tracked per account, not per service.750 hrs EC2 t2.micro/month

The #1 rule — set a billing alarm first

Before creating any AWS resource, set a billing alarm. This takes 3 minutes and protects you from every mistake in this tutorial.

console.aws.amazon.com/billing/home#/preferences
AWS Console
1

Enable billing alerts

Go to Billing → Billing Preferences. Under Alert preferences, tick Receive CloudWatch Billing Alerts and click Update. This is required before billing alarms will work — it is off by default.

Billing › Billing Preferences › Alert preferences
⚠️
Billing metrics are only available in us-east-1, even if all your resources are in another region. When creating billing alarms in CloudWatch, make sure your console is set to N. Virginia (us-east-1).
2

Create a $5 billing alarm in CloudWatch

Switch to us-east-1, open CloudWatch → Alarms → Create alarm. Click Select metric → Billing → Total Estimated Charge → USD.

Statistic
Maximum
Billing metrics update once per day. Maximum ensures you see the highest charge in the evaluation window.
Period
6 hours
Threshold
5
$5. Set this low — the goal is an early warning, not a limit. You can always increase it once you understand your spending patterns.
SNS topic
taskboard-alerts
Alarm name
taskboard-billing-over-5usd

Click through to Create alarm.

✓
You will now receive an email the moment your estimated monthly bill crosses $5. This is your safety net for every experiment in this tutorial.

AWS Budgets — set hard spending limits

CloudWatch billing alarms notify you after spending has happened. AWS Budgets lets you set a monthly spending target and get notified at 50%, 80%, and 100% of the budget — with the option to trigger automated actions (like stopping EC2 instances) when thresholds are breached.

console.aws.amazon.com/billing/home#/budgets/create
AWS Console
1

Create a monthly cost budget

Go to Billing → Budgets → Create budget. Use the Templates option for the fastest setup.

Billing › Budgets › Create budget
Budget setup
● Use a template (simplified)
Template
Zero spend budget
Zero spend budget alerts you the moment any charge appears — perfect while learning. Switch to a Monthly cost budget once you know your baseline spend.
Budget name
taskboard-zero-spend
Email recipients
you@example.com

Click Create budget.

Cost Explorer — understand where money goes

Cost Explorer is a free visual breakdown of your AWS spending. You must enable it once before the data starts populating (it takes up to 24 hours for the first view to fill in).

Option A — AWS Console

Enable Cost Explorer (one-time setup)

console.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/home#/cost-explorer
AWS Console
1

Enable Cost Explorer

In the console top search bar type Cost Explorer. On the landing page, click Enable Cost Explorer. This is a one-time action per account — Cost Explorer is free to use.

AWS Console › Cost Management › Cost Explorer → Enable
💡
Historical data is available from the day you enable it. AWS retroactively populates the last 12 months of spend. The dashboard will show data within 24 hours of enabling.

View 1 — Monthly spend by service

console.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/home#/cost-explorer?chartStyle=GROUP&groupBy=SERVICE
AWS Console
1

Set up the service breakdown view

In Cost Explorer, set the filters on the right-hand panel as follows:

Date range
Last 3 months
Granularity
● Monthly
Group by
Service
This splits the bar chart into coloured segments per service. EC2 and RDS will typically dominate. Data transfer charges appear under EC2 - Other — watch this carefully as it is often the source of surprise charges.

Click Apply. The stacked bar chart shows which services are driving your bill each month.

2

Switch to daily to spot unexpected spikes

Change Granularity to Daily. A spike on a specific date usually means a resource was launched and not stopped. Hover over the spike bar to see which service caused it.

Granularity
● Daily
Date range
Last 30 days
⚠️
If you see charges in a region you didn't use intentionally, resources were accidentally created there. Use the Filter → Region dropdown to isolate that region's spend, then go to that region in the console and delete the orphaned resources.

View 2 — Free Tier usage tracker

console.aws.amazon.com/billing/home#/freetier
AWS Console
1

Check free tier consumption

Go to Billing → Free Tier. This page shows a service-by-service table of how much free tier you have used vs your monthly limit.

Billing › Free Tier
ColumnWhat it means
MTD usageHow much you have used this calendar month
LimitThe free tier allowance for the month
Forecasted usageProjected usage by end of month — if this exceeds the limit, you will be charged
Usage typeThe exact billing dimension (e.g. BoxUsage:t2.micro for EC2)
💡
Use the Filter box to search for a specific service. Type RDS to see only RDS free tier rows. Green progress bars mean you are safe; red means you are already being charged for that service.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — check free tier usage and current month costbash
# Current month's cost so far, broken down by service
aws ce get-cost-and-usage \
  --time-period Start=$(date +%Y-%m-01),End=$(date +%Y-%m-%d) \
  --granularity MONTHLY \
  --metrics "BlendedCost" \
  --group-by Type=DIMENSION,Key=SERVICE \
  --query 'ResultsByTime[0].Groups[*].[Keys[0],Metrics.BlendedCost.Amount]' \
  --output table \
  --region us-east-1

# Check free tier usage
aws freetier get-free-tier-usage \
  --query 'freeTierUsages[?actualUsageAmount > `0`].[service,operation,actualUsageAmount,limit,unit]' \
  --output table

Cost Allocation Tags

Tags on AWS resources let Cost Explorer break down spending by project, team, or environment. There are two steps: first tag each resource (CLI or console), then activate the tag keys in the Billing console so Cost Explorer can use them as filter dimensions.

Option A — AWS Console

Step 1 — Tag resources using Tag Editor

console.aws.amazon.com/resource-groups/tag-editor/find-resources
AWS Console
1

Open Tag Editor

In the console search bar type Tag Editor. Tag Editor lets you find and tag multiple resources across all services in one place — much faster than going to each service individually.

AWS Console › Resource Groups › Tag Editor
2

Find all TaskBoard resources

Regions
us-east-1
Resource types
All supported resource types
Or narrow to AWS::EC2::Instance, AWS::RDS::DBInstance, AWS::S3::Bucket to find only TaskBoard resources.
Tags (optional filter)
Leave empty to find all resources

Click Search resources. Tick the checkboxes for your EC2 instance, RDS instance, and S3 bucket. Then click Manage tags of selected resources.

3

Add tags to all selected resources at once

Click Add tag for each key-value pair below. These tags are applied to all selected resources simultaneously.

KeyValue
Projecttaskboard
Environmentproduction
Owneryourname

Click Review and apply tag changes, then Apply changes to all selected.

✓
Tags are applied. Now activate them in Billing so Cost Explorer can filter by them.

Step 2 — Activate tags as Cost Allocation Tags (one-time)

console.aws.amazon.com/billing/home#/tags
AWS Console
1

Activate the Project and Environment tag keys

Go to Billing → Cost allocation tags. Under the User-defined cost allocation tags tab, search for Project. Tick the checkbox and click Activate. Repeat for Environment.

Billing › Cost allocation tags › User-defined › Activate
💡
Activated tags appear as filter dimensions in Cost Explorer within 24 hours. After activation you can go to Cost Explorer, click Filter → Tag → Project, and see spend broken down by project. This is invaluable when you have multiple apps in the same AWS account.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — tag all TaskBoard resourcesbash
# Tag the EC2 instance
aws ec2 create-tags --resources $INSTANCE_ID \
  --tags Key=Project,Value=taskboard Key=Environment,Value=production Key=Owner,Value=yourname

# Tag the RDS instance
aws rds add-tags-to-resource \
  --resource-name arn:aws:rds:us-east-1:ACCOUNT:db:taskboard-db \
  --tags Key=Project,Value=taskboard Key=Environment,Value=production

# Tag the S3 bucket
aws s3api put-bucket-tagging --bucket $BUCKET \
  --tagging 'TagSet=[{Key=Project,Value=taskboard},{Key=Environment,Value=production}]'

# Activate cost allocation tags — this part must be done in the Billing console.
# Billing → Cost allocation tags → search "Project" → Activate
# There is no CLI command for activating cost allocation tags.

Common surprise charges — and how to prevent them

ChargeWhy it happensPrevention
Stopped EC2 still chargesA stopped instance doesn't charge for compute, but EBS volumes keep billing. A 20 GB gp3 volume = ~$1.60/month even when stopped.Create a snapshot and terminate if unused for >1 month. Or accept the storage cost for convenience.
Elastic IP not associatedAllocated but unattached EIPs cost ~$3.65/month each.Release unused EIPs immediately. The teardown steps below handle this.
NAT Gateway data processing$0.045/GB of data through the NAT. A Lambda that downloads large files through NAT can rack up surprising charges.Use VPC endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB — traffic goes directly, bypassing the NAT for free.
CloudWatch Logs volumeVerbose logging at DEBUG level in production generates GB/day. At $0.50/GB ingestion that adds up fast.Set LOG_LEVEL=INFO in production. Set retention policies on every log group.
Data transfer across AZsEC2 ↔ RDS in different AZs costs $0.01/GB each direction. High-throughput apps can accumulate significant inter-AZ charges.Always put EC2 and RDS in the same AZ for the primary connection. Use Multi-AZ only for the standby.
Secrets Manager API calls$0.05 per 10,000 API calls. Fetching the secret on every request generates millions of calls.Cache the secret in memory for 5 minutes as shown in secretsService.js.
DynamoDB on-demand spikeOn-demand pricing is more expensive per request than provisioned at steady load. A sudden traffic spike can generate a large bill.Monitor ConsumedReadCapacityUnits. Switch to provisioned + auto-scaling once traffic stabilises.
Forgotten resources in other regionsThe AWS console defaults to the last-used region. Resources accidentally created in the wrong region quietly accrue charges.Use Tag Editor with All regions to find untagged resources across every region at once.

Quick teardown checklist

When you finish a tutorial session, use either option below to stop resources and prevent charges.

Option A — AWS Console

console.aws.amazon.com/ec2/home#Instances
AWS Console
1

Stop the EC2 instance

Go to EC2 → Instances. Tick taskboard-server. Click Instance state → Stop instance. Confirm. Compute charges stop immediately; the 20 GB EBS volume continues at ~$1.60/month.

EC2 › Instances › taskboard-server › Instance state › Stop instance
💡
If you are done with the tutorial entirely, choose Terminate instance instead — this deletes the instance and its EBS volume, stopping all charges. You cannot restart a terminated instance.
2

Release the Elastic IP

Go to EC2 → Elastic IPs. Tick your IP. Click Actions → Disassociate Elastic IP address first, then tick it again and click Actions → Release Elastic IP addresses. An unassociated EIP costs ~$3.65/month.

EC2 › Network & Security › Elastic IPs › Actions › Release Elastic IP addresses
3

Stop the RDS instance

Go to RDS → Databases. Tick taskboard-db. Click Actions → Stop temporarily. Confirm. Compute charges stop; storage continues to bill.

RDS › Databases › taskboard-db › Actions › Stop temporarily
⚠️
RDS automatically restarts after 7 days. If you leave it for a week, come back and stop it again. AWS does this to apply any pending maintenance updates.
4

Verify nothing is still running

Go to EC2 → Instances and confirm all instances show Stopped. Go to RDS → Databases and confirm Stopped. Go to EC2 → Elastic IPs and confirm the list is empty.

Finally check Billing → Bills — the current month's estimated charges should be zero or very close to it (only S3 storage and DynamoDB remain, both essentially free at our scale).

✓
All billable compute resources are stopped. Your Budgets zero-spend alert will email you if anything starts accruing charges again.

Option B — Terminal (CLI)

Terminal — pause everything to stop chargesbash
# Stop EC2 (compute charge stops; EBS still billed at ~$1.60/month)
aws ec2 stop-instances --instance-ids $INSTANCE_ID

# Stop RDS (compute charge stops; storage still billed)
aws rds stop-db-instance --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db
# Note: RDS auto-starts after 7 days — re-stop if leaving for a week

# Release Elastic IP to prevent $3.65/month charge
aws ec2 release-address --allocation-id $ALLOC_ID

# Verify nothing is still running and costing money
aws ec2 describe-instances \
  --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query 'Reservations[*].Instances[*].[InstanceId,InstanceType,Tags[?Key==`Name`]|[0].Value]' \
  --output table

aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --query 'DBInstances[*].[DBInstanceIdentifier,DBInstanceStatus,DBInstanceClass]' \
  --output table
✅ Free Tier survival tips

Stop EC2 and RDS when not in use. Keep only one EC2 t2.micro and one RDS db.t3.micro running — the free tier covers 750 hours each per month, which is exactly one instance running 24/7. A second instance immediately starts generating charges. Set retention on every CloudWatch log group. Release unused Elastic IPs. Use the Budgets zero-spend alert as your safety net.

What's Next
Missing Topics Audit & Advanced Topics
A review of every section in this tutorial — identifying what's covered, what's missing, and what to learn next. The second half covers advanced topics not touched in this tutorial at all: architecture patterns, security, containers, data engineering, IaC, and cost optimisation at scale.

IAM — Identity & Access Management

✅ Covered

Users, groups, roles, trust policies, least-privilege custom policies, instance profiles, policy simulator, SDK credential chain.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Permission boundaries — a ceiling on what permissions a role can ever grant, even if a broader policy is attached. Critical for large teams where developers can create roles.
  • Service Control Policies (SCPs) — organization-wide guardrails applied to entire AWS accounts via AWS Organizations. Prevents any user in the account from performing disallowed actions, even the root user.
  • IAM Identity Center (SSO) — replaces per-account IAM users with a single login for all accounts. The correct solution for teams with more than 2-3 people.
  • Cross-account roles — assume a role in a different AWS account. Essential for multi-account setups (e.g. dev/staging/prod in separate accounts).
  • Condition keys — restrict policies by IP, time of day, MFA presence, request region. Example: only allow S3 access from your office IP range.
  • Attribute-based access control (ABAC) — grant access based on resource tags matching principal tags. Scales better than role-per-resource for large teams.

VPC & Networking

✅ Covered

VPC creation, public/private subnets, Internet Gateway, route tables, security groups, NAT Gateway, VPC Endpoints (S3 and DynamoDB gateway type), Network ACLs (stateless rules, ephemeral ports), VPC Flow Logs (CloudWatch and Logs Insights queries), VPC Peering (same-account and cross-account), Transit Gateway overview. Console and CLI walkthroughs for all.

Still to explore

  • AWS PrivateLink — expose your own service privately to other VPCs without peering or a public endpoint. The mechanism powering Interface VPC Endpoints. Required when you want to sell or share a service to other AWS accounts without making it public.
  • Transit Gateway route tables — segment traffic between VPC attachments using multiple TGW route tables. For example: dev VPCs can reach shared services but not production VPCs, even though all are connected to the same TGW.
  • VPC Network Firewall — a stateful managed firewall (layer 7) inside your VPC. Inspects traffic based on domain names, TLS SNI, Suricata-compatible IDS/IPS rules, and protocol signatures. More powerful than NACLs for egress filtering.
  • Direct Connect — a dedicated physical network connection from your on-premises data centre to AWS. Bypasses the public internet entirely, with consistent latency and lower data transfer costs at high volume.
  • AWS Site-to-Site VPN — encrypted IPSec tunnel from your office or data centre to your VPC over the public internet. Cheaper and faster to set up than Direct Connect, with less consistent latency.

EC2

✅ Covered

Instance types, AMIs, key pairs, user data bootstrap, security groups, Elastic IP, EBS volumes, SSH management, pm2 process management, IAM instance profiles.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Auto Scaling Groups (ASG) — automatically add or remove EC2 instances based on CPU, memory, or custom metrics. Required for any production app expecting variable traffic.
  • Application Load Balancer (ALB) — distributes traffic across multiple EC2 instances. Required alongside ASG. Handles SSL termination, path-based routing, and health checks. Replaces direct instance access.
  • Launch Templates — versioned EC2 configuration used by Auto Scaling Groups. Replaces the older Launch Configurations.
  • EC2 Instance Connect — browser-based SSH without needing a .pem key file. More convenient for ad-hoc access.
  • Systems Manager Session Manager — SSH without port 22 open at all. More secure than traditional SSH.
  • AMI baking — create a custom AMI with Node.js pre-installed. New instances launch in seconds instead of minutes because the User Data setup script doesn't run. Tools: Packer.
  • Spot Instances — run EC2 at up to 90% discount. Ideal for batch jobs, CI/CD runners, and non-critical background processing. Requires handling interruption notices.
  • EBS Snapshots — point-in-time backups of EBS volumes. Automate with Amazon Data Lifecycle Manager.

S3

✅ Covered

Bucket creation, public access block, versioning, encryption, lifecycle rules, presigned URLs, server-side upload, client-side direct upload, list/delete operations.

Missing / What to learn next

  • S3 Event Notifications — trigger a Lambda function automatically when a file is uploaded. Use case: generate thumbnails, scan for viruses, index content in a database. Configure under the bucket's Properties → Event notifications tab.
  • Cross-Region Replication (CRR) — automatically copy objects to a bucket in another region for disaster recovery or reducing latency for globally distributed users.
  • S3 Object Lock — write-once-read-many (WORM). Prevents object deletion or overwrite for a set period. Required for financial records and compliance.
  • Multipart upload for large files — files >100 MB should be uploaded in parts using the multipart upload API. Required for files >5 GB (single PUT limit).
  • S3 Transfer Acceleration — speeds up uploads from end users worldwide by routing through CloudFront edge locations.
  • Static website hosting — host a React/Vue/Angular SPA directly from S3 (typically behind CloudFront). Much cheaper than EC2 for purely static frontends.
  • Bucket policies vs ACLs — bucket policies are the modern approach (already using them via the public access block). ACLs are legacy and should remain disabled.

RDS

✅ Covered

Instance creation via console and CLI, subnet groups, security groups, Knex.js migrations, connection pooling, CRUD routes, parameter groups, automated backups, deletion protection.

Missing / What to learn next

  • RDS Proxy — a managed connection pooler that sits between your app and RDS. Crucial when running many Lambda functions or EC2 instances, because Lambda can open thousands of simultaneous DB connections and exhaust RDS's limit. RDS Proxy multiplexes them.
  • Read Replicas — asynchronous read-only copies. Route SELECT-heavy queries to replicas to offload the primary. Knex supports multiple connections: configure a read pool pointing at the replica endpoint.
  • Multi-AZ failover testing — know how to trigger a manual failover (aws rds failover-db-instance) and observe your application behaviour during the ~60-second switchover.
  • Point-in-time restore — restore to any second within the backup retention window. Know how to do this before you need it.
  • RDS Performance Insights — visual query performance monitoring. Shows which SQL statements are consuming the most database time. Free for 7 days of history on db.t3.micro.
  • Database migration with pg_dump — how to export/import data when upgrading PostgreSQL major versions or moving between instances.
  • Knex seeding — knex.seed.run() populates the database with test/demo data. Useful for staging environments and local development.

Lambda

✅ Covered

Function creation, IAM execution role, SQS trigger, environment variables, memory/timeout tuning, cold starts, batch item failures, EventBridge cron schedules, EMF metrics, Knex query builder in Lambda context.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Lambda Layers — share code and dependencies (like knex and pg) across multiple functions without bundling them in every zip. Reduces deployment size and speeds up updates.
  • Provisioned Concurrency — pre-warm Lambda containers to eliminate cold starts for latency-sensitive endpoints. Costs money — only for functions in the critical path.
  • Lambda function URLs — give a Lambda a direct HTTPS endpoint without API Gateway. Simpler and cheaper for single-function APIs or webhooks.
  • S3 event triggers — invoke Lambda automatically when a file is uploaded to S3. Common pattern: process images, parse CSV files, trigger workflows.
  • Lambda destinations — route the result of an async invocation (success or failure) to SQS, SNS, EventBridge, or another Lambda. Alternative to SQS DLQ for async error handling.
  • Container image deployments — package Lambda as a Docker image (up to 10 GB). Required when your dependencies exceed the 50 MB zipped deployment limit.
  • Power Tuning — the open-source AWS Lambda Power Tuning tool runs your function at different memory settings and produces a cost/performance graph. The optimal setting is rarely the default 128 MB.

DynamoDB

✅ Covered

Table creation with console and CLI, partition key, sort key, GSI, TTL, on-demand billing, PutItem/GetItem/UpdateItem/DeleteItem/Query, marshall/unmarshall, session store, activity log pattern.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Transactions — TransactWriteItems / TransactGetItems execute up to 100 operations atomically across tables. Equivalent to a SQL transaction. Use when you need "update task AND log activity" to either both succeed or both fail.
  • DynamoDB Streams — a time-ordered sequence of item-level changes. Trigger Lambda whenever an item is created, modified, or deleted. Use case: real-time sync to OpenSearch, CDC pipelines, cross-region replication.
  • Single-table design — store multiple entity types (users, tasks, sessions) in one table using composite key patterns. Reduces costs and improves performance. Advanced topic — read "The DynamoDB Book" by Alex DeBrie.
  • DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) — in-memory cache in front of DynamoDB. Reduces read latency from single-digit milliseconds to microseconds. Worth considering for read-heavy, latency-sensitive workloads.
  • Provisioned capacity with auto-scaling — cheaper than on-demand at steady, predictable traffic. Set up auto-scaling policies to handle spikes without manual intervention.
  • Backup and restore — enable point-in-time recovery (PITR) for any table holding important data. Available under the table's Backups tab.

SQS

✅ Covered

Standard vs FIFO queues, DLQ with redrive policy, visibility timeout, long polling, batch send, batch delete, partial batch failure response, CloudWatch alarm on DLQ depth, console and CLI creation.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Message deduplication (FIFO) — FIFO queues deduplicate using a MessageDeduplicationId. Important for financial or idempotency-sensitive workflows.
  • Message filtering with SNS — use SNS as a fan-out layer above SQS. One publish to an SNS topic delivers to multiple SQS queues filtered by message attributes (e.g. only "TASK_COMPLETED" messages go to the email queue, "ATTACHMENT_UPLOADED" go to a virus scan queue).
  • Large message payloads — SQS has a 256 KB message size limit. For larger payloads, use the Extended Client pattern: store the payload in S3 and put only the S3 key in the SQS message.
  • SQS metrics for auto-scaling — scale EC2 Auto Scaling Groups based on ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible. When the queue grows, add instances; when it drains, remove them.
  • EventBridge Pipes — a newer way to connect SQS to Lambda with filtering, enrichment, and transformation — without writing a consumer. Can replace custom Lambda pollers for many patterns.

Secrets Manager

✅ Covered

Creating secrets via console and CLI, secret path naming convention, automatic rotation, in-memory caching in Node.js, fetching at startup in EC2 and Lambda.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Resource-based policies on secrets — grant other AWS accounts or specific IAM roles access to a secret directly on the secret's resource policy, without modifying IAM policies in every account. Used in multi-account setups.
  • SSM Parameter Store comparison — for non-sensitive config (feature flags, endpoint URLs, environment names), Parameter Store is free for standard parameters vs $0.40/secret/month for Secrets Manager. Know when to use each.
  • Secret versioning — Secrets Manager keeps previous secret versions during rotation. Applications should handle AWSPENDING and AWSCURRENT version labels correctly during the rotation window.
  • Rotation for non-RDS secrets — rotating third-party API keys requires writing a custom rotation Lambda. AWS provides templates but they require customisation for each secret type.

CloudWatch

✅ Covered

Log groups with retention, CloudWatch Agent on EC2, structured JSON logging, SDK log transport with batching, custom metrics, EMF, metric filters, alarms with composite alarms, Logs Insights with query playbook, dashboards, live log tailing. Console and CLI covered for log groups, alarms, and Logs Insights.

Missing / What to learn next

  • Anomaly detection alarms — instead of a fixed threshold, CloudWatch uses ML to learn the normal range for a metric and alarm when it deviates. Better than static thresholds for metrics with daily/weekly patterns (request rate, active users).
  • Contributor Insights — identifies which specific values (IPs, user IDs, endpoints) are the top contributors to a metric or log pattern. Great for finding which user is hammering your API.
  • CloudWatch Synthetics — run canary scripts (simulated user journeys) that call your API on a schedule and report availability and latency. Detects outages before real users do.
  • Container Insights — enhanced metrics for ECS and EKS containers: per-container CPU, memory, and network. If you move from EC2 to containers, enable this.
  • Cross-account observability — view metrics and logs from multiple AWS accounts in one CloudWatch dashboard. Required for multi-account architectures.
  • AWS X-Ray integration — distributed tracing. Traces a single request as it flows through EC2, Lambda, DynamoDB, and SQS — shows exactly where latency comes from. Add the X-Ray SDK alongside your existing structured logging.

CI/CD & Deployment

✅ Covered

GitHub Actions workflow with AWS credential injection, SSM-based deployment without SSH, deployment verification, blue-green deployment strategy.

Missing / What to learn next

  • OIDC for GitHub Actions — instead of storing long-lived access keys in GitHub Secrets, configure AWS to trust GitHub's OIDC provider. GitHub Actions can then assume an IAM role directly without any stored credentials. More secure, zero credential rotation.
  • AWS CodeDeploy — managed deployment service that handles blue-green and rolling deployments to EC2, Lambda, and ECS with automated health checks and rollback.
  • ECR (Elastic Container Registry) — if you containerise your app, ECR stores your Docker images. Integrates with CodeDeploy and ECS.
  • Infrastructure as Code (IaC) — define your entire VPC, EC2, RDS, and SQS setup in code using AWS CDK (TypeScript) or Terraform (HCL). Eliminates manual console/CLI work and makes environments reproducible. This is the most important next step after mastering the console.
  • Canary deployments — shift a small percentage of traffic (e.g. 5%) to the new version, monitor error rates, then shift the rest. Minimises blast radius of a bad deployment.

Important services not covered in this tutorial

ServiceWhat it doesWhen you need it
API GatewayManaged HTTP API layer. Routes requests to Lambda without EC2. Handles auth, throttling, and usage plans.When you want a fully serverless API (Lambda only, no EC2).
CloudFrontGlobal CDN. Caches your API responses and static assets at 400+ edge locations. Reduces latency for global users.Any app with users outside your primary region.
ElastiCache (Redis)Managed Redis cluster. Cache database query results, store sessions, implement rate limiting.When DynamoDB session latency is too high or you need pub/sub.

Advanced topics not covered in this tutorial

These are patterns and services that go beyond intermediate level. They are the typical gap between an engineer who can deploy to AWS and one who can design and operate a production system at scale. Work through them in roughly this order after completing this tutorial.

Architecture & Reliability

  • Multi-region active-passive — replicate your entire stack to a second region. Route 53 health-check routing fails over DNS within 60 seconds if the primary region becomes unavailable. Requires global DynamoDB tables, S3 cross-region replication, and RDS read replicas promoted to primary.
  • Chaos Engineering — deliberately inject failures (kill an EC2 instance, block a port, slow a database) to verify your application recovers gracefully. AWS Fault Injection Simulator (FIS) automates this. Essential before claiming a system is truly resilient.
  • Well-Architected Framework — AWS's six-pillar framework (Operational Excellence, Security, Reliability, Performance Efficiency, Cost Optimisation, Sustainability). Run the Well-Architected Tool review on your account to get a scored assessment and prioritised improvement plan.
  • Service Quotas and limits — every AWS service has default limits (e.g. 5 VPCs per region, 5 Elastic IPs per region, 1000 Lambda concurrent executions). Know your limits before launch and request increases in advance via the Service Quotas console.
  • Disaster Recovery strategies — Backup & Restore (RPO hours, cheapest), Pilot Light (RPO minutes, keeps a minimal hot copy), Warm Standby (RPO seconds, scaled-down but live), Multi-Site Active/Active (RPO near-zero, most expensive). Choose based on your RTO/RPO requirements.

Security

  • AWS GuardDuty — threat detection service that analyses CloudTrail, VPC Flow Logs, and DNS logs using ML. Detects compromised instances, unusual API calls, cryptocurrency mining, and data exfiltration. Enable it in every account — it costs a few dollars/month and requires zero configuration.
  • AWS Security Hub — aggregates findings from GuardDuty, Inspector, Macie, and third-party tools into a single prioritised dashboard. Runs automated security checks against the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark.
  • AWS Inspector — continuously scans EC2 instances and Lambda functions for known CVEs in OS packages and application dependencies. Integrates with ECR to scan container images on push.
  • AWS Macie — uses ML to automatically discover and protect sensitive data (PII, credentials, financial data) in S3 buckets. Alerts when a bucket contains credit card numbers or unencrypted secrets.
  • CloudTrail — logs every API call made in your account (who called what, when, from where). The foundational audit log for security investigations. Enable a multi-region trail and ship logs to a separate, locked-down S3 bucket immediately. Non-negotiable for any production account.
  • AWS Config — continuously records the configuration state of every resource and evaluates it against rules. Example rules: "all S3 buckets must block public access", "all EC2 instances must have an IAM role". Alerts or auto-remediates when rules are violated.
  • Secrets rotation best practices — understand the dual-password rotation strategy for RDS (create a new password, validate it works, delete the old one) to achieve zero-downtime credential rotation in production.
  • VPC Network Firewall — a managed, stateful network firewall inside your VPC. Inspects traffic at layers 3–7. More powerful than security groups for filtering based on domain names, protocol signatures, or Suricata-compatible IDS/IPS rules.

Performance & Scalability

  • Global Accelerator — routes user traffic over the AWS global backbone instead of the public internet. Reduces latency by 20–60% for global users compared to a regional ALB. Provides two static anycast IPs for your application.
  • ElastiCache for Redis — advanced patterns — beyond simple caching: use Redis Pub/Sub for real-time notifications, Redis Streams for append-only event logs, Redis sorted sets for leaderboards, and Redis scripting for atomic multi-key operations.
  • RDS connection management at scale — when you have 50+ Lambda functions all connecting to RDS, connection exhaustion becomes a real problem. The correct architecture is Lambda → RDS Proxy → RDS. RDS Proxy maintains a pool of connections and multiplexes thousands of Lambda requests through a small number of real DB connections.
  • DynamoDB capacity planning — understand Read Capacity Units (RCUs) and Write Capacity Units (WCUs). 1 RCU = 1 strongly consistent read of up to 4 KB. At scale, a single hot partition key can throttle your entire table — design partition keys with high cardinality to distribute load evenly.
  • S3 performance — S3 supports 3,500 PUT/COPY/POST/DELETE and 5,500 GET requests per second per prefix. For high-throughput workloads, use multiple prefixes (not all objects under the same key prefix). Use S3 Byte-Range Fetch to parallelise large downloads.
  • Lambda concurrency management — understand the difference between account-level concurrency limits, reserved concurrency (guarantee headroom for a critical function), and provisioned concurrency (eliminate cold starts). Use concurrency alarms to detect when you are approaching the account limit.

Containers & Modern Application Patterns

  • ECS on Fargate — run Docker containers without managing EC2 servers. Fargate allocates exactly the CPU and memory you request and bills per second. Simpler than EKS for teams that don't need Kubernetes. Pair with an ALB and Service Discovery for a complete microservices setup.
  • AWS App Runner — the simplest way to run a containerised Node.js app on AWS. Point it at a GitHub repository or ECR image, set the memory and CPU, and App Runner handles load balancing, scaling, TLS, and deployment. Zero VPC or IAM configuration required. Ideal for prototypes and small production apps.
  • ECR image scanning — enable Enhanced Scanning (powered by Inspector) on your ECR repositories. Every image push is automatically scanned for OS and language-level CVEs. Findings appear in the Inspector console and can trigger EventBridge rules to block deployment of vulnerable images.
  • Service mesh (AWS App Mesh) — adds traffic management, observability, and mTLS between microservices without changing application code. Useful when you have 5+ services that need circuit breaking, retries, and canary routing between them.
  • Serverless Framework / AWS SAM — define Lambda functions, API Gateway routes, DynamoDB tables, and SQS queues as YAML. Deploy in one command. SAM (Serverless Application Model) is AWS-native; Serverless Framework supports multiple clouds. Both are lighter-weight alternatives to CDK for pure serverless apps.

Data & Analytics

  • Amazon Athena — run SQL queries directly against files in S3 (JSON, CSV, Parquet). No infrastructure to manage. Pay per TB scanned. Use it to query CloudWatch log exports, analyse application event data, or build ad-hoc reports on S3-resident data without loading it into a database.
  • AWS Glue — serverless ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) service. Crawls S3 to infer schemas, runs Spark jobs to transform data, and loads it into Redshift, Athena, or another S3 location. The backbone of most AWS data lake architectures.
  • Amazon Kinesis Data Streams — real-time data streaming at high throughput. Ingest millions of events per second (clickstreams, IoT telemetry, application logs). Consumers process the stream in real time using Lambda or Kinesis Data Analytics. The AWS equivalent of Apache Kafka.
  • Amazon Redshift — petabyte-scale columnar data warehouse. Optimised for complex analytical queries across billions of rows. Integrates with S3 (Redshift Spectrum) to query data without loading it in. Use Redshift when you need BI tools like Tableau or Looker connected to your data.
  • DynamoDB → S3 export for analytics — export DynamoDB table contents to S3 in DynamoDB JSON or Amazon Ion format. Then query with Athena. Enables analytics on operational data without impacting your production DynamoDB throughput.

Infrastructure as Code (IaC)

  • AWS CDK (Cloud Development Kit) — define all your AWS infrastructure in TypeScript, Python, Java, or Go. CDK synthesises CloudFormation templates and deploys them. Lets you write real code (loops, conditionals, classes, tests) instead of YAML. The recommended IaC tool for teams already using TypeScript/Node.js.
  • Terraform — vendor-neutral IaC tool that works with AWS, GCP, Azure, and 300+ providers. Uses HCL (HashiCorp Configuration Language). Has a larger ecosystem than CDK, better support for multi-cloud, and stronger state management with Terraform Cloud. Learn this if you ever need to manage infrastructure across multiple cloud providers.
  • CloudFormation StackSets — deploy a CloudFormation stack across multiple AWS accounts and regions simultaneously. Essential for organisations using AWS Organizations to enforce baseline security configurations (GuardDuty, Config, CloudTrail) in every account automatically.
  • CDK Constructs — reusable IaC components. AWS publishes a library of higher-level constructs (e.g. aws-ecs-patterns.ApplicationLoadBalancedFargateService) that provision complete patterns in a few lines. The Construct Hub (constructs.dev) is the community marketplace for third-party constructs.
  • Drift detection — CloudFormation can detect when a resource's actual configuration has diverged from the template (someone manually changed a security group in the console). Run drift detection regularly and treat console-only changes as a policy violation — all infrastructure changes should go through IaC.

Cost Optimisation at Scale

  • AWS Compute Optimizer — analyses CloudWatch metrics for your EC2 instances, Lambda functions, EBS volumes, and ECS tasks, then recommends right-sized alternatives. Often identifies instances that are 50%+ over-provisioned. Free to use.
  • Savings Plans vs Reserved Instances — Savings Plans (Compute and EC2) offer up to 66% discount vs on-demand in exchange for a $/hr commitment over 1 or 3 years. More flexible than Reserved Instances because they apply automatically to any instance type or Lambda usage within your committed spend. Buy these after 3+ months of stable usage patterns.
  • S3 Intelligent-Tiering — automatically moves objects between access tiers (Frequent, Infrequent, Archive Instant) based on access patterns. No retrieval fees. Ideal for buckets with unpredictable access patterns — cheaper than Standard for data accessed less than monthly, with no management overhead.
  • AWS Cost Anomaly Detection — uses ML to learn your normal spending patterns and alerts you within hours when spend is unexpectedly high. More responsive than fixed billing alarms. Creates monitors per service, linked account, or cost category. Free to use — you only pay for the SNS notifications.
  • Spot Instance interruption handling — when AWS needs Spot capacity back, it gives a 2-minute interruption notice via the EC2 metadata endpoint and EventBridge. Your application should listen for this event, drain connections gracefully, and save state. Use Spot with the capacity-optimized allocation strategy and mix in On-Demand instances for critical minimum capacity.
Knowledge Check
Intermediate AWS Quiz
12 questions covering IAM, networking, storage, databases, and serverless. Explanations are shown after each answer.
Reference
AWS CLI Cheat Sheet
The most-used AWS CLI commands for everyday development. Bookmark this page.

IAM

IAM commandsbash
aws sts get-caller-identity                            # who am I?
aws iam list-users                                     # list all IAM users
aws iam get-user --user-name USERNAME                  # user details
aws iam list-attached-user-policies --user-name U      # policies for user
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn ARN --action-names s3:PutObject  # simulate permissions

EC2

EC2 commandsbash
aws ec2 describe-instances --query 'Reservations[].Instances[].[InstanceId,State.Name,PublicIpAddress,Tags[?Key==`Name`]|[0].Value]' --output table
aws ec2 start-instances --instance-ids i-xxx           # start
aws ec2 stop-instances --instance-ids i-xxx            # stop (no charge for compute)
aws ec2 terminate-instances --instance-ids i-xxx       # ⚠️ permanent delete
aws ec2 describe-instance-status --instance-ids i-xxx  # health checks
aws ec2 describe-security-groups --group-ids sg-xxx    # see firewall rules

S3

S3 commandsbash
aws s3 ls                                   # list all buckets
aws s3 ls s3://BUCKET/PREFIX/               # list objects
aws s3 cp file.txt s3://BUCKET/key.txt      # upload
aws s3 cp s3://BUCKET/key.txt ./file.txt    # download
aws s3 sync ./local-dir s3://BUCKET/dir/   # sync directory
aws s3 rm s3://BUCKET/key.txt               # delete object
aws s3 rb s3://BUCKET --force               # delete bucket + all contents
aws s3api head-object --bucket B --key K    # get object metadata
aws s3 presign s3://BUCKET/KEY --expires-in 3600  # generate download URL

RDS

RDS commandsbash
aws rds describe-db-instances                    # list all instances
aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --db-instance-identifier ID \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].Endpoint.Address'      # get connection host
aws rds stop-db-instance --db-instance-identifier ID   # stop (saves money)
aws rds start-db-instance --db-instance-identifier ID
aws rds create-db-snapshot \
  --db-instance-identifier ID \
  --db-snapshot-identifier my-snapshot           # manual backup
aws rds restore-db-instance-to-point-in-time \
  --source-db-instance-identifier ID \
  --target-db-instance-identifier NEW-ID \
  --restore-time 2024-01-15T14:00:00Z            # point-in-time restore

Lambda

Lambda commandsbash
aws lambda list-functions                        # list functions
aws lambda invoke \
  --function-name FUNCTION \
  --payload '{"key":"val"}' \
  --cli-binary-format raw-in-base64-out \
  output.json && cat output.json                 # invoke and see response
aws lambda get-function --function-name FUNCTION # function details
aws lambda update-function-code \
  --function-name FUNCTION \
  --zip-file fileb://function.zip                # deploy new code
aws logs tail /aws/lambda/FUNCTION --follow      # live tail Lambda logs

DynamoDB

DynamoDB commandsbash
aws dynamodb list-tables
aws dynamodb describe-table --table-name TABLE
aws dynamodb put-item --table-name TABLE \
  --item '{"pk":{"S":"user-1"},"email":{"S":"a@b.com"}}'
aws dynamodb get-item --table-name TABLE \
  --key '{"pk":{"S":"user-1"}}'
aws dynamodb query --table-name TABLE \
  --key-condition-expression "pk = :pk" \
  --expression-attribute-values '{":pk":{"S":"user-1"}}'
aws dynamodb scan --table-name TABLE \
  --filter-expression "attribute_exists(email)"  # ⚠️ expensive — reads whole table

SQS

SQS commandsbash
aws sqs list-queues
aws sqs send-message --queue-url URL --message-body '{"msg":"hello"}'
aws sqs receive-message --queue-url URL --max-number-of-messages 10
aws sqs delete-message --queue-url URL --receipt-handle HANDLE
aws sqs get-queue-attributes \
  --queue-url URL \
  --attribute-names ApproximateNumberOfMessages   # queue depth
aws sqs purge-queue --queue-url URL               # ⚠️ delete all messages

CloudWatch

CloudWatch commandsbash
aws logs describe-log-groups                        # list log groups
aws logs tail /taskboard/api --follow               # live tail logs (like tail -f)
aws logs filter-log-events \
  --log-group-name /taskboard/api \
  --filter-pattern "ERROR" \
  --start-time $(date -d "1 hour ago" +%s)000      # find errors in last hour
aws cloudwatch describe-alarms --state-value ALARM  # firing alarms
aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics \
  --namespace AWS/EC2 \
  --metric-name CPUUtilization \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=$ID \
  --start-time $(date -u -d "1 hour ago" +%FT%TZ) \
  --end-time $(date -u +%FT%TZ) \
  --period 300 --statistics Average               # CPU over last hour

Teardown (save money!)

scripts/teardown.shbash
#!/bin/bash
echo "⚠️  This will delete all TaskBoard AWS resources!"
read -p "Type 'yes' to confirm: " CONFIRM
[ "$CONFIRM" != "yes" ] && exit 1

# Stop and terminate EC2
aws ec2 terminate-instances --instance-ids $INSTANCE_ID

# Delete RDS (disable deletion protection first)
aws rds modify-db-instance --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db --no-deletion-protection
aws rds delete-db-instance --db-instance-identifier taskboard-db --skip-final-snapshot

# Empty and delete S3 bucket
aws s3 rm s3://$BUCKET --recursive
aws s3api delete-bucket --bucket $BUCKET

# Delete DynamoDB tables
aws dynamodb delete-table --table-name taskboard-sessions
aws dynamodb delete-table --table-name taskboard-activity-logs

# Delete Lambda
aws lambda delete-function --function-name taskboard-email-notifier

# Delete SQS queues
aws sqs delete-queue --queue-url $QUEUE_URL
aws sqs delete-queue --queue-url $DLQ_URL

# Release Elastic IP
aws ec2 release-address --allocation-id $ALLOC_ID

echo "All resources deleted. Check AWS console for anything missed."
